PATH 01

Sell an AI playbook

You already know one job well enough to write it down. A stranger will pay to skip the guessing.

19 minute read · 4,211 words

Who this is for
Someone who can explain a single result — a week of content, a client intake, a job application pack — better than a blank chat window.
First sale
A $19–$47 download bought by a person who is not you.
Time to first
7–21 days if you already know the job
Cash window
$19–$47 first offer
Tools
Google Docs or Word, Stripe Payment Link or Stan Store, one simple page, a second email to test-buy.
Leave this alone
A 200-page ‘everything AI’ book, fifteen products, or waiting for a course platform.

What this actually is

A job, written down

A playbook is not a memoir about AI. It is a job, written so a tired person can finish it without inventing the steps. The buyer is not shopping for inspiration. They are shopping for a sequence they can run this week.

Narrow beats free

The products that sell in this category are narrow. "How to use ChatGPT" sits in a pile of free posts. "A 10-day content system for a one-person landscaping company" is a product. The more specific the job, the less you compete with free generic advice.

Draft with AI. Ship the rewrite.

You are allowed to use AI to draft. You are not allowed to ship the draft. The money is in the rewrite: your examples, your order of operations, your "do this next" at the end of every chapter. If a chapter does not end with a next action, it is an essay. Cut it or finish it. Read a chapter to someone who does the job. If they ask "so what do I do?" rewrite it. If they say "I can do that tonight," keep it.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This fits you if

This path fits if you have done a job more than three times and can name what went wrong the first time. That scar tissue is the product. People pay for the mistakes you already made.

You do not need a following. You need one finished result you can explain in plain English: a week of content, a client intake, a job application pack, a month-end close.

Skip this path if

Skip this path if you have never finished a result for anyone, including yourself. Write the playbook after you have a result, not as a way to avoid getting one. If you need cash this week, use a service path first (Productized AI service or Local businesses + AI), then turn the notes into this playbook.

The honest middle

If you have done the job twice and the third time is this week, capture that run as you do it. The playbook is the notes, cleaned. You are not waiting to become an expert. You are waiting to have a sequence you would hand a tired friend.

The offer, named in one sentence

Fill this before you outline

Write this sentence before you outline: "I help [specific person] get [named result] in [time] using [named tools], even if [honest objection]." If you cannot fill the brackets, you do not have an offer yet.

Specific person is a job plus a constraint. Named result is a file or a week of work, not a feeling. Time is days or hours. Named tools are what they already have. Honest objection is why they have not done it.

Sentences that work

  • I help one-person landscapers get 10 Google-ready posts in 10 days using ChatGPT and photos they already have, even if they hate writing and only have 20 minutes at night.
  • I help solo accountants close the month in Claude with a checklist they can rerun, even if their books are messy.
  • I help realtors write listing descriptions that do not sound like brochures, in one sitting, using the MLS notes they already typed.

Price the result, not the page count. First playbooks sell at $19-$47. Raise to $67-$97 when the file has templates, scripts, and a 14-day plan. Do not start at $297. You have no proof yet.

One sentence, one product

If you need "and" to name the result, you have two products. Split them. Sell the one you can finish this week.

What "done" looks like

The bar for the file

The playbook is done when a stranger can finish chapter one without you in the room. That is the bar. Everything else is decoration.

Done also means: 8-15 chapters, each titled as a verb plus an object, each ending with one next action. A cover that states the job. Page numbers. Your email on the last page for questions you actually want. A template or prompt they can copy.

The bar for the checkout

One product, one price, one file. A Stripe Payment Link or Stan page whose title matches the one-sentence offer. You have bought it yourself from a second email, on a different browser, and the file arrived and opened.

The bar for the first week

Ten personal messages sent, at least one person who is not you owning the file, and a note of what they used. That note becomes the next post. If nobody has the file yet, send the next three messages before you rewrite the cover.

Pricing that holds

Start where proof is

$19 is a coffee-and-a-tip price. People will try it if the job is named. $29 is the default first playbook if you include a template or a prompt block. $47 is fair when there is a 10- or 14-day plan plus scripts. Do not hide the price behind "DM me." Put it on the page. Pick $29 or $47 and sit there until a stranger has bought.

When to raise

Raise after a stranger sale and a sentence from them about what they used. Then add the missing template or a 14-day calendar and move to $67-$97. Raise because the file does more, not because you got bored of $29. Do not add five bonuses to justify a jump. Add one working file.

What not to do

Do not start at $297. You have no proof yet. Do not do $9 "to get traction." You will attract collectors, not operators. Do not price by word count. A tight 18-page job at $29 outsells an 80-page tour of your opinions.

Refunds: offer them, no speech. "If it is not useful, email me and I will refund." You will refund almost nobody if the first chapter works.

The first 72 hours

Hours 1-3: pick and name

Pick the buyer and the result. Write the one-sentence offer. If you have two ideas, pick the one you can finish without research you have not done. Put the sentence at the top of a Google Doc. Do not survey Twitter. Do not build a waitlist. One buyer, one result, one sentence.

Hours 4-8: outline like a tired person

Outline 8-15 chapters. Each chapter title is a verb plus an object: "Write the three hooks," "Set the checkout," "Send the ten messages." Put a next action under each. First chapter: what "done" looks like. Last chapter: what they do next week. Middle chapters are the work.

Paste this if you want a starting outline, then rewrite every title in your voice:

You are outlining a paid playbook, not a blog. Buyer: [person]. Result they can finish: [result]. Time: [days]. Tools they already have: [tools]. Honest objection: [objection]. Give me 12 chapter titles as verb + object. Under each, one next action a tired person can do in 20 minutes. No hype. No "mindset" chapters.

Day 2: ugly draft, then human

Draft ugly. Use AI for a first pass if you want, then rewrite every paragraph in the voice you would use to a friend at a kitchen table. Cut hype. Keep the steps. Read a page out loud. If you would not say it, delete it. Do not format. Do not design a cover. Words first.

Day 3: checkout, self-buy, ten asks

Make the checkout. One product, one price, one file. Buy it yourself from a second email. If the download fails, nothing else matters. Then send ten messages to people who already know you. That is the whole 72 hours.

  • One buyer, one result, one sentence
  • 8-15 chapters that each end with a next action
  • Ugly complete draft, then a human rewrite
  • Working checkout, self-purchase, ten asks

The path, step by step

Steps 1-4: name, gather, outline, draft

Step 1. Name the job on a sticky note. If it takes more than one line, it is two products. Split it. Write the sticky note in the buyer's words, not yours.

Step 2. Collect the raw material you already have: old emails, checklists, prompts that worked, screenshots of a finished result. Dump it into the same Doc under Raw. You will mine it, not show it.

Step 3. Outline for a reader who is tired. First chapter: what "done" looks like. Last chapter: what they do next week. Middle chapters are the work. If a chapter is "Why AI matters," delete it. They already bought.

Step 4. Draft. Do not format yet. Do not design a cover. Words first. Leave holes marked HOLE rather than stopping to research a tool you do not use.

Steps 5-8: voice, files, PDF, checkout

Step 5. Rewrite for voice. Read a page out loud. If you would not say it, delete it. Keep one example per chapter from real work.

Step 6. Add the working files: a template, a prompt block, a checklist. Buyers pay more when they can copy, not just read. Put the checklist on its own page at the back so they can print it.

Step 7. Export a clean PDF. Number the chapters. Put your email on the last page. Filename: something a tired person can find later, like landscaper-10-day-content-playbook.pdf.

Step 8. Checkout. Stripe Payment Link or Stan. Title matches the one-sentence offer. Description is five lines: who, result, time, what is in the file, price.

Steps 9-12: test, ask, learn, then stop

Step 9. Test-buy. Different browser, different email. Confirm the file arrives and opens on a phone.

Step 10. Ten personal messages. Not a broadcast. "I wrote this for [job]. $19. If it is useful: [link]. If not, ignore."

Step 11. After the first stranger sale, ask what they used. That sentence becomes your next ten posts. "Which chapter did you actually do?" is better than "Did you like it?"

Step 12. Only then add a second product or a $9 membership. Not before.

A worked example

Dana, one-person landscaping companies, $29

Dana already writes a few Google posts for her own company when she remembers. She is not a "content person." She is a person who got tired of a blank chat window every Sunday night. She writes a playbook called "10 days of Google-ready posts for a one-person landscaping company." Price: $29. File: one PDF plus a one-page caption template.

Her one sentence: "I help one-person landscapers get 10 Google-ready posts in 10 days using ChatGPT and job photos they already have, even if they hate writing and only have 20 minutes at night."

What she put in the file

Twelve chapters, each with a next action:

  1. What done looks like — 10 posts scheduled, each with a photo, a caption, and a phone-number line.
  2. Pull 10 photos off your phone from jobs you finished this month.
  3. Pick three post types only: before/after, this-week offer, FAQ.
  4. Write the three hooks you will reuse.
  5. Turn one finished job into a before/after caption.
  6. Write the "this week" offer without sounding cheap.
  7. Write the FAQ post that answers "why does this cost that?"
  8. Paste the batch prompt and generate the other seven captions.
  9. Put the phone number in the last line of every post. Same line, every time.
  10. The 10-day calendar (which post, which photo, which morning).
  11. What to do when nobody comments (you still posted; call three old clients).
  12. What you do next week (repeat the same three types; do not invent a fourth).

She included one prompt they paste with a photo description and a price, and a caption template with blanks. She did not include a module on personal branding.

How she sold the first copies

Day 3 she made a Stripe Payment Link titled the same as the sentence. She bought it from her other Gmail. The PDF opened on her phone. Then she sent ten texts: owners she had swapped trailers with, people from a local Facebook group she already helps, her cousin who runs a lawn crew, and old clients who ask how she "does the posting."

The text: "I put a short playbook up for one-person landscaping companies that need 10 Google posts. One file, $29, instant download. If that is useful: [link]. If not, no need to reply."

Two people she knew bought on day 3. A stranger bought on day 11 after she posted one before/after from her own yard with the last line "I wrote the 10-day sequence I use. $29. Link in bio." She asked the stranger which chapter they used. They said chapter 5, the before/after caption. That became her next five posts. She did not write book two.

Copy you can actually send

DM (text or email)

I put a short playbook live for [job]. One file, $19, instant download. If that is useful: [link]. If not, no need to reply.

Post

Blank chat is expensive. I wrote the sequence I actually use for [result]. $19. Link in bio.

Or, with a photo of the finished job, not the PDF cover:

This is 10 days of posts for a one-person [trade], written so you can do it after dinner. $29. Not another AI dump.

Listing title and body

Title: [Job] in [time] — a playbook for [person], not another AI dump.

Body, five lines:

Who it is for: [person].
What they finish: [result] in [time].
What they get: a PDF with [N] chapters, a paste-ready prompt, and a checklist.
What they need: [named tools], no extra subscriptions.
Price: $29. Instant download. Email me if it is not useful and I will refund.

Email to people who already know you

Subject: the [job] sequence, written down

I got tired of explaining this in pieces, so I wrote a short playbook. It is the order I actually use for [result], including the prompt and the checklist. $19, one file.

If you want it: [link].
If you do not, delete this. I will not follow up.

Reply to an objection

"I can just ask ChatGPT."

You can. The playbook is the order, the examples from [job], and the next action at the end of each chapter so you do not stall at a blank window. If you already have a sequence you like, you do not need this. If you keep starting over, $19 is cheaper than another Sunday night.

"I do not have time to read a book."

It is [N] short chapters. Chapter one is what done looks like. Chapter two is a 20-minute task. If you cannot do chapter two this week, wait.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1: outline and ugly draft

No public launch. Two evenings. Evening one: sticky note, one sentence, outline of 8-15 chapters with next actions. Evening two: ugly draft of the whole thing, holes marked HOLE. Do not open Canva. Finish the words. If you only have 45 minutes a night, do three chapters a night for four nights.

Week 2: rewrite, templates, checkout, ten asks

Rewrite out loud. Add one template and one prompt block. Export the PDF. Set Stripe or Stan. Test-buy from a second email on your phone. Send ten personal messages. First sale is allowed to be someone you know. Do not add a second product this week.

Week 3: teach one chapter, three times

Three posts or emails that teach one chapter. Soft link. Example: post the before/after caption formula. End with "I wrote the rest of the 10-day sequence. Link in bio." If you are not public, send three more personal messages instead of posts.

Week 4: fix, do not expand

Fix whatever the first buyers tripped on. A broken download. A prompt that needed one extra line. A chapter that assumed they had a desktop. Do not write book two. If nobody bought, send ten new asks to people who do the job, then look at the title. The title is usually the problem, not the prose.

Quality bar

Voice

Kitchen table, not conference stage. Short sentences. Named tools. Named times. If a paragraph could run in a motivational clip, cut it. Read the first page to someone who does the job. "I could do that" means you passed.

Structure

Every chapter: one point, one example, one next action. Put the working files where they can steal them — a box, a back page, a second PDF in the zip. Number the chapters. Use the same words in the title, the checkout, and the first paragraph.

Proof inside the file

One real example per chapter beats five generic screenshots. Blur names. Keep the texture: the actual price, the actual photo description, the bad first draft and the good second.

Metrics that matter (and vanity numbers to ignore)

Count these

  • Checkout works: self-purchase from a second email, file opens on a phone.
  • Messages sent: ten in the first 72 hours, then ten more if nobody bought.
  • Sales from people who are not you. One stranger sale is the real start.
  • "Which chapter did you use?" answers. That sentence is your next post.
  • Refunds. If you get more than one in the first ten sales, chapter one is unclear or the title oversold.

Ignore these

Landing page visits with no checkout. Follower counts. Cover polish. Word count. A playbook with 12 visitors and three sales is healthy. A playbook with 2,000 visitors and zero sales has a broken offer or a broken title.

A simple weekly log

One line a week: date, messages sent, sales, refunds, one buyer sentence.

Objections you will hear

"I can get this free from ChatGPT."

Plain answer: they can get a draft. They cannot get your order of operations, your examples from this job, and a next action at the end of every chapter. If they already have that sequence, they should not buy. If they keep staring at a blank window, $19 is the sequence.

"I do not know if I am qualified to write this."

If you have done the job three times and can name what went wrong the first time, you are qualified for a $19-$47 playbook. You are not applying to teach a university course. You are handing a tired person the notes you wish you had.

"Nobody will pay for a PDF."

People pay for PDFs every day when the job is named and the checkout is simple. Name the person, the result, and the time. Put the price on the page. Ask ten people.

"I need a course platform / a big list / a funnel."

You need Google Docs, a Payment Link, and ten messages. A course platform is how people delay chapter one. After a stranger has bought, you can put the same file on a nicer page. Not before.

"What if they want a refund?"

You refund them. One sentence. Then you ask what they expected. If two people say the same thing, you fix that chapter. Refunds are cheaper than a reputation for arguing.

How this feeds other paths

Products that grow out of the same notes

The playbook is often the first product because it is the sequence in your head, written down. The same notes become Niche prompt stacks (the prompt blocks, sold on their own), Ship a Claude or ChatGPT skill pack (the instructions, installed so they never paste again), and AI templates and SOPs (the checklist as a filled file).

Do not split them until the playbook has a stranger sale. If they say "can I just have the prompt," that is a prompt stack. If they say "I want this inside Claude," that is a skill pack.

Distribution that uses the same chapters

AI-assisted short-form: each chapter is a 20-second video. Same offer at the end. A weekly AI letter: one chapter taught, one link to the file. Your front door: a diagnostic that tells them which chapter they need, then the playbook.

Services and later products

Local businesses + AI and Productized AI service are how you get paid while the playbook is still a draft — do the job once for a nearby owner, then write what you did. A small AI membership comes after, when you have updates worth a monthly. Do not open all of those doors this month. Finish the playbook.

Mistakes that kill this path

The usual deaths

Writing for "entrepreneurs." That is not a buyer. Writing 80 pages of theory. Shipping a ChatGPT paste. Hiding the price. Launching with five bonuses and no core file. Waiting until it feels ready. Naming it "The AI Playbook" with no job in the title.

The quieter deaths

Building a course platform before a PDF. Adding a membership so you can delay shipping. Redesigning the cover four times. Putting your whole catalog in the listing. Using someone else's examples from a different trade.

The bar, again

The playbook is ready when a stranger can finish chapter one without you in the room. Ship at that bar. Fix after the first ten asks.

Tools and how you actually use them

Writing

Google Docs or Word. One document. The model you already use is enough for the ugly draft. The rewrite is you. Export PDF. Open it on your phone. If the font is tiny or the prompt boxes break, fix the Doc, export again.

Checkout

Stripe Payment Link or Stan Store. One product. Title = the offer sentence, shortened. After payment, the file. You do not need a full site. A simple page that repeats the five-line listing is enough.

The second email

Create it before you launch. Buy your own product. If the download never arrives, fix that before you send a single ask. This step feels small. It is the whole business on day three.

Ship checklist

Before anyone else sees it

  • One-sentence offer with all five brackets filled
  • 8-15 chapters, verb + object titles, each ending with a next action
  • First chapter is what done looks like; last chapter is next week
  • One real example, names blurred; one prompt they can paste; one checklist
  • Rewrite read out loud; hype cut
  • PDF opens on a phone; email on the last page

Before you send the ten asks

  • Checkout title matches the offer; price on the page ($19-$47)
  • Bought from a second email, different browser; file arrived and opened
  • Ten names written down; message copied, link tested
  • No second product, no bonus pile

After the first sale

  • Ask: which chapter did you actually use?
  • Fix the first trip-up
  • Do not start book two

FAQ

Do I need a big audience?

No. You need ten people who already know you or do the job, a working checkout, and a file that survives chapter one without you.

How long should it be?

Long enough that a tired person can finish the job, short enough that they will start tonight. Most first playbooks are 12-25 pages including the checklist. If you are at 80 pages, you wrote essays. Cut or split.

Can I use AI to write the whole thing?

You can use it to draft. You cannot ship the draft. The buyer is paying for your order, your examples, and your next actions. A pasted ChatGPT article with a cover is the fastest way to a refund.

What if I do not have screenshots or old emails?

Run the job once this week for yourself or for one person. Capture as you go. If you cannot run the job this week, this is not your path yet. Do a service job, then write.

Stripe or Stan?

Either. Stripe Payment Link is enough if you are comfortable with a raw link. Stan is enough if you want the page and the file in one place. Pick one tonight. Do not compare six platforms.

Should I put it on Etsy or Gumroad too?

Not in week one. Sell where you can send a link to ten people. After a stranger sale, you can add a marketplace if the title matches how people search.

What do I do after ten asks and zero sales?

Ask one of the ten: "Was the job wrong, the price wrong, or the timing wrong?" Then look at the title. Most first misses are a vague title. Rewrite the sentence. Send ten new asks. Do not rewrite the whole PDF yet.

Can I charge $97 on day one if I include a call?

You can, but you just built a service with a PDF attached. If you want the playbook path, keep the file at $19-$47 and do not include your calendar. If you want to get paid this week with your time, use Productized AI service and come back.

When do I write the second playbook?

After a stranger has bought the first, told you what they used, and you have fixed the trip-ups. Then only if they ask for the next job.

Is a playbook the same as a prompt pack?

No. A playbook is the sequence, with prompts inside it. A prompt stack is the prompts, with a one-page SOP for which to use when. Dana wrote a playbook because landscapers needed the 10-day order, not 200 clever lines.

PATH 02

Ship a Claude or ChatGPT skill pack

They already pay the model. They are paying you to stop starting from zero.

18 minute read · 4,000 words

Who this is for
People who use Claude, ChatGPT, or Gemini every week and keep rebuilding the same chat.
First sale
One $19 order from someone who used the model this week.
Time to first
3–10 days
Cash window
$19–$39
Tools
Claude Projects or Custom GPTs, a zip of the skill files, a one-page install, Stripe.
Leave this alone
A 200-prompt dump with no job in the title. Competing with free GitHub lists on volume.

What this actually is

A small appliance, not a prompt PDF

A skill pack is a working stack: instructions the model follows, examples of good output, and a way to install it in under ten minutes. It is not a PDF of prompts. It is closer to a small appliance. Plug it in, get the job.

The buyer has already decided AI is useful. Their pain is the blank window. Your job is to replace that window with a named job: weekly content, invoice follow-up, meeting notes into tasks, a listing description, a study plan.

The title is the product

If they cannot tell from the title what file they get and what job it does, they will not buy. "Claude Power Pack" is a mood. "Client follow-up skill for solo bookkeepers" is a product.

A skill pack ships as files they can paste or upload: a system-instructions file, one sample input, one sample output, and a one-page install. If you only send a list of prompts, you shipped a weaker version of Niche prompt stacks. This path is the installable version of a job.

What you are not selling

You are not selling access to a model. They already pay Anthropic or OpenAI. You are not selling 200 clever lines. You are selling the first message being raw material — an overdue invoice list, a messy email thread — and the model already knowing the format, the tone, and when to stop and ask.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This fits you if

You use Claude, ChatGPT, or Gemini every week and keep rebuilding the same chat. You have a job you have run in a window more than twice. You can write instructions as if you will never be in the room: role, inputs, output format, quality checks, what to ask if information is missing.

You do not need to be a developer. You need to have finished the job in a chat and been annoyed that you had to re-explain it the next time.

Skip this path if

Skip if you have never run the job in a model yourself. Skip if you want to compete with free GitHub lists on volume. Skip if your title would be "200 ChatGPT prompts." That market is noise, and this path loses when you play it.

If you need cash this week and you do not already live in these tools, write a playbook or take a service job first. A skill pack is fast only if the job is already in your fingers.

The buyer on the other side

The buyer is not a prompt tourist. They already have a Plus or Pro tab open. They are a bookkeeper, a recruiter, a clinic manager, a realtor who pastes the same messy input every Friday and rewrites the instructions from memory. They will pay $19 to never start from zero again.

The offer, named in one sentence

Fill this first

"I help [specific person] finish [named job] in [named tool] in under [time], even if they are not a prompt person." If you cannot name the tool and the job, you do not have a skill pack. You have a vibe.

Examples that work: a solo bookkeeper who needs client follow-up emails from an invoice list in Claude; a recruiter who needs a scored shortlist from a pile of messy resumes; a tradesperson who needs a job-to-Google-post in one paste.

What they get, said plainly

They get a zip: system instructions (or a Custom GPT / Claude Project recipe), a sample input, a sample output, and a one-page install they can follow on a tired night. They do not get a course. They do not get a community. They get the appliance.

Price the job, not the file count

First skill packs sell at $19. $29-$39 is fair when the install is clean, the sample is real, and you include a 20-second screen recording of blank chat versus the skill. Do not bundle 40 jobs to justify $49. One job, done, at $19, outsells a suitcase of half jobs.

What "done" looks like

The install test

Done means a person who did not write it can install it in under ten minutes and get a usable first output from the sample. If they need you on a call, it is not a skill pack yet. It is a service.

Write the install as clicks: where to open, what to paste, what to send first, how they know it worked. If a step is "set up your workspace," you hid the work.

The quality test

Run the skill on a real example. Save that output as the sample in the zip. Then run it on a broken example — missing amount, missing date, rude client note — and confirm it asks once and stops instead of guessing. Silent guessing is how people lose trust.

The sales test

Checkout title is the job. Price is $19. You have bought it from a second email. The zip opens. The install PDF is one page. You have asked five people who already use Claude or ChatGPT. Done is not a catalog page with six other ideas.

Pricing that holds

$19 is the default

One $19 order from someone who used the model this week is the first sale that matters. $19 matches "I already pay for Claude; this saves me the blank window." It is low enough to try tonight and high enough that you will answer a support email.

Do not price at $9. You will attract collectors who never install it, and you will hate the one-star "too simple" review from someone who wanted 200 prompts.

When $29-$39 is honest

Raise when you have: a 20-second screen, a real sample, failure modes in the instructions, and at least one stranger who installed it without you. That is a better product, not a different mood. Stay under $39 until you have a version history and a second related skill you can bundle.

What you never do

Do not promise it works on every model. Build for one: Claude Projects, Custom GPTs, or Gemini Gems. Say which one on the listing. If a buyer uses the other, refund and point them at the install they would need — or do not sell it to them. Do not add a $97 "with a setup call" on day one unless you mean to run Productized AI service instead.

The first 72 hours

Pick the job and write the room

Pick one job you have done in a chat more than twice. Open a new project. Write the system instructions as if you will never be in the room: role, inputs they must paste, output format, quality checks, what to ask if information is missing.

Paste this to get a first draft of the instructions, then rewrite every line in your voice:

Write system instructions for [tool] that a tired [person] will use. Job: [job]. The user's first message is only the raw material: [what they paste]. You will return [format]. If [critical field] is missing, ask once, then stop. Do not invent numbers. Include three quality checks you run before you show the draft.

Run it, package it, film it

Run it on a real example. Save the output as the sample. Write a one-page install: where to click, what to paste, how to test. Zip it. Price $19. Record a 20-second screen: blank chat versus the skill.

Put the video and the checkout in one place. Ask five people who already use Claude or ChatGPT. Do not explain the catalog of other ideas you have.

The 72-hour list

  • One job, written as system instructions
  • One real example output in the zip
  • One-page install a tired person can follow
  • Twenty-second screen recording
  • Five asks, not fifty features

The path, step by step

Steps 1-4: watch, write, fail, version

Step 1. Watch how the buyer actually works. If they live in email, do not build a Notion skill. If they live in Claude on a phone, the install cannot assume a desktop sidebar they have never opened. Sit with one real run of the job.

Step 2. Write the skill so the first message from the user is just the raw material. The skill should not need a prompt engineer on their side. If they have to write a paragraph of instructions after they install it, you failed.

Step 3. Add failure modes: "If the invoice amount is missing, ask once, then stop." Silent guessing is how people lose trust. List the three fields you will never invent. Put that list in the instructions, not in a blog post about your process.

Step 4. Version the file. v1 is allowed to be narrow. You will ship v1.1 after the first buyer. Put "v1.0" in the README and the filename. When you fix install wording, bump it. Buyers should know they can re-download.

Steps 5-8: package, title, prove, sell

Step 5. Package: README, skill or instructions file, sample input, sample output. That is enough. The README is the one-page install, not a manifesto. Filename the zip after the job: bookkeeper-client-followup-claude-v1.zip.

Step 6. Checkout title is the job. Thumbnail is the screen recording still. Description names the tool, the install time, and the sample. "Works in Claude Projects. Ten-minute install. Sample invoice list included."

Step 7. Record the 20-second screen. Left: blank chat, slow. Right or after: paste the raw list, get the follow-ups. No music. No intro. The last two seconds are the $19 line and the link.

Step 8. Sell where those users already complain: X threads, Reddit help posts, Discord. Help first. Link in profile. Do not dump the zip in a comment. Dump the result of the job, then let the profile hold the buy link.

Steps 9-12: ask, support, patch, stop

Step 9. Five personal asks to people who already use the model. "I built a [job] skill for [person]. $19, ten-minute install. If you want it: [link]. If not, ignore."

Step 10. After purchase, a three-line email: install, test with the sample, reply if it breaks. That reply is your product roadmap.

Step 11. Fix whatever the first buyer could not install. Ship v1.1. Same checkout, new zip. Email the people who already bought.

Step 12. Do not add a second skill until this one has a stranger sale. One appliance that works beats a store of toys.

A worked example

Marcus, solo bookkeepers, $19

Marcus is a solo bookkeeper. Every Friday he writes the same follow-up emails: invoice sent, invoice 7 days late, invoice 21 days late, polite close-the-loop. He used to paste a messy spreadsheet into Claude and spend twenty minutes restating the tone. He ships a Claude skill: "Client follow-up for solo bookkeepers." Price: $19.

His one sentence: "I help solo bookkeepers turn an invoice list into send-ready follow-up emails in Claude in one paste, even if they are not a prompt person."

What is in the zip

  • SKILL.txt — system instructions: you are writing follow-ups for a solo bookkeeper. Inputs: client name, invoice number, amount, days late, last contact. Output: subject line plus a short email in the bookkeeper's voice (plain, not cute). Never invent an amount or a date. If amount or days late is missing, ask once, then stop. Three tones: reminder, firm, final. Default to reminder unless days late is over 14.
  • SAMPLE-INPUT.txt — eight fake invoices, names blurred from a real week, amounts intact in spirit.
  • SAMPLE-OUTPUT.txt — the eight emails the skill produced, which Marcus actually sent after a human pass.
  • INSTALL.pdf — one page: open Claude, new Project, paste SKILL.txt into instructions, drop SAMPLE-INPUT as the first message, compare to SAMPLE-OUTPUT, then paste your own list.

He recorded 20 seconds: blank chat ("write follow-ups for these invoices") versus the Project with the skill. Same list, two very different first drafts.

How he sold it

He asked five people: two other bookkeepers in a Slack, his old firm colleague, a freelancer Discord, and a person who had tweeted "Claude keeps making up invoice amounts." He did not pitch a catalog. Two of the five bought. A stranger bought on day 6 after he replied to that tweet with the result (amounts correct, tone plain) and left the link in his profile.

The first buyer could not find "Project instructions" on mobile. Marcus added two screenshots to INSTALL.pdf, bumped to v1.1, and emailed both buyers. He did not start a second skill for tax organizers. Not yet.

Copy you can actually send

DM

I built a [job] skill for [person] who already uses Claude. Ten-minute install, $19. If that is useful: [link]. If not, no need to reply.

Post

I wasted two hours prompting this. The skill does it in one paste. $19.

Or, with the screen still:

Blank chat vs the follow-up skill. Same invoice list. Left one invented an amount. Right one asked. $19, install in 10 minutes. Built for solo bookkeepers. Not a prompt dump.

Listing

Title: Client follow-up skill for solo bookkeepers — install in 10 minutes.

Body:

What you get: Claude Project instructions, a sample invoice list, a sample of the emails, and a one-page install.
What you do: paste your list. Get send-ready follow-ups. It will not invent amounts.
What you need: Claude (Projects). Not a prompt engineer.
Price: $19. Instant zip. Email me if it breaks.

Email after purchase

Subject: install, then test with the sample

  1. Open the INSTALL page. Ten minutes.
  2. Run SAMPLE-INPUT first. Match it against SAMPLE-OUTPUT so you know it loaded.
  3. Then paste your own list. If it breaks, reply to this with a screenshot. I will fix the file.

Reply to an objection

"Can't I just save a prompt?"

You can. A skill pack is the instructions plus failure modes plus a sample so you know it loaded. If your saved prompt already asks for missing amounts and never invents them, you do not need this. If you rewrite the instructions every Friday, $19 is cheaper than another Friday.

"Does it work in ChatGPT?"

This one is built for Claude Projects. I will not pretend it is the same in a Custom GPT. If you only use ChatGPT, do not buy this version. Refund if you already did.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Days 1-2: write and test

One job you already run. Write the instructions. Run a real example. Run a broken example. Save both. You can do this in two evenings after work. Do not design a landing page yet.

Day 3: package and checkout

README, skill file, sample in, sample out. Zip. $19 Payment Link or Stan. Buy it from a second email. Record the 20-second screen on your phone if you have to.

Days 4-7: five asks, one post a day

Five personal messages. Post the screen recording once a day in one place — same clip, same offer. Help in two threads where people complain about the job. Link stays in the profile.

Week 2: patch, then wait

Fix whatever the first buyer could not install. Ship v1.1. Do not add a second skill until this one has a stranger sale. If nobody bought, the title or the tool is wrong. Ask one of the five: "Would you even install this?" Then change the title, not the whole stack.

Quality bar

Instructions that survive you leaving the room

Role in one line. Inputs named. Output format named. Three quality checks. A stop rule for missing facts. No "be helpful" filler. No claim that it replaces a bookkeeper, a lawyer, or a clinician. It drafts. They send.

The sample is real enough to trust

Blur names. Keep the ugly: a client who pays late every time, a line with a missing PO, a note in all caps. If the sample is a perfect table, the skill will look smart and fail on Friday.

Install a tired person can finish

One page. Numbered clicks. One screenshot if a menu is hidden. A line that says "you will know it worked when the sample output looks like this." If they have to watch a 12-minute video, you wrote a course. Cut it.

Metrics that matter (and vanity numbers to ignore)

Count these

  • Installs that produce a first output from the sample (ask: "Did the sample run?")
  • Stranger sales at $19
  • "It broke" emails — these are the roadmap
  • Time-to-install reports. If two people say it took 30 minutes, the one-pager failed
  • Refunds because of the wrong model. That is a listing problem. Fix the title.

Ignore these

Stars on a prompt marketplace. How many skills you listed. GitHub-style star counts. Views on the screen recording if nobody clicks the checkout. Lines of instructions. "Community" member counts you do not have yet.

One line a week

Date, asks sent, sales, break-emails, version number. If v1.0 never becomes v1.1, you are not talking to buyers.

Objections you will hear

"I already have a Custom GPT / a Project."

Good. This is a better set of instructions plus a sample so they can test it. If theirs already does the job, they should not buy. If they keep tweaking it every week, $19 is the freeze-frame that works.

"Skills are going to be free from the labs."

Some will be. Generic ones. A client-follow-up skill that knows a solo bookkeeper's tone and will not invent an amount is not on the default home screen. You are selling the job, not the concept of a skill.

"I am not technical."

Neither is the buyer, if you wrote the install. If you cannot write the install without the word "repo," this path will hurt. Use Claude Projects or Custom GPTs, not a local install.

"What if the model changes and it breaks?"

You version the file. You date the README: "Tested on Claude, [month year]." You email v1.1 to people who bought. That is the job after the first sale. It is also why you do not sell 40 jobs in one zip — you cannot maintain them.

"Can you just set it up for me?"

Yes, as a service, for a different price. This path is the zip. If you want setup-for-hire, that is Custom project setups or Productized AI service. Do not throw a call into a $19 product.

How this feeds other paths

Same job, other shapes

The instructions inside the skill are a Niche prompt stack if someone wants to paste instead of install. The sequence you used to build it is Sell an AI playbook. The checklist in the README is an AI template. Let the first buyers tell you which shape they asked for.

Where it gets sold

AI-assisted short-form: the 20-second blank-vs-skill clip is the whole path. A weekly AI letter: one failure mode explained, link to the zip. Your front door: a diagnostic ("do you rewrite the same Claude chat every Friday?") then the skill.

When it becomes a service or a membership

Local businesses + AI: install the same skill for a nearby owner and charge a setup fee. Custom project setups and Automation setups are the grown-up version for people who want it connected to their inbox. A small AI membership is how you send v1.2, v1.3 as the model changes — after the $19 file has strangers using it.

Do not open a membership to avoid shipping v1.

Mistakes that kill this path

The listed ones

Shipping prompts without install steps. Promising it works on every model. Bundling 40 jobs so none of them are good. Using other people's copyrighted prompt packs as your file.

The quieter ones

A title with no job. A sample that is too clean. Instructions that guess at missing money or medical facts. A 14-page install. A video instead of a one-pager. Asking fifty people for feature ideas before five people have installed v1. Naming it after yourself instead of the job.

The trust ones

Invented numbers. Invented legal language. A "medical" or "legal" skill that claims it is compliant. This path drafts operational writing. It does not practice. Put that limit in the instructions and on the listing.

Tools and how you actually use them

Where the skill lives

Claude Projects: paste the instructions into the project. Custom GPTs: paste into the GPT builder instructions. Gemini Gems: same idea, different menu. Pick one for v1. Write the install for that one only.

You are not shipping an API app. You are shipping text files a human pastes. If you need Zapier, you wandered into Automation setups.

The zip and the page

Folder: README (the install), SKILL or INSTRUCTIONS, SAMPLE-INPUT, SAMPLE-OUTPUT. Zip it. Stripe Payment Link or Stan delivers the zip. That is the stack from the original tools line. Do not add a course platform.

The 20-second screen

Phone screen record is enough. Desktop is cleaner if the buyer is on desktop. Show the blank chat failing or stalling, then the skill succeeding on the same paste. No intro bumper. The still from second 3 is the thumbnail.

Ship checklist

Before you zip

  • One job in the title
  • Instructions: role, inputs, output format, quality checks, stop rules
  • Will not invent named fields (amounts, dates, diagnoses)
  • Real sample input and sample output
  • Broken-example test: it asked, then stopped
  • One-page install with the actual clicks
  • Version number on the README

Before you ask anyone

  • Zip opens on a phone
  • Checkout title = the job; price $19
  • Self-purchase from a second email
  • 20-second screen recorded
  • Five names who already use the model
  • After-purchase three-line email written

After the first install

  • Fix the install trip-up
  • Ship v1.1 to people who bought
  • No second skill until a stranger sale

FAQ

Is this just a prompt pack?

No. A prompt pack is paste-as-you-go. A skill pack is installed once so the first message can be the raw material. If they want 25 separate prompts for a clinic week, that is Niche prompt stacks. If they want follow-up to live in Claude, that is this.

Claude, ChatGPT, or Gemini?

Pick the one you actually use for this job. Write for that one. Saying "works everywhere" is how you get refunds.

Do I need to code?

No. You need to write instructions and a one-page install. If you are building an API wrapper, you left this path.

What if Anthropic or OpenAI ships this exact skill?

Then you sell the version that knows this job's tone and failure modes, or you move to the playbook and the service. Generic will always be free later. Specific stays yours if the sample is real.

How many skills should I launch with?

One. Five asks. One stranger sale. Then a second skill only if they ask for the next job.

Can I put it on a GPT store or a skill directory?

After it works at $19 on your own checkout. Directories are extra. They are not the first 72 hours.

What do I do when a buyer says it "sounds like AI"?

The instructions need their voice. Add three sample sentences they actually send. Marcus put two real follow-ups he had already mailed (names changed) into SKILL.txt under "match this tone." That killed the peppy bot voice.

Is $19 too low if it saves a Friday every week?

It is the right first price. Raise to $29-$39 after a stranger has installed it and you have the screen and the failure modes. Do not start at $79 with no proof.

What if they want it connected to Gmail?

That is Automation setups or Custom project setups, priced as a project. Do not promise inbox connection inside a $19 zip.

Can I use someone else's skill as a base?

No. Do not use other people's copyrighted prompt packs as your file. Write the instructions from the job you actually do. The scar tissue is the product.

PATH 03

Niche prompt stacks

Twenty-five prompts that finish a job beat a thousand that sound clever.

17 minute read · 3,716 words

Who this is for
A trade or hobby you know from the inside: realtors, tutors, gyms, writers, clinics.
First sale
A niche buyer who uses the first prompt the same day.
Time to first
5–14 days
Cash window
$12–$29
Tools
A PDF or Notion doc, three before/after examples, Stripe or Etsy or Stan.
Leave this alone
Generic ‘1,000 ChatGPT prompts.’ That market is dead and noisy.

What this actually is

Why niche wins

Generic prompt packs are free on every platform. A stack for "pediatric clinic SMS that stays compliant-sounding and human" is not. You are selling vocabulary and constraints the general pack does not have: the words that trade uses, the lines it will not cross, the format that actually gets sent.

Twenty-five prompts that finish a job beat a thousand that sound clever. The buyer is not collecting prompts. They are trying to get through this week's writing: texts, emails, captions, listings, lesson notes.

The three parts every prompt needs

Every prompt in the stack needs three parts: inputs (what they paste), output format (what they get), and a done check (how they know it is usable). Without those, it is a toy.

A prompt that says "write a reminder text" is free and useless. A prompt that says "Paste parent first name, child first name, appointment day, appointment time, clinic phone. Return one SMS under 160 characters. No diagnosis language. No 'just checking in.' Done when a tired parent could tap send." is a product.

What you are not selling

You are not selling medical, legal, or financial advice. You are not selling a promise that a model is "HIPAA compliant" or "court ready." You are selling writing help for repeating operational jobs — reminders, reschedules, bios, captions — with the constraints a practitioner already lives with. If you cannot name those constraints from the inside, skip this niche.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This fits you if

You know a trade or hobby from the inside: realtors, tutors, gyms, writers, clinics. You have sat in the job long enough to list the ten writing tasks people hate. You can tell a good reminder from a creepy one. You already belong to a group or forum where those people talk.

You do not need a huge audience. You need the niche word they would search, and enough respect in that room to share once without getting banned.

Skip this path if

Skip if your only edge is "I use ChatGPT a lot." Skip if you planned to copy someone else's stack and swap the niche word. Skip if you want to write for "everyone who uses AI." That is the dead market in the leave-alone line: generic 1,000-prompt dumps.

Skip medical, legal, or financial claims. If the job is diagnosis, contracts, or investment advice, this path is the wrong container. Operational writing around those fields can be fine (appointment SMS, not treatment plans). Know the line.

The buyer

A niche buyer who uses the first prompt the same day. They have a shift or a showing tomorrow. They will not "go through the course." They will paste one input and send one message. Your stack has to survive that first paste.

The offer, named in one sentence

Fill this

"I help [niche person] write [repeating job] every week with 25 prompts that include inputs, output format, and a done check, even if they hate sounding like a robot."

Title includes the niche word they would search. "25 prompts for pediatric clinic SMS and emails" is searchable. "Ultimate AI Prompt Vault" is not.

What they get

A PDF or a Notion doc. Twenty-five prompts covering a month of the weekly jobs. Three before/after examples, names blurred. A one-page SOP: which prompt when. Price $12-$29. Default $19.

What they do not get

A thousand prompts. A claim of compliance. A community. A new model. They get the vocabulary and the constraints, in an order that matches a real week.

What "done" looks like

The file

Twenty-five prompts. Each has inputs, output format, a constraint ("I will not..."), and a done check. A one-page SOP that maps the week: Monday reminder, Tuesday waitlist, and so on. Three before/after pairs from realistic inputs. A dated line: "Tested on [model], [month year]."

If a prompt is clever but would never be sent in that trade, it is not done. Cut it.

The proof

You have run all 25 on real or realistic inputs. You kept the three best before/after pairs. Names blurred. Texture kept: the short-notice cancellation, the parent who texts at 9 p.m., the price question you are allowed to answer.

The checkout

Title has the niche word. Price is on the page. You have bought it from a second email. You have asked, helpfully, once, in a group you already belong to. Done is not five marketplace listings and a brand name.

Pricing that holds

$19 is the home price

$12 is for a tiny stack (10 prompts, one job). $19 is the default for 25 prompts plus SOP plus three proofs. $29 is fair if you add a one-page weekly calendar. Stay in the $12-$29 window from the original cash line. This is not a $97 course.

Why cheap still needs a spine

A $19 file with no done checks is a worse buy than a $19 file with 25 complete prompts. Do not race to $9 to "beat" Etsy. Race to a title that matches search and a first prompt they can use today.

When to bundle

Week 4, if they ask: add the calendar or checklist and raise to $29, or keep $19 and add the extra page. Do not invent a $49 "pro vault" with the same 25 prompts in a different font.

The first 72 hours

List the jobs, then write

List the ten repeating writing jobs in that niche. Pick the five that happen every week. Write five prompts each, or write 25 that cover a month of work. Do not start with "fun" prompts. Start with the Friday-afternoon ones.

Paste this to draft a prompt, then rewrite it in the trade's voice:

Write a prompt for [person] who has to [job] every week. The prompt must have: (1) inputs they paste, named; (2) output format, including length; (3) a hard constraint of what it will not say; (4) a done check in one line. Kitchen-table English. No hype. No medical, legal, or financial claims.

Run, proof, SOP, price

Run all 25 on real or realistic inputs. Keep the three best before/after pairs as proof. Write a one-page SOP: which prompt when. Price $19. Title includes the niche word they would search. Ask in the Facebook group or forum you already belong to — helpfully, once.

Helpfully means: answer someone's actual question with a useful line, then mention the file once in your profile or in a comment if the group allows it. Not a dump of 25 prompts in the thread.

The 72-hour bar

If you do not already know the ten jobs, you do not have 72 hours. You have research. Sit with a practitioner (even if it is you) before you write a single prompt.

The path, step by step

Steps 1-4: sit, form, polish, prove

Step 1. Sit with a practitioner, even if it is you. Write the jobs they hate writing. Ten of them. Star the five that happen every week. Those five become the spine of the 25.

Step 2. For each job, write the prompt as a form: "Paste [x]. I will return [y] in [format]. I will not [constraint]." If you cannot fill those blanks, you do not have a prompt. You have a slogan.

Step 3. Add a second-pass prompt: "Tighten this. Keep the facts. Cut the fluff." Bundles sell when there is a polish step. Pair it with the draft prompt so they are not stuck with a first-pass robot paragraph.

Step 4. Show three before/after. Blur names. Keep the texture of real work. Before is what they would have sent (or a generic ChatGPT dump). After is what your prompt produced, edited by you so it is actually sendable.

Steps 5-8: date, SOP, file, title

Step 5. Update when the model changes. A dated "tested on [month year]" line builds trust. Put it on page one and next to the three proofs.

Step 6. Write the one-page SOP. A table: day or trigger, job, prompt number, where it gets sent (SMS, email, Google). This is the difference between a pile and a stack.

Step 7. Put it in a PDF or a Notion doc. PDF is simpler for Stripe and Etsy. Notion is fine if you include a duplicate-this link and a PDF backup. Three before/after screenshots in the file, not only on the sales page.

Step 8. Title includes the niche word they would search. "25 prompts for pediatric clinic SMS and parent emails — inputs, format, done check." Not "AI magic for healthcare creators."

Steps 9-12: checkout, share, listen, stop

Step 9. Checkout. Stripe, Etsy, or Stan. Sell on your site first. Marketplace second if the titles match search. Test-buy from a second email.

Step 10. Ten personal shares in the niche: DMs, a helpful comment, a post in a group you already belong to, once. Help first.

Step 11. Ask the first buyer which prompt they used the same day. That prompt becomes your public example.

Step 12. Only add a calendar or a second stack if they ask. Do not copy someone else's stack and swap the niche word. That is both a mistake and a way to get a file that does not match how the job is actually done.

A worked example

Priya, pediatric clinic SMS and emails, $19

Priya has worked the front desk of a pediatric clinic. She knows the week: reminder texts, waitlist offers, "we have a cancellation" messages, after-visit thank-yous, vaccine-clinic hours, school-note instructions, no-show follow-ups, form reminders, weather closures, and the "please use the patient portal" redirect. She does not write treatment advice. She writes the operational messages parents actually need.

Her stack: "25 prompts for pediatric clinic SMS and parent emails." Price: $19. File: PDF plus a one-page SOP.

Her one sentence: "I help pediatric clinic staff write the week's SMS and emails in 25 prompts with inputs, format, and a done check, even if they are scared of sounding cold or of saying the wrong medical thing."

What the 25 cover

Five weekly jobs, five prompts each:

  1. Appointment reminders (SMS, 24-hour, same-day, sibling double-book, interpreter need).
  2. Schedule changes (cancellation, waitlist offer, weather, provider out, running late).
  3. After-visit admin (thank you, forms still due, portal how-to, school note pickup, billing office hours — not bills as advice).
  4. Front-desk FAQs the clinic is allowed to answer (hours, parking, what to bring, sick-visit vs well-visit booking, "we cannot diagnose by text").
  5. Polish passes (shorten to 160 characters, warmer without being cute, Spanish-language draft to be checked by a bilingual staffer, cut diagnosis language, done-check against the clinic's "never say" list).

Every prompt starts with Paste: [fields]. Every prompt ends with Done when: [send test]. Every prompt includes I will not: diagnose, guess a result, promise a clinical outcome, or scold a parent.

How she proved and sold it

She ran all 25 on realistic inputs (fake names, real rhythms). She kept three before/afters: a reminder that sounded like a bill collector, a waitlist text that leaked another child's name (her prompt forbids extra names), and a thank-you email that invented a follow-up plan (her prompt forbids plans). Those three are why someone trusts the file.

She asked once in a clinic-managers Facebook group she already helped in: she answered a thread about no-show texts with two principles (no shame, one next step, no clinical content), then linked her profile. Listing title had "pediatric clinic SMS." Two people from the group bought. A stranger found it on day 10 because the title matched what they searched. She asked what they used first. Prompt 3, same-day reminder. She did not add a "HIPAA pack." She dated the PDF "tested March 2026."

Copy you can actually send

DM

I put together 25 prompts for [niche job] — each one has inputs, output format, and a done check. $19. If that is useful: [link]. If not, ignore.

Post

Generic prompt packs are free. This one is for [person] who has to write [job] every week. $19.

Listing

Title: 25 prompts for [niche job] — inputs, output format, done check. Tested this month.

Body:

Who: [person] who writes [job] every week.
What: 25 prompts, a one-page SOP for which to use when, three before/afters.
What it will not do: medical, legal, or financial claims. No "compliance certificate."
Tested on [model], [month year].
Price: $19. Instant download.

Email

Subject: 25 prompts for [job], not 1,000 generic ones

I was tired of [person] staring at a blank text window every [day]. Each prompt tells you what to paste, what you get, and how you know it is sendable. $19.

[link]
If the niche is wrong, delete this.

Reply to an objection

"I already have ChatGPT."

Good. These prompts are the constraints and the vocabulary for [niche], not a new model. If your current prompt already refuses extra names, diagnosis language, and 200-character rambling, you do not need this. If you rewrite the instructions every time, $19 is the freeze.

"Is this HIPAA / legally compliant?"

No pack of prompts is a compliance program. These are writing aids for operational messages. Your clinic's policies still apply. I will not sell you a "HIPAA prompt." I will sell you SMS that stay human and avoid diagnosis language. If you need a compliance vendor, that is a different purchase.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1: 25 prompts and three proofs

Two evenings to list the jobs, three evenings to write and run 25. Keep the three best before/afters. Do not list it yet if you have not run them all. A prompt you have not run is a guess.

Week 2: listing live, ten personal shares

Checkout. Title with the niche word. Ten shares in the niche: DMs, one helpful group comment, one post. Help first. First sale can be someone you know who does the job.

Week 3: add the SOP

If buyers ask "which one do I use on Monday," you needed the SOP. Add the one-page which-prompt-when. Email it to people who already bought. Do not start a second niche.

Week 4: bundle only if they ask

A calendar or checklist if they ask for it. $19 stays, or $29 for the bundle. Update the "tested on [month year]" line if the model moved. Do not copy someone else's stack and swap the niche word.

Quality bar

Every prompt is a form

Paste [x]. I will return [y] in [format]. I will not [constraint]. Done when [check]. If a prompt is a clever one-liner, it fails the bar.

It matches how the job is actually done

Clever prompts that do not match the job are a listed mistake. Sit with a practitioner. If clinics send SMS under 160 characters, do not return a paragraph. If they are not allowed to name another patient, the prompt forbids extra names.

No claims you cannot stand behind

No medical, legal, or financial claims. No "HIPAA compliant prompts." No "this will win your case." Operational writing only. Put the limit on page one and in every clinical-adjacent prompt: "I will not diagnose, guess a result, or tell a parent to skip a visit."

Metrics that matter (and vanity numbers to ignore)

Count these

  • First-prompt-used-the-same-day (ask the buyer)
  • Stranger sales
  • Which prompt number they used
  • Refunds for "I thought this was compliance / medical advice" — listing problem
  • Prompts that failed the run-through and got rewritten before launch

Ignore these

Prompt count above 25. Etsy hearts. "1,000 prompts" as a flex. Downloads of a free 5-prompt teaser that never converts. Page views from people searching "free ChatGPT prompts."

One line a week

Date, shares, sales, prompt-used-first, tested-on date. If the tested-on date is six months old, run the 25 again before you advertise.

Objections you will hear

"There are free prompt lists everywhere."

Yes. Generic ones. This stack is the vocabulary and constraints for [niche]. If a free list already has your "I will not" line and your done check, use the free list. If it writes a paragraph a parent will not send, $19 is the niche version.

"Is this HIPAA / legally safe?"

No prompt pack is a compliance program. These are writing aids for operational messages. Clinic policy still applies. I will refund you if you bought thinking this was a legal product. It is not.

"Can't I write my own prompts?"

You can. You are paying for 25 that already ran on realistic inputs, with before/afters, and a SOP for which to use when. If you like writing prompts, write them. If you have a clinic week to get through, buy the stack.

"Will they still work next month?"

Models move. The file is dated. When they move, you run the 25 again and send v1.1. That is why you sell 25 for one niche, not 1,000 for everyone.

"Can you add clinical advice prompts?"

No. Operational writing only. That is a hard line on this path. If they need clinical content, they need a clinician, not a $19 PDF.

How this feeds other paths

Products that are the same job in another shape

The SOP and the sequence become Sell an AI playbook. The installed version of the best five prompts becomes Ship a Claude or ChatGPT skill pack. The calendar becomes AI templates and SOPs. Do not split until a stranger has used prompt one the same day.

Where the before/afters go

AI-assisted short-form: one before/after per video. A weekly AI letter: one prompt taught, link to the 25. Your front door: "which of these five weekly jobs hurts" then the stack. Live drops of AI packs: a dated zip when the model changes.

Services

Local businesses + AI and Productized AI service: sit with one clinic, write their 25, charge a project fee, then productize the generic version with names removed. Custom project setups if they want it inside their SMS tool. Do not promise that inside the $19 PDF.

Mistakes that kill this path

The listed ones

Clever prompts that do not match how the job is actually done. Medical, legal, or financial claims. Copying someone else's stack and swapping the niche word.

The quiet ones

A title without the niche word. Prompts with no done check. Before/afters that are fake-clean. A thousand-prompt dump to "look like value." Promising HIPAA, court, or tax outcomes. Selling to "entrepreneurs." Skipping the run-through so you only discover a bad prompt after a refund.

The ethics ones

Do not put another child's name in a waitlist text example. Do not invent clinical advice to make a before/after dramatic. Do not scrape a clinic's real SMS. Blur, reconstruct, respect the room.

Tools and how you actually use them

The file

A PDF or a Notion doc. PDF is easier to deliver. Notion is fine with a duplicate link and a PDF export as backup. Three before/after examples in the file. Stripe, Etsy, or Stan for checkout. That is the original tools line. Do not add a prompt marketplace account in week one.

How you run the 25

One sitting per job family. Paste realistic inputs. Save the output. If you would not send it, rewrite the prompt, not the output. The product is the prompt that produces sendable work, not a hand-edited miracle you cannot reproduce.

Dated tests

Page one: "Tested on [model], [month year]." When the model changes, rerun the three proofs. If they break, fix the prompts, bump the date, email buyers if you have their addresses.

Ship checklist

Before the file is a product

  • Niche word in the title
  • 25 prompts, each with inputs, output format, constraint, done check
  • All 25 run on realistic inputs
  • Three before/afters, names blurred
  • One-page SOP: which prompt when
  • No medical, legal, or financial claims
  • "Tested on [month year]" on page one
  • Second-pass polish prompts included

Before you share

  • Price $19 (or $12-$29)
  • Self-purchase works
  • Ten personal shares listed
  • One helpful group ask, once
  • Refund line for people who wanted a compliance product

After the first use

  • Ask which prompt they used the same day
  • Fix that prompt if they tripped
  • Do not add a second niche

FAQ

Why 25? Why not 100?

Twenty-five that finish a month of weekly jobs beat 100 clever orphans. If you have more, you probably have a second stack. Sell this one first.

PDF or Notion?

Either. PDF for Stripe and Etsy. Notion if your buyer already lives there. Include the three proofs in both cases.

Can I sell on Etsy in week one?

You can. Site first is cleaner so you can email v1.1. Etsy second if the title matches search. Generic "ChatGPT prompts" on Etsy is a graveyard. "Pediatric clinic SMS prompts" is a search.

What if I am not in that trade?

Sit with a practitioner or skip the niche. Swapping the niche word on someone else's stack is a listed mistake and it produces prompts that do not match the job.

Can I include clinical or legal language?

No. Operational writing only. No medical, legal, or financial claims. If Priya's clinic stack starts drafting treatment plans, she cut those prompts. Appointment SMS, not care.

Do I need before/afters on the sales page?

Yes. Three. Blur names. Keep the texture. They are the reason a stranger believes this is not another free list.

$12 or $19?

$19 for 25 plus SOP plus proofs. $12 if you only have one job and ten prompts. $29 if they asked for the calendar. Stay in $12-$29.

What do I do when the model changes?

Rerun the 25. Fix what broke. Date the file. Email v1.1 if you can. That is why you do not sell 1,000.

Can I copy a competitor and change "realtor" to "clinic"?

No. Copying someone else's stack and swapping the niche word is how you ship clever prompts that do not match the job. Write from the inside.

Is this the same as a skill pack?

No. Prompt stacks are paste-as-you-go. Skill packs are installed so the first message is raw material. If they want 25 separate jobs for a clinic week, this path. If they want one follow-up machine in Claude, Ship a Claude or ChatGPT skill pack.

PATH 04

AI-assisted short-form

AI writes the outline. You say it. The offer sits at the end, every time.

17 minute read · 3,668 words

Who this is for
You can talk to a camera or screen-record, and you have one thing to sell.
First sale
A profile visit that becomes an email or a $19 checkout.
Time to first
14–30 days of posting
Cash window
Traffic to a $19–$47 file
Tools
Phone, CapCut, one platform for 30 days, Stan or Beacons.
Leave this alone
Five platforms. Faceless slideshows with no offer. Buying views.
