What this actually is

The job of a video

Every video does one of three things: teach a small piece, diagnose a mistake, or send them to the next step. If it does none of those, it is entertainment you are not getting paid for.

This path is not "become a creator." It is 14-30 days of posting on one platform so a profile visit becomes an email or a $19 checkout. The offer sits at the end, every time. Same offer. Same link in the bio. You already have one thing to sell — a playbook, a skill pack, a prompt stack, an SOP. Short-form is how strangers find it.

What AI is for

AI is useful for hooks and outlines. It is poison if you read it like a press release. Generate ten hooks. Speak the three you would actually say to a friend. Cut the rest.

You do not post AI voiceover over stock clips and call it a business. Faceless slideshows with no offer are in the leave-alone line. If you will not show your face, screen-record the work. Either way, a human says the words.

What this is not

Not five platforms. Not buying views. Not a 20-second tour of your whole catalog. Not waiting for the algorithm to "pick you" before you put a link in the bio. The algorithm is not the bottleneck. Volume of honest posts is.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This fits you if

You can talk to a camera or screen-record, and you have one thing to sell. You do not need to like how you look. You need to be willing to post 30 times on one app without switching when a post dies.

You already have the file, or you will have it in week 1 (a $19 SOP is enough). Short-form without an offer is a hobby. Short-form with a $19-$47 file is this path.

Skip this path if

Skip if you will not press record. Skip if you planned to run TikTok, Instagram, Shorts, LinkedIn, and Pinterest "to be safe." Skip if you want to buy views or followers. Skip if you have no file and no email capture — then go write the playbook or the SOP first.

If you freeze on camera, screen-record the job: the SOP, the prompt, the before/after. Your voice on the work is enough. If you will not even do that, this path will punish you for 30 days and then you will quit.

The honest middle

Shy is allowed. Inconsistent is not. One platform, one offer, 30 days. That is the deal.

The offer, named in one sentence

One line in the bio

The bio is the offer. Not a menu. Not "links, courses, coaching, merch." One file, one price, one link (Stan or Beacons). Example: "The 1-page [job] SOP. $19. I post how I use it."

If you cannot name the file in one line, you do not have an offer. Go back to Sell an AI playbook or AI templates and SOPs and ship the file, then return.

What every video points at

The same checkout. Teach a piece of the SOP, diagnose a mistake the SOP prevents, or walk through one step. Last line, spoken: "The file is $19. Link in bio." Same words, every time, so people who watch three videos know the next step without thinking.

Price on the path

This path does not sell the videos. It sells traffic to a $19-$47 file. If you have nothing at checkout, you are posting for free. Put the file up before day 1 of posting, even if it is ugly. You can pretty it later. You cannot retrofit an offer onto 30 videos with no link.

What "done" looks like

After 72 hours

One platform chosen. One-line offer in the bio. Checkout works. Three 20-second videos filmed (a mistake, a principle, a walkthrough). One posted today. Five more batched or scheduled. Twenty hooks on a note, three of them spoken in your mouth.

After 30 days

Thirty posts on that one platform. Same offer at the end. Comments read; at least five scripts taken from objections. Five shapes that got saves or comments, kept. The rest, retired. At least one profile visit that became an email or a $19 checkout — that is the first sale this path names.

What done is not

A viral post with no link. A second platform "because this one is dead." A new offer in week 2. A ring light haul. Done is boring on purpose: volume, one file, one place.

Pricing that holds

The file, not the video

You are not selling a "content package." You are pointing at a $19-$47 file. $19 SOP is the cleanest first product for this path. $29-$47 if the file already has templates. Do not launch a $97 cohort from a 20-second clip. The clip cannot hold that weight yet.

Do not hide the price

Say "$19" out loud at the end. People who will never buy still respect a number. People who might buy should not have to DM you to hear it. Pin a comment with the next step if the platform allows it: "SOP is $19. Link in bio."

Views are not revenue

Buying views is in the leave-alone line. A cheap view on a video with no offer still converts to zero. Spend nothing on ads until a stranger has bought from organic posts and you know which shape they watched. Creator ads with AI scripts is a later path, not day one.

The first 72 hours

Pick the room and the line

Pick one platform. TikTok, Instagram, or YouTube Shorts — not all three. Write the one-line offer that every video can point to. Put it in the bio with Stan or Beacons. Test the link on your phone.

If you already spend time watching one of those apps, post there. Do not pick the "serious" platform you never open.

Hooks, three films, post today

Generate 20 hooks. Film three 20-second videos: a mistake, a principle, a walkthrough. Same offer at the end. Same link in the bio.

Paste this, then speak only the lines you would say to a friend:

Give me 20 spoken hooks for a 20-second video. Buyer: [person]. File I sell: [SOP name], $19. Each hook is one sentence I would say in a kitchen. Pattern: "Stop doing [common thing] if you want [result]." No hype. No "wait for it." No hashtags in the hook.

Publish one a day starting today. Batch the next five on day three. That is the 72 hours. The algorithm is not the bottleneck. Volume of honest posts is.

Do not do these in the 72

Do not buy a ring light if your phone camera works. Do not open a second account. Do not design a brand kit. Do not wait until Monday.

The path, step by step

Steps 1-4: offer, hook, style, batch

Step 1. One offer in the bio. Not a menu. Stan or Beacons, one button, one file. If you have two products, pick the $19 one for 30 days. The other can wait.

Step 2. A hook formula: "Stop doing [common thing] if you want [result]." Then show the better way in 15 seconds. The hook is spoken in the first second, also on-screen for people who watch muted.

Step 3. On-screen text for people who watch muted. Your face or your screen — pick one style and keep it for 30 days. Switching from talking-head to slideshow to car-vlog trains nobody, including you.

Step 4. Batch. Two hours, ten videos, beats ten days of "I should post." Phone on a stack of books. Same shirt is fine. Same wall is fine. CapCut to add captions. Export, schedule or dump into a folder named by day.

Steps 5-8: comments, pin, types, end card

Step 5. Read comments. The objections are next week's scripts. Paste them into AI and ask for outlines, then rewrite. "This is too simple" becomes a video that shows the mistake of overbuilding. "I do not have time" becomes a 20-second timer of you doing one step.

Step 6. Pin a comment with the next step if the platform allows it. "SOP is $19. Link in bio. One job, not a catalog." Do this on every post for 30 days so you are not relying on people to read a tiny bio.

Step 7. Film the three types on a loop: a mistake, a principle, a walkthrough. You do not need a fourth type in month one. Variety is how people stall.

Step 8. End card, spoken, every time: "The file is $19. Link in bio. One job, not a catalog." Same words. Boredom here is a feature. Recognition is the point.

Steps 9-12: captions, 30, keep, do not switch

Step 9. CapCut auto-captions, then fix names and the $19 line. Wrong captions kill trust. 15 minutes a batch is enough.

Step 10. Post one a day. If you miss a day, post two the next. Do not "restart on Monday." The 30-day clock includes the ugly days.

Step 11. After 30 videos, keep the 5 that got saves and comments. Repeat those shapes. Kill the rest. Saves and comments beat views. Views without saves are tourists.

Step 12. Do not switch platforms when a post dies. Dead posts are normal. Switching is how you stay at video three forever.

A worked example

Jordan, one platform, 30 days, $19 SOP

Jordan has a one-page SOP: "Friday follow-up for solo service people" — a sequence they already use to email clients who went quiet. Price: $19. They pick Instagram. They do not open TikTok "as well." Bio: "Friday follow-up SOP. $19. I show the steps here." Link: Stan.

They generate 20 hooks. They speak three:

  • Stop rewriting the same 'just checking in' email every Friday if you want people to actually reply.
  • If your follow-up is three paragraphs, they are not ignoring you. They are tired.
  • I time this. One client, 90 seconds, send. The SOP is $19.

The 30-day shape

Week 1: seven videos, same offer. Three mistakes (the long email, the guilt tone, the missing next date), two principles (one ask, one next date), two walkthroughs (screen-record the SOP, fill it for a fake client).

Week 2: comments become scripts. Someone says "this only works if you have a list." Jordan films: "You need three names, not a CRM." Someone says "AI emails sound fake." Jordan films the human pass: "Generate, then cut the smile."

Week 3: repeat the two shapes that got saves — the 90-second timer and the "three paragraphs" diagnosis. Same words, new example.

Week 4: one longer walkthrough (60-90 seconds) of filling the SOP, still pointing at the same $19 checkout. No live required if they hate live; a longer screen-record counts.

What happened

Views were uneven. Two posts did nothing. One walkthrough got saves from other solo operators. A stranger bought on day 18 from the bio after watching the timer video and the three-paragraph video. Jordan asked what they watched. They said the timer. That became the next five posts. Jordan did not start TikTok on day 19. They did not explain their other two file ideas in a 20-second clip. They posted the SOP, $19, one platform.

Copy you can actually send

Hook (spoken, first second)

Stop starting from a blank chat if you need [result] this week.

Other hooks that fit the formula:

  • Stop doing [common thing] if you want [result].
  • If your [artifact] looks like this, that is why [bad result].
  • I timed this. [N] seconds. Then I sent it.

End card (spoken, last two seconds)

The file is $19. Link in bio. One job, not a catalog.

Caption

One sentence that repeats the hook. Then: SOP is $19, link in bio. No 15 hashtags. Three at most if the platform is picky, none if it is not.

Pin comment

SOP is $19. Link in bio. If you want the [job] sequence without guessing, that is the file.

DM when someone comments "link?"

Here: [link]. $19, one page, instant. If it is not useful, email me and I will refund. I will not hop on a call to recap the SOP.

Reply to an objection on camera

"I do not want to show my face."

Then screen-record the SOP. Your voice on the page is enough. Faceless slideshows with no offer are how people post for months and sell nothing. The offer still sits at the end.

"I need more followers first."

You need a profile visit that becomes a checkout. Fifty of the right watchers beat 5,000 tourists. Do not buy followers. Post the next one.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1: 7 videos, same offer

Evenings: one batch session (two hours, seven clips) on Sunday or Monday. Post one a day. Same end card. Do not check analytics for more than five minutes. You do not have enough posts to learn anything yet.

Week 2: 7 more, comments become scripts

Read every comment. Paste the objections into a note. Generate outlines, rewrite, film. If you have no comments yet, film the three objections you already believe: too simple, no time, I could do this myself. Answer them in 20 seconds.

Week 3: repeat winners

Look at saves and comments, not views. Repeat those shapes with new examples. Kill the clever ones nobody saved. Still one platform. Still $19.

Week 4: one longer piece, same checkout

One live or one longer walkthrough pointing at the same checkout. If live terrifies you, a 60-second screen-record of the SOP filled in is enough. Then keep posting the five shapes you are keeping. Plan the next 30 only after a stranger has bought or you have 30 honest posts, whichever comes first.

Quality bar

Voice

A voice you would use in a kitchen. If you would not say "smash that" to a friend, do not say it here. AI outlines; you say it. If a line makes you wince when you hear the playback, cut it.

Picture

On-screen text, always, for muted watchers. Face or screen, one style for 30 days. Lighting can be a window. Audio can be the phone mic if you are in a quiet room. Fix captions in CapCut so the $19 line is spelled right.

Offer

If the last two seconds do not name the file and the price, the video failed the bar even if it performed. Entertainment you are not getting paid for is the original warning. Put the offer on.

Metrics that matter (and vanity numbers to ignore)

Count these

  • Posts published (aim 7 a week, 30 in the window)
  • Saves and comments, especially objections you can reuse
  • Profile visits
  • Link taps
  • Emails captured or $19 checkouts from the bio
  • Which 5 shapes earned the saves

Ignore these

Follower count as a goal. View spikes with no taps. Likes from people who will never buy. Completions on a video with no offer. How you look. Whether a stranger on the internet said you should try a different platform.

The first-sale definition

A profile visit that becomes an email or a $19 checkout. That is the metric this path names. If you have 40,000 views and zero taps, the bio or the end card is wrong. Fix those before you film video 41.

Objections you will hear

"The algorithm hates me."

You have not posted 30 times. Post. Then look at saves, not at a theory about the algorithm. Switching platforms when a post dies is a listed mistake.

"I need to be on all five apps."

Five platforms is in the leave-alone line. One platform for 30 days. After a stranger buys, you can remix the five winning shapes onto a second app. Not before.

"Faceless is easier."

Faceless slideshows with no offer are how people hide. If you screen-record the work and speak, that is not a slideshow. If you post silent carousels of quotes, you are not on this path.

"I will buy views to jump-start."

Buying views is in the leave-alone line. You will get tourists and a fake sense of momentum. Organic posts that name a $19 file convert. Paid views of a no-offer clip do not.

"I do not have anything to sell yet."

Then this is not your first path. Ship a $19 SOP or playbook this week (Sell an AI playbook, AI templates and SOPs, Niche prompt stacks). Then post. Short-form without a file is a hobby with better lighting.

How this feeds other paths

What you are driving traffic to

Sell an AI playbook, Niche prompt stacks, Ship a Claude or ChatGPT skill pack, AI templates and SOPs — any one $19-$47 file. Short-form is the tap. The file is the drink. Your front door can sit in the bio later: a diagnostic, email required, then the file. Not in week 1 if a Stan checkout already works.

What the comments become

Objections become A weekly AI letter. Walkthroughs become Long-form explainers. The winning hook becomes Creator ads with AI scripts when you are ready to pay for traffic — after a stranger has bought organic. Live drops of AI packs can be announced in the same 20-second end card.

What you do not feed yet

A small AI membership, a catalog, a coaching call. Thirty days, one file. Local businesses + AI can use the same clips as proof you can make simple videos for a nearby owner, billed as a service. That is extra, not the 30-day job.

Mistakes that kill this path

The listed ones

Posting with no offer. Switching platforms when a post dies. Using a voice you would not use in a kitchen. Buying followers. Explaining your whole catalog in 20 seconds.

The quiet ones

Waiting for a better microphone. Batching zero videos so you negotiate with yourself every morning. Changing the offer in week 2. Reading the AI outline word for word. Hashtag stuffing instead of a pin comment. Checking views hourly. Starting over on Monday.

The 20-second catalog

If you name three products, they will buy none. One job. One price. One link. Jordan did not mention the other files. You should not either.

Tools and how you actually use them

Capture and edit

Phone. Front camera or screen record. CapCut for captions and a trim. No extra apps in week 1. A window is the light. A quiet room is the mic. If the first second is sharp enough to read on mute, you passed.

One platform for 30 days

TikTok, Instagram, or YouTube Shorts. Pick the one you already open at night. Post from the phone you filmed on. Do not build a desktop pipeline until you have 30 posts.

The link

Stan or Beacons in the bio. One button. The $19-$47 file. Test it on cellular, not only wifi. If the link needs a landing page with five choices, you built a menu. Cut it back to one.

Ship checklist

Before you post video 1

  • One file live at $19-$47
  • One platform chosen
  • Bio is one line plus the link
  • Link tested on your phone
  • 20 hooks generated; three you would actually say
  • Three videos filmed: mistake, principle, walkthrough
  • End card words decided and reused

Every posting day

  • On-screen text for mute
  • Offer spoken at the end
  • Pin comment with the $19 next step
  • Captions fixed in CapCut
  • Comments read and saved into a note

After 30

  • Keep 5 shapes (saves and comments)
  • Kill the rest
  • Do not switch platforms
  • Ask the first buyer which video they watched

FAQ

Which platform?

The one you already open. TikTok, Instagram, or YouTube Shorts. Not all three. If you do not open any of them, this path will feel like homework. Pick Instagram if you hate TikTok's tone. Pick Shorts if you already lurk on YouTube. Then stay.

Face or screen?

Pick one for 30 days. Face if you can talk. Screen if the product is the SOP or the prompt. Mixing both every other day is how you never get a recognizable shape.

How long is a video?

20 seconds is the default. 15 seconds of teaching, 2 seconds of offer. A week-4 walkthrough can be 60 seconds. Do not start at 3 minutes.

What if I miss a day?

Post two the next day. Do not restart the 30. The clock includes messy weeks. You have a job; batch on the weekend so weekdays are just tap-post.

Do I need CapCut Pro?

No. Captions and a trim. Phone tools are enough if CapCut annoys you. The original tools line is Phone, CapCut, one platform, Stan or Beacons. Stay there.

Can I use AI voice?

You can, and it usually sounds like a press release. This path wants you to say it. Screen-record plus your voice is the backup if you will not show a face.

What if nothing sells after 30 posts?

Check, in order: is the file live, is the price spoken, is the link working, is the bio one offer. Then look at the 5 shapes with saves. Repeat those. Ask two watchers what they thought you sold. If they cannot name the file, your end card failed.

Should I buy a microphone and lights?

Not in the first 30. A quiet room and a window. Spend after a stranger checkout if audio was the complaint.

Can I post the same video on two platforms?

Not as a way to skip the 30 on one. After 30, remix the five winners. During the 30, one room.

Is this the same as Creator ads with AI scripts?

No. This is organic posting to a $19 file. Creator ads is paid traffic later, with scripts, after you know which shape converts. Do not run ads to a video you posted twice.

PATH 05

A weekly AI letter

A list is the one asset a platform cannot take from you in an afternoon.

19 minute read · 4,212 words

Who this is for
You can write clearly once a week. You want an asset that compounds.
First sale
Fifty honest subscribers and one reply that is not spam.
Time to first
2–4 weeks to a real list
Cash window
Sells the playbook after trust
Tools
Beehiiv or MailerLite, a one-page diagnostic as the lead magnet, a form.
Leave this alone
Daily blasts. Buying a list. Essays nobody asked for.

What the letter is for

The letter is not a blog. It is a weekly appointment. One idea, one story, one ask. That is the whole shape. If you cannot say those three things about an issue before you write it, you do not have an issue yet.

A blog sits on a URL and waits. A letter arrives. The reader did not hunt for you that morning. You showed up in a place they already check: the inbox. That is why a list is the one asset a platform cannot take from you in an afternoon. A handle can vanish. An inbox you earned stays.

One idea, one story, one ask The idea is a single useful claim, small enough to finish in four hundred words. Not “how to use AI.” Something like “the follow-up I send twenty minutes after a call, and the two lines I never skip.” The story is one real moment that makes the idea true: a client who went quiet, a Tuesday you almost skipped, a prompt that failed until you added one constraint. The ask is one action. Reply with a score. Try the prompt. Forward this to the one person who keeps starting from a blank page. Not three actions. One.

AI drafts. You cut. You may let a model outline the issue or tighten a muddy paragraph. You may not let it publish. Cut until it sounds like you on a Tuesday. If you would not say the sentence to a person across a table, it does not go in the letter. The reader can smell a paste. They came for a person who does the job, not for a summary of a summary.

When you sell You sell inside the letter after they have heard from you enough to trust the voice. Email one is the diagnostic and the welcome. Emails 2–6 teach. Email 7 can ask. If you pitch in email one like a late-night ad, they leave. Trust is not a mood. Trust is six useful Tuesdays in a row.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you can write clearly once a week and you want an asset that compounds. You do not need a following. You need one job you can talk about without inventing a persona, and the discipline to show up on the same day.

You are a fit if You already explain things in messages, comments, or client notes, and people write back. You can keep a promise that is boring: same day, same length, same person.

You are also a fit if you already have (or will soon have) a $47 playbook, a template, or a named package. The letter is how those things get sold without shouting.

Skip this path if You cannot keep a weekly date for four weeks. The letter dies from missed Tuesdays, not from a weak logo. Skip it if you want daily growth hacks, if you plan to buy a list, or if you only write when you have a launch. Skip it if you have never finished a result for anyone, including yourself. Teach after you have a scar. Not before.

If you need money this week, run a productized package first, then start the letter with the notes from that job.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this before you pick a logo: “Every Tuesday I send one useful note to [specific person] about [named job], plus a free one-page diagnostic. After you have a few issues, you can buy the $47 playbook if you want the full sequence. If not, you still get the letter.”

If you cannot fill the brackets, you do not have a letter. You have a mood.

The free thing is the door The lead magnet is a one-page scorecard or a 7-day checklist. It has to be useful even if they never buy. That is the deal you keep. A scorecard that tells them which step to skip this week is a door. A “free” PDF that is only a sales letter is a trap, and they will treat you like one.

Put the form on the same page as your paid offer, but second. Free first is how strangers become a list. The paid button can wait.

What you are not offering You are not offering a community, a course, a daily brief, or “all of AI.” You are offering a weekly appointment and one paid file when trust exists. Keep the sentence short enough to paste into a bio.

What ‘done’ looks like

Done for the first month is not a launch. Done is fifty honest subscribers and one reply that is not spam. Honest means they gave you an email because they wanted the next Tuesday, not because a pop-up lied.

The weekly bar - Same day every week. If you promised Tuesday, it leaves Tuesday. - Same length. Four hundred to seven hundred words. Long enough to teach one thing. Short enough to finish on a phone. - One ask at the end. Count them. If you have three, delete two. - A subject line a human would open. Not “Newsletter #4.”

The money bar Email 7 (or the quieter monthly issue) points to the $47 playbook without a countdown clock or a fake scarcity line. A sale from a subscriber who has read you is done. A sale from a stranger who never joined the list is a different path. Do not mix them in your head.

If you have the fifty names, the weekly habit, and one real reply, you have a letter. Everything else is decoration.

How the money works

The letter is free. That is not a slogan. It is the business model. You do not charge for the appointment. You charge for the file after the appointment has earned the right.

What you sell, and when The first paid offer inside the letter is the playbook: $47, theirs to keep. Not a subscription in month one. Not a $297 cohort. You have no proof yet, and they have six issues of trust, not a year.

Emails 2–6 should not sell. They should make the playbook obvious without naming a cart. When you finally ask, the sentence is calm: the full sequence is $47, and next Tuesday still arrives either way.

What you never do for money You never sell the list. You never rent it. You never buy someone else’s. Deliverability is the business. One rented blast can train the inbox to treat you as junk, and you will not know until Tuesday stops landing.

A sponsorship can wait until you would run the ad even if they paid you nothing. If you would not, skip it.

The first 72 hours

You do not need a brand kit. You need issue one, a form, and a free file that works.

Hours 1–8: write the first issue by hand Pick the reader. Pick the one job. Write issue one without a model in the room. A story you actually lived, one idea, one ask. Then, if you want, run it through AI to tighten — not to inflate. Cut every sentence that sounds like a keynote.

Subject line for issue one: something they can picture. “The 20-minute follow-up I actually send” beats “Welcome to the letter.”

Hours 9–16: the free file and the form Build a one-page scorecard or a 7-day checklist. Seven questions that change the advice, or seven days that a tired person can actually do. Export a clean PDF. Put your name and a reply-to on it.

Open Beehiiv or MailerLite. Create one form. One field besides email is plenty: first name, or the job they do. Not a twelve-field survey. Connect the welcome that delivers the file at once. If the file arrives tomorrow, you already taught them you are slow.

Hours 17–24: ten humans, not a launch Send issue one to ten people you know. Ask them to reply with the line that was useful. That reply is your subject-line research. Do not ask “did you like it?” Ask for the line. Put the form on the same page as your offer, but second.

By hour 72 you should have a live form, a working welcome, issue one in ten inboxes, and one sentence from a real person you can steal for the next subject line.

The path, step by step

Twelve steps. Do them in order. Skipping ahead to “write a better brand” is how letters stay imaginary.

Step 1. Promise one specific weekly note Name the day. Name the length. Name the person it is for. Keep it. Same day, same length. If you cannot keep Tuesday, do not print Tuesday on the form.

Step 2. Name the one reader Write one sentence about one person. Not “entrepreneurs.” A solo bookkeeper who hates the blank follow-up. A tradesperson who wants five posts without sitting on a phone. If two readers appear, you have two letters. Pick one.

Step 3. Build the diagnostic One page. Scorecard or 7-day checklist. Useful if they never buy. If you cannot use it yourself this week, it is not ready.

Step 4. Stand up the form Beehiiv or MailerLite. One form, one welcome, one tag if you must. Test from a second email. Confirm the file arrives and the unsubscribe works.

Step 5. Write issue one by hand Then optionally tighten with a model. Publish only the version that sounds like you.

Step 6. Subject lines that are specific “The 20-minute follow-up I actually send,” not “Newsletter #4.” Steal the useful line from those first ten replies. Specific beats clever. Clever dies in a promotions tab.

Step 7. End with one ask Reply, click, or try the prompt. Not three. The reply is the gold. A click is fine. A prompt they can run in five minutes is better than a survey.

Step 8. Teach for five issues Emails 2–6 are the job, in pieces. One piece per Tuesday. No cart. No “by the way I sell a thing” in paragraph two. You are earning the seventh email.

Step 9. The quieter ask Once a month, a quieter issue that points to the paid file. Email 7 can ask. Calm, priced, easy to ignore.

Step 10. Never buy a list. Never scrape Your deliverability is the business. Fifty names who open beat five thousand who never did.

Step 11. Turn replies into issues When someone writes “I get stuck after the call,” that is next Tuesday. The letter gets easier when you stop inventing topics and start answering mail.

Step 12. Keep the date for twelve weeks before you add anything No daily version. No second letter. Twelve Tuesdays. Then look at what they replied to, and only then consider a second product.

Worked example: Elena’s Tuesday letter

Elena used to coordinate jobs at a small studio. She was good at the twenty minutes after a client call: recap, next step, date. When she went solo, the blank follow-up came back. She wrote the sequence down so she would stop rewriting it. That file sat on her desktop for two months. Then she started a Tuesday letter so the file would have somewhere to live.

The promise Every Tuesday, one note for people who sell work from a kitchen table and freeze after the call. Four hundred to six hundred words. No daily blast, no “week in AI.” She named it after the day.

The door The free file is a one-page scorecard: seven questions about how they follow up, a score, and one next step. “Reply with your score and I will tell you which path to skip.” That line is in the welcome. She put the form above the $47 button on her page. Free first.

The first four Tuesdays Issue one: the 20-minute follow-up she actually sends, with the two lines she never skips. She wrote it by hand, then asked a model to cut forty words, then put twenty back because the cuts had removed her. She sent it to ten people she had worked with. Four replied. Three quoted the same sentence about not asking “how are you” in sentence one if you already know. That sentence became the subject line of issue two.

Issues two through six taught the rest of the job: the recap template, the prompt that turns messy notes into a recap without sounding like a robot, what to do when they go quiet, how to close a loop on Friday. No pitch.

The ask Issue seven was quieter. She told the story of the desktop file. Then: “If you want the full sequence, it is $47 and yours to keep. If not, see you next Tuesday.” Seven people bought in ten days. She had eighty-one subscribers. Not a viral moment. A letter.

Elena did not buy ads. She did not scrape. She asked those first ten, then asked each new subscriber to forward the diagnostic to one person who hates the blank follow-up.

Copy you can paste

Use these as drafts. Swap the job. Keep the calm.

Form headline Seven questions. A score. One next step. Free even if you never buy. The Tuesday letter comes after.

Welcome (email one) Here is the one-page diagnostic. Reply with your score and I will tell you which path to skip. I write once a week, on Tuesday, about [job]. One idea, one story, one ask. If that is not useful, unsubscribe. I will not be sad, and you will not hear from me again.

Issue close (teaching weeks) If you try this, reply with the line that worked — or the line that died. I read them. See you next Tuesday.

Issue close (the ask) If you want the full sequence, it is $47 and yours to keep. If not, see you next Tuesday.

Personal invite (send to ten people) I started a Tuesday letter for [person] who [job]. Issue one is the [specific thing]. If you want it: [form]. If you have two minutes, reply with the line that was useful. That is the whole ask.

Subject lines that behave The 20-minute follow-up I actually send Stop asking “just checking in” The recap I send before I open the next tab A 7-question score, not another pep talk What I cut from last week’s prompt

Bio line One useful Tuesday note about [job]. Free diagnostic at the link. No daily blasts.

Week-by-week

Assume you have a job and evenings. This is four weeks to a real list, not a media company.

Week 1: form plus issue one Day 1–2: write the diagnostic and issue one by hand. Day 3: Beehiiv or MailerLite, form, welcome, test-buy of the free file from a second email. Day 4: send issue one to ten people you know. Day 5–7: collect the useful lines. Do not redesign the header.

Week 2: issue two plus ten personal invites Send issue two on the promised day. Invite ten more humans with the personal copy, not a broadcast. If someone forwards you a kind reply, ask if you can use the line (no last name required).

Week 3: teach one path Issue three is one chapter of the job. No pitch. If you have the $47 playbook drafted, do not mention the price yet. Mention the problem the playbook solves, in human words.

Week 4: a quiet ask for the paid file If you have sent four useful issues and you have a file that works, you may ask. If the file is not ready, keep teaching. A bad ask costs more than a late one. After week 4, the cadence is the job: same day, one idea, one story, one ask. Once a month, the quieter issue with the $47 link.

Do not add a Friday bonus letter in month two because you felt guilty about a slow week. Guilt is not a publishing calendar.

The quality bar

A good issue can be read in four minutes and used in twenty. If they cannot use it this week, it was an essay.

The kitchen-table test Read the issue out loud. If you would not say it to one person at a table, cut it. Hype, throat-clearing, and “as you know” go first. Named tools and named steps stay.

The Tuesday test Would this still be true next Tuesday if a model version changed? Teach the job, not the button labels of one chat window. You can name a tool. Do not marry a menu.

The AI test If you paste the issue back into a blank chat and it could have written the whole thing, you have not written it. Add the scar: what failed, what you still do, the sentence you almost sent. That is the part they cannot download for free.

One typo will not kill you. A voice that sounds like a press release will.

What to count (and what to ignore)

Count a short list. Ignore the rest for the first two months.

Count these - Honest subscribers. Fifty is the first milestone. Not a purchased fifty. - Replies that are not spam. One real reply is worth more than a 60 percent open rate with silence. - Issues sent on the promised day. A 12-for-12 streak beats a viral issue you cannot repeat. - Unsubscribes per issue. A few means people are reading. Zero can mean you are too bland to bother leaving. - Playbook sales from the list after the ask. Note the issue number. That is your sales page, whether you like the design or not.

Ignore these for now Follower count on the platform you used to announce the letter. Daily growth charts. Open-rate panic below a number you saw in a thread. Different tools count opens differently, and image-blocking will lie to you.

If fifty people are on the list, ten open, and two reply, you have a letter.

Objections you will hear (including your own)

“I do not have an audience.” You need ten people who will tell you the useful line. Audience is what the letter builds. It is not a prerequisite. Write issue one, send it to ten names in your phone, stop waiting for permission from a feed.

“Email is dead.” It is not. It is quiet. Quiet is why it still works. The people who say it is dead are tired of blasts. Do not send blasts. Send a Tuesday.

“I should wait until I have the playbook perfect.” The letter can start with the diagnostic. The $47 file can land in week 4. Waiting for perfect is how you miss twelve Tuesdays.

“People will copy me.” They might copy the structure. They will not copy the scar. Teach anyway.

“I write too slow.” Good. Slow is a filter. Four hundred honest words beat twelve hundred padded ones. Set a two-hour cap. When the cap hits, cut, do not expand.

“What if nobody replies?” Ask a better ask. “Was this good?” dies. “Reply with the line you would actually send” lives. Also: write to one person. Letters to a crowd get silence. Letters to a name get mail.

What this path feeds

The letter is not a side hobby. It is the pipe.

What it fills It sells the playbook after trust — that is the cash window on purpose. It also sells templates when you mention the filled example you used this week. It throws off service work when someone replies “can you just do this for me?” That reply is a package, not a free consulting hour. Put the price in the reply or send the cheaper file.

Affiliate mentions belong below the reasoning, never above it, and only for tools you still use. The letter can carry that link because the trust already exists. Do not turn Tuesday into a tool dump.

What it needs It needs a door: a diagnostic and a form. It needs one paid file worth asking for. It does not need a membership, a chat room, or a second newsletter. Those come after the Tuesday habit is boring, which is the goal.

Mistakes that kill the letter

Writing for a crowd Writing for search engines instead of one person. The inbox is not Google. A keyword in the subject that no human would say is how you train them to skim.

Letting AI publish unedited. The first draft from a model is a pile of true-sounding sentences. Your job is to make it specific, short, and slightly uncomfortable, the way a real Tuesday is.

Breaking the Tuesday promise Changing the promise every week. Tuesday is follow-up, then Friday is branding, then you skip a week for a “personal update.” That is not a letter. That is a mood board with a send button.

Sending only when you have a launch. Then you are not a letter. You are a store that opens four times a year and wonders why the door sticks.

Daily blasts. Buying a list. Essays nobody asked for. Those three are how small senders become noise. Do not. A 40-field form, a welcome that is only a pitch, and hiding the unsubscribe all show up later as a deliverability problem you will blame on the tool.

Tools (keep this small)

Beehiiv or MailerLite. Pick one. Do not split the list. A one-page diagnostic as the lead magnet. A form. That is the stack.

Why these and not a maze You need a place that sends, tracks unsubscribes, and delivers a file. You do not need a customer-data platform, a popup that follows people around, or five landing-page builders. One page with the form second and the paid button third is enough.

How to use AI here Draft and tighten. Not send. Not invent subscriber stories. Not write fake testimonials. If you use a model to outline issue two, you still rewrite every paragraph in the voice you would use at a table.

What to leave on the shelf Daily sending tools. Scrapers. Anything that promises you a list by Friday. The list you did not earn will not open.

Checklist

Print this. Tick it.

Before you send issue one - One reader, written in one sentence - One promised day and a length you can keep - Diagnostic: one-page scorecard or 7-day checklist, useful if they never buy - Beehiiv or MailerLite account, one form, welcome that delivers the file at once - Test from a second email: file arrives, unsubscribe works - Issue one written by hand, then tightened, then read out loud - Subject line a human would open - One ask at the end - Form on the same page as the offer, but second

The first month - Ten people you know received issue one - You collected the useful lines - Issues 2–6 teach; no cart in paragraph one - Once, a quieter issue that can point to the $47 playbook - No list bought, no scrape, no daily extra

You may ask for money when - The free file works without you in the room - You have kept the day - You can say the price in one calm sentence

FAQ

How long should each issue be? Four hundred to seven hundred words. If you need two thousand, you have two issues. Split them. Tired people finish short letters.

Beehiiv or MailerLite — which one? Either. Pick the one you will actually log into. Switching later is a tax. Features you will not use are not a reason.

Can I send on Sunday if I promised Tuesday? No. Send Tuesday, even if it is shorter. If you must move the day, say so once, then keep the new day. Random is how they stop opening.

What if I have nothing to sell yet? Run the letter on the diagnostic for a month. Teach. Build the $47 playbook from the replies. The list is still the asset. The cart can wait four weeks. It should not wait four years.

Should I add a podcast / YouTube / daily notes? Not until twelve Tuesdays have left on time. Extra channels are how letters starve.

Is fifty subscribers too small to ask for $47? No. Elena asked at eighty-one and sold seven. Fifty honest names who have read you will outbuy five thousand quiet ones. Ask calmly. Do not apologize for the price.

What do I do with a mean reply? Unsubscribe them if they want out. If it is a real critique of the work, steal the useful part and ignore the heat. Do not write a Tuesday about your feelings unless that is the job you promised.

Can I use their reply in the next issue? Ask. “Can I use this line, no name?” Most people say yes. A few will want a first name. Do not invent a testimonial.

When do I buy ads to the form? After the welcome works, the diagnostic is useful, and you have at least four issues in the can.

What if I miss a week because life happened? Send a short note: “I missed Tuesday. Here is a short one. Back next week.” Then be back. Two misses in a row is a new habit.

PATH 06

AI templates and SOPs

A filled example sells. An empty grid is homework.

18 minute read · 4,006 words

Who this is for
You already run a repeatable process: intake, content calendar, invoice, weekly review.
First sale
A $19 template that saves someone an afternoon.
Time to first
3–10 days
Cash window
$12–$29, or $47 for a bundle of three
Tools
Notion, Google Sheets, Canva, the prompts that generate the filled version.
Leave this alone
Empty aesthetic planners. Fifty messy files.

What you are selling

The file is the product. The prompt that fills it is the leverage. Together they let a buyer go from zero to a working system in ten minutes. That is the promise. If it takes an hour of setup, you built software, not a template.

A template in this path is not a pretty empty grid. It is a working example of a job you already do — intake, content calendar, invoice, weekly review — plus the block at the top that rebuilds a filled version from a few inputs. The buyer should be able to duplicate, paste, and move. They should not need a course to sit down.

Why the filled example is the product Show a filled example. A blank Notion dashboard photographs well and converts poorly. People buy the picture of work already done. They do not buy the homework of naming columns. If your listing image is a lavender empty table with cute icons, you are competing with every aesthetic planner on earth, and you will lose on taste.

What this is not It is not an “OS.” It is not a second brain. It is not fifty nested pages. Those words hide a checklist. Call it what it does on a Tuesday: the weekly content calendar, the intake that does not lose notes, the invoice tracker you actually open. If you cannot say the Tuesday problem in one line, you do not have a product yet. You have a file dump.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you already run a repeatable process. You do not need to be a Notion consultant. You need a job you have done more than three times, in a tool you can duplicate without breaking.

You are a fit if You have a calendar, a sheet, or a form you reuse. Other people have asked “can I have that?” You can screenshot a filled week without leaking a client. You are willing to write one prompt that takes a few inputs and returns the filled table, not a poem about productivity.

Skip this path if You only have a blank aesthetic you liked on Pinterest. Empty planners are a crowded street, and you will spend a month on icons. Skip it if your process only lives in your head and has never survived a second week. Skip it if you want to ship fifty messy files and call it a pack. One clean file beats a zip of leftovers.

If you have never kept a calendar for yourself, do not sell one. Use it for two weeks. Then sell the version that has scars: the column you deleted, the status you actually use, the prompt that stopped making twelve posts you would never publish.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this on a sticky note: “A filled [job] template plus the prompt that rebuilds it in ten minutes, for [person] who [Tuesday problem]. $19. Duplicate, paste, go.”

Examples that work: a filled weekly content calendar for a one-person shop that never knows what to post. An intake database for a freelancer who loses notes in chat. An invoice tracker for a tradesperson who forgets to send the second bill. The job is in the title. The prompt is in the file. The price is on the button.

The bundle, later A bundle of three related files at $29 outsells one lonely file at $12 if they clearly belong together. Calendar plus intake plus weekly review is a week. Calendar plus a mood board plus a habit tracker is a drawer. Related means they share a Tuesday. Unrelated means you are hoping they like your taste.

A $47 bundle of three is allowed when each file is used, filled, and documented. Do not jump to $47 because three sounds premium. Jump when a buyer would spend an afternoon without them.

The ten-minute promise If a reasonably tired person cannot get a usable filled week in ten minutes with your how-to open, you overbuilt. Delete tabs until the promise is true.

What ‘done’ looks like

Done is a $19 template that saves someone an afternoon. Not a library. Not a mobile app. A file they can duplicate today.

The file bar - One Tuesday problem on the cover and in the title - A filled example, not an empty grid, in the first screenshot - The AI prompt as a block at the top: inputs in, filled table out - Personal data stripped. Fake names are fine. Real client emails are not - A one-page how-to with screenshots. No video required for v1 - Works on a phone well enough to read, even if they edit on a laptop

The money bar Checkout works. They get the file. A stranger who is not you can follow the how-to without sending you a voice note. That is done. A three-file bundle at $29 is a second done, after the first file has been used by someone else.

How to price without guessing

First file: $19. That is the default. $12 if the job is tiny (one sheet, ten rows). $29 if the file is clearly a week of work and the prompt is tested. Do not start at $79. You have no reviews and a duplicate button.

Why $19 is the honest first price It is small enough that a tired person will try it to save an afternoon. It is large enough that you will not feel used when they email a question. If you are ashamed of $19, you either overbuilt or you do not believe the afternoon is real. Cut the file, or use it yourself for another week.

The bundle math One lonely file at $12 looks cheap and unfinished. Three related files at $29 look like a week. Chris does this on purpose (see the example): the calendar at $19, then the three-file bundle at $29 so the calendar buyer has a next step that is not “buy a course.” A $47 bundle of three is the later price, when you have proof and a how-to that has been yelled at.

What you do not price You do not charge extra for “lifetime updates” you will not ship. You do not add a $9 monthly for a template. If they need you every month, that is a service or a membership, not this path.

The first 72 hours

You are not inventing a system from a blank page. You are cleaning the one you already use.

Day 1: duplicate and strip Open the template you already live in. Duplicate it. Strip personal data. Rename the sample person to something obviously fake. Fill one real-looking week so the screenshot has texture: statuses, dates, a note that sounds like work, not “lorem.” Delete every tab you have not opened in fourteen days. Forty tabs is how buyers bounce.

Day 2: the prompt and the how-to Write the prompt that regenerates a filled sample from a few inputs: business, audience, offer, days they can post, voice in one line. Put that prompt in a block at the top of the file. Run it. Paste the output into the filled example. Fix the prompt until the table is usable, not until it is clever.

Write one page of how-to. Screenshots of: duplicate, paste inputs, run prompt, paste results, what “done” looks like. No video required for v1. Export. Screenshot the filled version, not the empty grid.

Day 3: listing and $19 Price $19. Title: “Filled [job] template + the prompt that rebuilds it in ten minutes.” Put it on your site. Etsy if the search terms are obvious (content calendar, invoice tracker, client intake). Buy it yourself from a second email. If the file does not arrive, nothing else matters. Then send ten messages to people who have the Tuesday problem.

The path, step by step

Twelve steps. The file comes from a job you already do. Do not reverse that.

Step 1. Name the Tuesday problem “I never know what to post.” “I lose intake notes.” “I forget to invoice.” One sentence. If you need a paragraph, you have two products. Split them.

Step 2. Start from the file you already use Duplicate. Do not rebuild in a new tool because a thread said it was nicer. The scars are in the current file.

Step 3. Build only the columns or pages that solve that Delete the rest. A content calendar needs: date, channel, hook, body, asset, status, link. It does not need a vision board, a reading list, or a sleep tracker.

Step 4. Fill one honest week Use fake names. Keep the texture of real work. This filled week is your listing image and your quality bar.

Step 5. Put the AI prompt at the top Inputs in, filled table out. Include constraints: length, tone, “do not invent offers I did not type.” Run it twice. If the second run is unusable, the prompt is the product to fix, not the colors.

Step 6. One-page how-to with screenshots Duplicate, paste, run, paste, done. Mobile note: which parts are painful on a phone. No video required for v1.

Step 7. Strip, export, name the file like a human “Weekly-content-calendar-filled.html” or a Notion share the buyer owns after duplicate. Not “FINAL_v7_USE_THIS.”

Step 8. Listing on your site. Etsy if the search terms are obvious Title matches the offer sentence. First image is the filled example. Price $19.

Step 9. Test-buy. Then ten asks Different browser. Confirm they get access. Message ten people who have the Tuesday problem. Not a blast to a group you lurk in.

Step 10. Fix what the first buyer trips on Usually: permissions, a locked database, a prompt that assumes a tool they do not have. Write that into the how-to. Do not add a tab.

Step 11. Week 2: a three-file bundle Only if the files share a Tuesday. $29. The first file can stay at $19 for people who only need the calendar.

Step 12. Do not design forty tabs When you want to add a dashboard, go use the file for a week instead. New tabs are how templates become homework again.

Worked example: Chris and the filled calendar

Chris runs a one-person design practice. Every Sunday he used to stare at a blank week and then post nothing until Wednesday. He built a Notion calendar so he would stop staring. Six columns. One view for the week. A status he actually updates. After two months it was ugly and used, which is the only aesthetic that sells.

The $19 file He duplicated the calendar. He renamed his clients to “River Bakery” and “Lane Tutoring.” He filled one week: five posts, two stories, one email, statuses in plain words (draft, need photo, scheduled). At the top he wrote a prompt:

Paste: what you sell, who it is for, the one offer this month, days you can post, how you sound in one sentence. Return: a seven-day table with date, channel, hook, body under 80 words, asset needed, status set to draft. Do not invent a second offer. Do not sound like a billboard.

He ran it on his own inputs. He edited two hooks that were cute and useless. He put that filled week in the first screenshot. Title: “Filled weekly content-calendar (Notion) + the prompt that rebuilds it in ten minutes.” $19. How-to: one page, five screenshots. No video.

The first sales He sent it to eight people who had complained about posting. Three bought in a week. One asked where to put intake notes. That question is how the bundle starts. It is not a reason to add a tab to the calendar.

The $29 three-file bundle Week 2 he cleaned two other files he already used: a client intake (so notes stop dying in chat) and a Friday weekly review (what shipped, what slipped, what to put in next week’s prompt). Same treatment: filled example, prompt at the top, one-page how-to. Bundle of three at $29. The calendar stayed available alone at $19. People who only wanted posts could stay small. People who wanted the week bought the bundle.

Chris did not call it an OS. He did not add a CRM. He did not film a twenty-minute walkthrough for v1. He sold the picture of a week already done, and the prompt that rebuilds it.

Copy you can paste

Swap the job. Keep the filled promise.

Listing title Filled [job] template + the prompt that rebuilds it in ten minutes.

Listing first line You get a filled week, not an empty grid. Duplicate the Notion (or sheet). Paste a few inputs. Run the prompt. Paste the table. About ten minutes. $19.

Post under a screenshot of the filled example This is last week’s calendar, names changed. Empty templates are homework. This one is already working. $19, link in bio. The prompt that rebuilds it sits at the top of the file.

DM I use this every Sunday so I stop staring at a blank week. Filled [job] template plus the prompt that rebuilds it. $19. If that saves you an afternoon: [link]. If not, no need to reply.

Bundle line Three files that share a Tuesday: weekly calendar, intake, Friday review. $29. Each one has a filled example and the prompt that rebuilds it.

What not to write “Aesthetic Notion dashboard for CEOs.” “Ultimate content OS.” “200+ templates.” Those lines sell a vibe to people who will not open the file. You are selling an afternoon back.

Week-by-week

You can do the first sale inside ten days if the file already exists. Do not use week one to learn a new tool.

Days 1–2: clean the file you already use Duplicate. Strip. Fill one week. Write the prompt. Run it. Fix it. Draft the one-page how-to. Screenshot the filled version.

Day 3: listing $title, $19, filled screenshot first, test-buy, ten asks. Etsy only if someone would search the job by name.

Days 4–7: silence is data If nobody buys, ask two of the ten what was unclear. Usually the title is vague or the screenshot is empty. Fix those. Do not add a tab.

Week 2: a three-file bundle Only related files. $29. Keep the single at $19. Do not design forty tabs. Do not start a fourth file because bundling felt good.

Weeks 3–4 Use your own file in public: one post that shows this week’s filled row (blur what you must). Answer the two questions buyers actually ask. Update the how-to. That is the whole month.

The quality bar

A stranger should go from purchase to a filled week in ten minutes without you. That is the bar. Pretty is optional. Clear is not.

The filled-week test Hand the how-to to someone who has not seen the file. Time them. If they need you at minute four, the how-to is missing a screenshot. If they need you at minute twenty because of a nested relation, you built software. Flatten it.

The mobile test Open the file on a phone. Can they read the week? Can they see the prompt? Editing on a phone can be ugly. Reading should not be. Forgetting mobile is how you lose the people who buy at 10 p.m. on a couch.

The prompt test Run the prompt on two different businesses. If both outputs look like the same billboard, add constraints: offer they typed, words they use, length. The prompt is part of the product. A cute template with a lazy prompt is an empty grid with extra steps.

Naming Call it a checklist or a calendar if that is what it is. Calling it an OS when it is a checklist trains buyers to expect a product you did not make, then to leave a review that says so.

What to count

Count a short list for the first twenty sales.

Count these - Sales of the $19 file. First sale from someone who is not you is the milestone. - Refunds and “I can’t open this” notes. Those are how-to bugs, not personality flaws. - Time-to-filled-week from the one person you watch. Aim under ten minutes. - Bundle attach: how many $19 buyers take the $29 three-file set. If none do, the extra files are not related, or you never said they share a Tuesday. - Which screenshot they linger on, if your host shows it. It should be the filled example.

Ignore these for now Likes on a template aesthetic post. Number of tabs as a flex. A competitor with 4.8 stars and two hundred files. You are not in a volume contest. You are in an afternoon-saving contest.

If you have three sales and zero open tickets, you are fine. If you have thirty views and zero sales, the screenshot is empty or the title hides the job. Fix the listing before you rebuild the file.

Objections you will hear

“Notion templates are everywhere and cheap.” Empty ones are. Filled ones with a prompt that rebuilds the week are not everywhere. Compete on the Tuesday problem and the example, not on pastel.

“People will just copy the structure.” They will. The prompt, the filled week, and the how-to still save an afternoon. You are selling time, not a secret column name.

“I should record a course first.” No. One-page how-to. Screenshots. No video required for v1. A course is a different path and a longer delay. If they cannot sit down without a forty-minute video, the file is too clever.

“$19 feels too low.” Good. Sell ten. Then offer the $29 bundle. Then, with proof, a $47 set of three that is actually used. Pride is not a price.

“What if they want it in a different tool?” Pick Notion or Google Sheets or Canva — the one you already use. A second version is a second product. Mention the tool in the title so they do not buy the wrong one.

“I do not have three files.” Then sell one at $19. The bundle is week 2, not a requirement for week 1. A single honest file beats a padded zip.

What this path feeds

A template is a thin product that throws off thicker ones.

What it fills The letter can show this week’s filled row and link the $19 file. The playbook can include the template as the working file at the back. People who write “can you just fill this for me?” are asking for a productized package, not a free hour. Send the price or send the file. Do not hop on a call that becomes the job.

What it needs It needs a Tuesday problem you have already solved in a tool you control. It does not need a membership to “get all templates.” Fifty messy files is the thing to leave alone. One file that works, then a bundle of three that belong together.

Mistakes that stall this path

Aesthetic, tabs, and homework Competing only on aesthetic. Taste is not a Tuesday problem. A filled ugly calendar will outsell an empty beautiful one.

Shipping 40 tabs. Each tab is a place to get lost. Delete until the how-to fits on one page.

Forgetting mobile. If the week cannot be read on a phone, a lot of your buyers will bounce after they pay, which is the worst bounce.

No example. An empty grid is homework. People do not buy homework when they are already tired.

Naming and the zip of leftovers Calling it an “OS” when it is a checklist. You will attract buyers who want a company, and they will tell you your checklist is incomplete. They are right about the naming.

Also: fifty messy files in a zip. A prompt that generates fluff. Listing photos of mockups with no real rows. Hiding the tool name so both Notion people and sheet people are annoyed. Locking the database so duplicate does nothing. Those are how $19 turns into a refund.

Tools (the ones you already use)

Notion, Google Sheets, Canva, and the prompts that generate the filled version. Pick the tool the job already lives in.

Notion Good for calendars, intakes, and reviews with a few views. Bad when you nest five relations and call it simple. Duplicate must work. Permissions must not block the buyer.

Google Sheets Good for invoices, trackers, and anything that wants a sum. Put the prompt in row 1 as a note, or on a tab named HOW-TO so it cannot be missed. Filled example on the first tab.

Canva Good when the deliverable is a thing they will post or print: a carousel, a one-pager, a simple invoice. Still show a filled example. An empty brand kit is another aesthetic trap.

The prompt Lives in the file. Inputs in, filled table out. Tested on two businesses. This is not a separate course. It is a block at the top.

Leave empty aesthetic planners and fifty messy files on the shelf. You already know they do not save an afternoon.

Checklist

Tick this before you raise the price.

File - Tuesday problem in one line - Duplicated from a file you actually use - Personal data stripped - One filled week with fake names and real texture - Extra tabs deleted - Prompt at the top: inputs, output format, constraints - Prompt run twice on different inputs - One-page how-to with screenshots - Readable on a phone

Listing - Title: Filled [job] template + the prompt that rebuilds it in ten minutes - First image is the filled example, not the empty grid - Tool named (Notion, Sheets, or Canva) - Price $19 (or $12 if truly tiny) - Test-buy from a second email - Ten personal asks

Week 2 (optional) - Two more related files, same treatment - Bundle $29 (or $47 when each file is proven) - Single file still for sale

FAQ

Do I need to be a Notion expert? No. You need a file you already use. If you cannot duplicate it without breaking, simplify until you can. Expertise is not the product. The afternoon saved is.

Can I sell on Etsy only? Sell on your site first so you own the buyer. Etsy if the search terms are obvious: content calendar, invoice tracker, client intake. Title like a search, not like a brand poem.

Should I include a video? Not for v1. One-page how-to. Screenshots. If ten buyers ask the same question, add one short clip of that step. Do not film a course to avoid writing a page.

What if my process lives in three tools? Sell the one that hurts on Tuesday. Mention the others in the how-to as optional. A three-tool setup is how ten minutes becomes an hour.

Is $29 too cheap for three files? Not if they share a Tuesday and each has a filled example. Chris’s bundle is $29 on purpose. $47 is later, with proof. Cheap is fifty messy files. Related and filled is the value.

Can I use AI to build the template itself? You can draft columns. You still have to live in the file for a week. A model will give you twenty tabs. You need six.

What license do they get? Personal use, duplicate for their work, no resale of the template as a template. Say that in one paragraph on the how-to. You are not launching a legal department.

Someone wants custom columns. That is a service. Quote a package or say no. Do not turn $19 into an unpaid rebuild.

The buyer says the prompt is generic. It is. Fix it with their inputs as required fields and a constraint list. Update the file. Send them the new prompt. That update is worth more than a new tab.

When do I make a second product? After the first file has a stranger sale and a how-to that no longer gets the same question. Then a related file, then a $29 bundle. Not a 50-file vault.

PATH 07

Productized AI service

Do not sell hours. Sell a named package with a date on it.

17 minute read · 3,672 words

Who this is for
You can get a result for someone else in a known number of days, using AI for draft and you for quality.
First sale
One paid package with a written scope.
Time to first
3–14 days with outreach
Cash window
$300–$1,500 first packages
Tools
Calendly, Stripe invoice or deposit link, a one-page agreement.
Leave this alone
A menu of 12 services. Unlimited revisions. Custom everything.

ON THIS PAGE
