Why this prints cash faster than a file

A service is slower to scale and faster to cash. If you need money this month, this path beats a playbook. You will still take notes. Those notes become the file you sell later.

Do not sell hours. Sell a named package with a date on it. Hours train people to haggle your Tuesday. A package trains them to say yes or no.

What a package is The package has a name, a deliverable, a timeline, a price, and a definition of done. “I’ll help with your AI” is not a package. “12 posts and one landing draft in 7 days for $600” is. You can hold the second one in your hand. You can put it on a calendar. You can take a deposit against it.

Where AI sits AI drafts. You edit. They never see the raw model output. Quality is your name, not the model’s. If they wanted the raw window, they would already have it. They are paying for a result that does not sound like a press release, delivered by a date.

What this is not It is not a retainer with vague “AI support.” It is not a menu of twelve services. It is not unlimited revisions. It is not custom everything. Those are how a $600 job becomes a $600 relationship that eats your month.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you can get a result for someone else in a known number of days, using AI for draft and you for quality. You need one job you have finished more than twice. You need the spine to take a deposit before you open a chat.

You are a fit if People already ask you to “just look at this.” You can name what “done” looks like without a two-hour discovery call. You can keep a 7-day or 14-day date. You are willing to write a one-page agreement that fits on a phone.

Skip this path if You freeze when someone asks what it costs. You want to be available for any AI problem. You cannot say no to a sixth round of “quick tweaks.” You have never shipped the result for yourself. Do not sell a week of posts if you have never posted a week.

If you only want to sell files and never talk to a buyer, use the playbook or template path. This path is faster cash because a human is in the loop. That human is you. Plan for that.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this before you design a logo: “[Name of package]: [deliverable] in [days] for [price]. You get files, not a login to my brain. One revision window. Deposit holds the seat.”

Sam’s sentence is the model: 12 posts and one landing draft in 7 days for $600. You can swap the deliverable. You cannot swap the specificity. If you cannot fit the offer on one line, you have two packages. Split them, then pick one.

Who it is for, in the same breath Name the buyer next to the deliverable. “For a one-person shop that needs a week of posts and a page that matches, without living in a chat window.” If the buyer is “anyone using AI,” you will spend intake explaining what you do.

What is in, what is not In: the 12 posts, the one landing draft, one revision, a 15-minute walkthrough, files in a folder they own. Not in: a strategy offsite, a brand workshop, managing their accounts, a second landing page, unlimited voice notes. Write the “not in” list. Buyers relax when they see the walls.

What ‘done’ looks like

Done is one paid package with a written scope. Not a maybe. Not a “we’ll figure it out on a call.” Money in, scope on a page, date on a calendar.

The job bar - Deliverable they can open without you (a folder they own) - Timeline you kept, or you told them before the date slipped - One revision window, used or expired - A 15-minute walkthrough, not a second project hiding in a call - They never saw the raw model output

The business bar Deposit arrived before you started. The one-page agreement was sent. You asked for a referral while they were happy. You wrote the SOP so the second job is faster. If you skip the SOP, you sold a job. You did not build a path.

Price, deposit, and the first-seat discount

First packages live at $300–$1,500. Under $300 you will attract people who want a miracle and a friend. Over $1,500 on job one, you will invent a process during a job that is too expensive to fumble. Sam’s $600 for a 7-day content-and-landing package sits in the honest middle: real money, finishable week.

How to pick the number Estimate the hours if you were slow, including edits. If it is ten hours and you cannot do it in a week, the package is too big. Cut the deliverable, not the sleep. Then pick a round number you can say out loud without coughing: $600, $800, $1,200. Do not use $597. You are not a storefront banner.

Deposit Take a deposit before you open a chat window. Half is clean. The rest on delivery, before the revision window if you like, or after the walkthrough. Stripe invoice or a deposit link. If they need to “just hop on a call first,” the call is the job. Charge for a paid consult or send the cheaper file instead.

The first-seat discount Offer one discounted first seat if you need a case, then never discount it again without a reason you can say out loud. “First seat, $400, I can use the result as a named example” is a reason. “You seem nice” is not. After that seat, full price.

The first 72 hours

You do not need a portfolio site. You need one page, ten asks, and a way to take money.

Hours 1–6: write the package on one page Who it is for. What is in. What is not. Timeline. Price. What they must provide (voice notes, old posts, offer, deadline, three examples they like). How many revisions (one is enough). Deposit. Where the files will live. Put your name and a start date like “next Monday if the deposit is in by Friday.”

Hours 7–24: the rails Calendly for a 15-minute kickoff or walkthrough — not a free hour. Stripe invoice or deposit link. A one-page agreement that repeats the package page in slightly drier words. Test the payment link with a dollar if you need to know it works.

Hours 25–72: ten people who already trust you Send the package to ten people who already trust you. Not a cold list. People who have asked you for help, hired you adjacent, or complained about the exact job. One discounted first seat if you need a case. When they say yes, take the deposit before you open a chat window. Then intake. Then you start.

If nobody says yes in 72 hours, you still have a page. Keep asking. Three to fourteen days with outreach is the window, not three hours of hoping.

The path, step by step

Twelve steps. One package all the way through. If you have two, you have none.

Step 1. One package Name, deliverable, days, price. Write it so a tired person can forward it. If you add a second package this week, you will explain both and sell neither.

Step 2. Intake: problem, budget, ability to implement If they have no problem you can name, no budget for $300–$1,500, or no ability to post what you make, stop. Send them the cheaper file instead. A buyer who cannot implement will blame the posts.

Step 3. Deposit, then chat Invoice or deposit link first. Kickoff after. Working before the deposit is how “quick questions” become the whole job, unpaid.

Step 4. Kickoff form Assets, voice, deadline, examples they like, words they refuse, the offer the posts must serve. A form beats a 45-minute call that you have to transcribe.

Step 5. You run AI internally Draft in a tool they do not sit in. Edit until it sounds like them, not like a model. They never see the raw output. If a line is wrong, it is your line.

Step 6. Midpoint check, short A screenshot or three sample posts on day 3, not a new scope. If they want a different product, that is a different package after this one.

Step 7. Delivery in a folder they own Google Drive, Notion, a zip — they keep it if you disappear. No hostage login.

Step 8. A 15-minute walkthrough Show them where things live and what to post first. Then stop. The walkthrough is not a workshop.

Step 9. One revision window Written dates. “One round, 48 hours after delivery.” After that, a new package or a polite no.

Step 10. Ask for the referral while they are happy The day of the walkthrough, not a month later. One sentence. One ask.

Step 11. Write the SOP What you asked for, which prompts you used, where you edited, how long it actually took. The second job should be faster. If it is not, you did not write the SOP. You remembered vibes.

Step 12. Full price, no discount The first seat may have been cheaper. This one is not, unless you have a reason you can say out loud. Book the next seat before you invent package two.

Worked example: Sam’s $600 week

Sam writes for small service businesses on the side of a day job. People kept asking for “help with AI content.” That sentence made Sam tired. Sam named a package instead: 12 posts and one landing draft in 7 days for $600. Files in a folder. One revision. Deposit holds the seat.

The one-pager Who: a one-person shop that already knows what they sell and needs a week of posts plus a page that does not sound like a brochure. In: 12 posts (mix of short updates and two longer notes), one landing draft (headline, sections, FAQ, closing ask), one revision, 15-minute walkthrough. Not in: posting for them, a logo, a second offer, strategy calls. They must provide: offer, three examples they like, words they hate, any photos they already have, a deadline if a launch is real. Timeline: 7 days from deposit. Price: $600. Deposit: $300. Revision: one round, 48 hours after delivery.

The first seat Sam sent the page to ten people who had asked for help. One owner of a tutoring practice said yes at $450 as the case-study seat, with permission to describe the result without student names. Deposit first. Kickoff form the same day. Sam drafted in a private chat, edited every post, and sent three samples on day 3. The owner changed one promise that was too strong. Sam fixed the rest to match. Day 7: folder, walkthrough, invoice for the balance. One revision on two posts. Done.

After Sam wrote a two-page SOP: form questions, the prompt stack, the edit pass, the folder structure. Next job was $600, no discount. A referral from the tutor paid for week three. Sam still takes notes for a playbook. The package prints cash this month. The file can wait.

Copy you can paste

Say the package. Then stop talking.

One-pager headline 12 posts and one landing draft in 7 days. You get files, not a login to my brain.

Ask (to ten people who already trust you) I have one seat this week for [package] at [price]. Deposit holds it. Here is what is in, what is not, and the date: [link]. If it is not useful, ignore this.

First-seat version I am taking one discounted first seat at [lower price] so I can use the result as a named example (you can say no to the name). Same scope, same week. After this, it is [full price].

After they say yes Sending the deposit link now. Kickoff form follows when it clears. I start on the date in the one-pager, not before.

Referral ask If someone you know needs [package] this month, send them the page. I have [one] seat left in [week]. I will treat them like I treated you.

What not to write “Let’s hop on a quick call and I’ll see how I can help.” That call becomes the job. “Unlimited revisions until you love it.” That is a hostage situation with extra steps.

Week-by-week

This is a 3–14 day path if you already know the job. Treat week one as a job, not a brand launch.

Day 1: package page Write the one-pager. Price, deposit, in/out, revision rule. Calendly, Stripe invoice or deposit link, one-page agreement. Test the link.

Days 2–4: ten asks Personal messages. One discounted first seat only if you truly need a case. Do not post a menu. Do not add a second package because the first ten were quiet.

Days 5–7: deliver the first job Deposit, form, draft internally, edit, midpoint sample, folder, 15-minute walkthrough, one revision window. They never see raw model output.

Week 2: SOP Write what you actually did. Time it. Cut anything that did not earn its minutes. Ask for the referral.

Week 3: full price, no discount Same package. Same days. Better SOP. If you want a second product, make it the playbook from the notes, not a second service that splits your calendar.

The quality bar

They should be able to use the files the day you hand them over, without you in the room after the walkthrough. That is the bar.

The raw-output test If you would be embarrassed to put your name under a paragraph, it does not ship. Models draft. You cut hype, check facts they gave you, and match their examples. A landing draft that could belong to any business is not done.

The date test 7 days means 7 days from deposit, not from the fifth follow-up email. If you cannot keep the date, the package is too big. Cut posts, not honesty.

The revision test One round. Written. If you need three rounds to make it good, your midpoint check is missing or you skipped the form. Do not buy peace with extra rounds. You will teach the next buyer that the wall is fake.

The folder test They own it. A login you control is not delivery. If the host dies, they still have the words.

What to count

Count jobs, not vibes.

Count these - Paid packages with written scope. First one is the milestone. - Days from ask to deposit. If this is over two weeks, your page is vague or you are asking strangers. - Hours you actually spent versus the SOP. If job two is not faster, the SOP is a diary, not a process. - Revision use. If every job uses the round to change the offer, intake is broken. - Referrals asked vs referrals received. Asking once, on the happy day, is the whole system.

Ignore these for now A public “AI agency” identity. Hour charts. A second service line. Testimonials you wrote for them. Count cash collected and dates kept.

A good first month is one or two $600-class jobs delivered on time, not twelve half-sold conversations.

Objections you will hear

“Can we hop on a call first?” You can do a 15-minute Calendly after they have the one-pager, or you can send the cheaper file. A free hour is the job. Do not donate it.

“I need a custom thing.” Custom everything is on the leave-alone list. If their job is 80 percent your package, sell the package. If it is 20 percent, say no or quote a different dated package later — not this week.

“$600 is a lot for something AI can do.” AI can draft. They are paying for 12 posts and a landing draft that match their offer, in 7 days, with your name on the quality. If they want the window, they already have it. Stay kind. Do not argue economics. Point at the date and the files.

“Can you just do hours?” No. Hours have no done. Offer the package or the file.

“Unlimited revisions until I’m happy.” One is enough. Happiness with no wall is a project without a date. Write the window. Mean it.

“I do not have examples or an offer yet.” Then they cannot implement. Send a cheaper diagnostic or playbook. Do not invent their business inside a $600 week.

What this path feeds

This path is the cash pipe. The notes are the product pipe.

What it fills Every job becomes a playbook chapter, a template, or a letter issue. Sam’s 12-post week is a filled calendar someone else might buy as a $19 file later. Referrals fill the next seats. People who cannot afford $600 still need a door: send the cheaper file instead of shrinking the package.

What it needs A named package, a deposit link, a spine. It does not need a menu of 12 services. It does not need a team in month one. When the SOP is real and you are full, raise the price inside $300–$1,500 or add a week of wait, before you hire a stranger to talk to your buyers.

Mistakes that eat the week

Work before money, and no walls Working before the deposit. Then you are a friend with a skill, and friends do not get a date on a calendar.

Unlimited revisions. Each round teaches them the first delivery was a suggestion.

Letting them “just hop on a call” that becomes the whole job. Calendly is for kickoff and walkthrough, 15 minutes, after money or after the one-pager. Not for free design-by-conversation.

Raw output and a menu of twelve Delivering unedited AI text. They can smell it. Your name is on it. The model does not refund them.

A menu of 12 services. Custom everything. Starting two packages because you were afraid the first one was too narrow. Narrow is how you keep the date.

Also: disappearing into Slack so the job never ends. Hosting files in a place they cannot export. Discounting seat two because seat one felt awkward. Skipping the referral ask. Skipping the SOP. Those turn a path into a string of tired one-offs.

Tools (keep them boring)

Calendly, Stripe invoice or deposit link, a one-page agreement. That is enough to take money and a date.

Calendly 15-minute kickoff or walkthrough. Not a 60-minute “discovery.” Put the package link in the invite. If they need longer, they need a different product.

Stripe invoice or deposit link Deposit before work. Balance before or at delivery. Do not take payment in a chat app that will not help you when a charge is disputed.

One-page agreement Repeats the one-pager: in, out, date, price, deposit, revision window, where files live, what they must provide. You are not playing lawyer. You are writing the same sentences in a form they can screenshot.

AI Internal. Draft only. You edit. No shared chat with the client as the deliverable.

Leave retainers, agency CRMs, and a stack of proposal software on the shelf until you have delivered the same package three times.

Checklist

Tick this before you start job two.

Package page - One package, one line: deliverable, days, price - Who it is for - What is in / what is not - What they must provide - One revision, with a clock - Deposit amount and what it holds - Definition of done (folder they own, walkthrough)

Rails - Calendly 15 minutes - Stripe invoice or deposit link, tested - One-page agreement - Kickoff form

First job - Ten asks to people who already trust you - One discounted first seat only if you need a case - Deposit before chat - AI internally; they never see raw output - Delivery on the date - Referral ask while they are happy - SOP written the same week - Next seat at full price

FAQ

Can I sell two packages if they are related? Not in week one. If you have two, you have none. Deliver the same one three times. Then, maybe, a smaller file for people who fail intake.

What if they want me to post for them too? That is a different dated package, or a no. Do not slide posting into the $600 week for free. It has no done.

Do I need Calendly if we already chat? Yes, or something with a hard 15-minute wall. Chat has no wall.

How do I handle a bad fit after they pay? Refund the unused work, keep the deposit if you already started and the agreement says so, and stop. Do not work angry to “make it right” with extra scope.

Can I use their job as a case? Ask on the form. Default to fake names. Sam’s tutor said yes to a nameless description. That is enough.

What if I miss the 7-day date? Tell them before the date. Cut scope with their written OK, or finish and own it. Silent delay is how you pay in reputation.

Is $300 too low? It can be a first-seat price. As a habit it attracts people who want a friend. Move toward $600–$1,500 as soon as the SOP is real.

Should I hire someone to draft? Not until you have an SOP and you are full at full price. Hiring on job one is how quality becomes a rumor.

They want unlimited Slack access. No. Folder, form, one revision window, 15-minute walkthrough. Access without a date is hours with extra typing.

When do I turn this into a playbook? When you have notes from two jobs. The package still sells. The file is extra cash from people who will never hire you. Do not pause the package to write the book unless you are full.

PATH 08

Affiliate the tools you actually use

The review must stand if the link is removed. That is the whole ethic, and the whole conversion trick.

17 minute read · 3,644 words

Who this is for
You already recommend models, hosts, editors, or shops. You can write why, not just drop a URL.
First sale
One commission from a review a real person used.
Time to first
14–45 days (cookie + trust lag)
Cash window
Varies; treat as a sidecar, not the engine
Tools
Official partner programs, a simple page or newsletter, honest disclosure.
Leave this alone
Twenty links in a thread. Tools you have never opened.

ON THIS PAGE

How this pays without rotting your name

Affiliate is monetization, not a traffic plan. If nobody reads you, the link does nothing. Short-form and the weekly letter bring the people. This path takes a cut when they buy a tool you would keep using if the commission died.

The review must stand if the link is removed. That is the whole ethic, and the whole conversion trick. Write as if tomorrow a lawyer strips every URL. What remains should still help a tired person decide. If what remains is empty cheer, you wrote an ad. Ads from strangers do not convert, and they do not age well next to your name.

Why honest beats a glowing list Write what broke. What you still use. Who should skip it. That piece outsells a glowing list because tired people can smell a list. They have been burned by “best 50” pages that were updated never and used never. One page, one tool, your stack, a date on it — that is the product.

What this is not It is not a coupon blog. It is not twenty links in a thread. It is not a tool you opened once on a free weekend. It is a sidecar on work you already do: a letter, a playbook, a package. Treat commissions as extra, not as the engine. If the engine is affiliate, you will start recommending whatever pays this month, and your name will follow the payout.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you already recommend models, hosts, editors, or shops, and you can write why, not just drop a URL. You need a real stack. You need a place to publish one page or one issue. You need the patience for cookie lag.

You are a fit if You pay for a tool with your own money. You have told a friend, in plain words, why you kept it. You are willing to put a disclosure at the top that you would want from that friend. You already have (or are building) a letter or a simple page where a review can live for a year.

Skip this path if You do not use anything you would recommend with the link ripped out. You want cash this week — cookies take 14–45 days, and some programs pay later than that. You like roundups of tools you have never opened. You will not update a page when the price changes. A dated lie is worse than no page.

If you have no readers yet, start the letter or the front door first. Affiliate does not create attention. It rides it.

The offer, named in one sentence

You are not selling the tool. You are publishing a review a real person can use, with a link underneath for the ones who decide to buy. The sentence: “I use [tool] for [job]. Here is who it is for, how long setup took, what I tried, what failed, and who should not buy. If you purchase through this link I may earn a cut. The review is the same either way.”

One tool, one page Do not build a “best 50 AI tools” page. That is a farm. One honest page per tool you love. If you use two things (a model and a host), that is two pages, written months apart if you need the time, each with its own disclosure and its own date.

What the reader gets even if they never click A decision. Setup time. A failure they can avoid. A skip rule. If they finish the page and choose a competitor, you still did the job. That is how the next review gets believed.

What ‘done’ looks like

Done is one commission from a review a real person used. Not a screenshot of a dashboard full of pending cookies. A human told you they bought because the page named the thing that was stuck, or you saw a conversion you can tie to that URL.

The page bar - Disclosure at the top, in words you would want from a friend - Link below the reasoning, not above it - Who it is for, setup time, what you tried, what failed, who should not buy - Your own stack. Screenshot your own dashboard if the program allows it. No fake screenshots. - A date. Updated when pricing or the product changes.

The money bar Treat it as a sidecar until the list is real. One commission is done for v1. A farm of fifty unpaid roundups is not a business. It is a hole.

How the money actually shows up

You do not set the product price. The program does. Your job is to pick tools you already use, join official partner programs, and accept that cash is lumpy.

Cookie and trust lag Time to first is 14–45 days because people read, wait, compare, then buy, and the cookie has to still be alive. Some programs pay on a schedule after that. If you need rent on Friday, this is the wrong path for Friday. Run a package. Let this ride along.

What “varies” means A host or a model can pay a cut of a month, a year, or a one-time seat. Do not build a spending plan on a number you saw in someone else’s screenshot. Count commissions after they clear. Until then, it is a maybe.

What you never do for a higher cut You never hide the disclosure. You never pretend you still use a tool you cancelled. You never stuff every paragraph with links. You never invent a claim the program bans (“guaranteed income,” “official partner of the year” when you are not). The extra click is not worth the name.

The first 72 hours

You are not launching a media company. You are joining two programs and writing one review.

Hours 1–8: programs and rules Join two official partner programs for tools you already use. Not four. Not a directory of “high paying.” Two you can screenshot from your own login. Read the trademark rules and the disclosure rules. Note what you may not say. Note whether screenshots of the dashboard are allowed. If a program wants you to hide the relationship, skip it.

Hours 9–24: write the review Structure: who it is for, setup time, what you tried, what failed, who should not buy. Disclosure at the top. Link below the reasoning, not above it. Date it. Use a failure you actually had — a setup that took three hours, a feature that sounded magic and was not, a price change that annoyed you. Then say why you stayed.

Hours 25–72: publish and tell the people who already asked Put it on a simple page or in the newsletter. Same disclosure you would want from a friend. Share it once with people who asked you about that tool last month. Once. Not a week of reminders. Then wait. Cookies do not clear because you refreshed the dashboard.

The path, step by step

Twelve steps. One tool until the page is good.

Step 1. Only tools in your actual stack If you would cancel it when the affiliate account dies, it does not belong on the page. Screenshot your own dashboard if allowed.

Step 2. Join official programs only No third-party link cloaks you do not understand. No “network” that will swap the vendor later. Read the rules.

Step 3. Pick one tool for review one A model you draft with, or a host you send with. Not both in one URL soup.

Step 4. Write the structure on paper before the pitch Who it is for. Setup time. What I tried. What failed. Who should not buy. If a section is empty, you do not know the tool yet. Use it another week.

Step 5. Disclose at the top Quiet honesty converts better than a hidden link. Use the line in the copy section. Do not bury it under a fold of adjectives.

Step 6. Put the link below the reasoning After the skip rule is a good place. Once, maybe twice. Not in every sentence.

Step 7. Date the page Month and year. A dated review is a trust machine. “Updated” without a date is a trick people know.

Step 8. Publish on a simple page or in the letter The letter is allowed to carry the review. The page is allowed to be ugly. Working beats a microsite.

Step 9. Share once where people already asked Last month’s DMs. The group you actually help. Not a spray of “best tool??” comments under strangers.

Step 10. Do not build a best-50 farm One honest page per tool you love. A second tool waits until the first page has been read by someone who is not you.

Step 11. Update when pricing or the product changes A weekend, twice a year, or when they move a button you described. Wrong prices rot faster than wrong adjectives.

Step 12. Treat commissions as a sidecar until the list is real Do not quit a package or pause the letter because a cookie might convert. Keep the engine. Let this ride.

Worked example: Riley’s one honest review

Riley drafts client follow-ups in a paid model and sends a Tuesday letter from a paid host. People in the inbox kept asking which model. Riley did not drop a link in a reply. Riley wrote one page.

The disclosure, on top “I use this model for drafts. If you buy through this link I may earn a cut. The review is the same either way. I would keep paying for it if the program vanished tomorrow. Updated March.”

The review Who it is for: people who write the same kind of client note every week and are tired of restating the voice. Not for people who need a model to invent a business.

Setup time: forty minutes to a project with instructions, three good examples, and a “never do this” list. Riley said the first evening was messy. That line mattered more than a feature table.

What they tried: recap emails, a landing draft, a joke post. The recap emails were the win. The joke post was embarrassing and got cut. What failed: asking it to “sound more human,” which made it sound like a brochure. The fix was pasting two real emails and saying “match these, do not improve my personality.”

Who should not buy: anyone who will paste the raw output to a client. Riley says that twice. The model drafts. The human sends.

The link sits after the skip rule. One link. No “top 10 models.” No fake dashboard. Riley’s own project list, names blurred, because the program allows it.

What happened Riley shared the page once with six people who had asked last month, and once in the Tuesday letter, below a story about the joke post that failed. Two weeks later a commission cleared. A third person wrote to say they skipped the tool, which Riley counts as the page working. Month 2, the host changed a plan name. Riley updated the date and one paragraph. Still one tool. Still not a farm.

Copy you can paste

Keep the disclosure boring. Boring is trustworthy.

Disclosure at the top I use this. If you buy through this link I may earn a cut. The review is the same either way.

Longer disclosure (if the program wants more) I am a [program] partner. I pay for [tool] with my own money and use it for [job]. Affiliate links may earn me a commission. I do not get paid to hide problems. Last updated [month year].

Page title I use [tool] for [job] — who it is for, what failed, who should skip it.

Share to people who already asked You asked me about [tool] last month. I wrote the page I wanted to send you: setup time, what broke, who should not buy. Disclosure at the top. Link at the bottom if you still want it: [url].

Letter close (after a real story) I drafted this issue in [tool]. If you want the review, it is here. If you want next Tuesday, it still arrives with no click required.

What not to write “Best AI tool of the year!!” Twenty links in a thread. “I made $12,000 in a weekend.” Claims the program bans. A disclosure in gray eight-point under a hero button.

Week-by-week

This path is slow on purpose. Plan the wait so you do not sabotage it with a farm.

Week 1: two programs, one review Join two official programs you already use. Read the rules. Write one review with the structure above. Disclosure on top. Link below. Publish. Share once with people who asked.

Week 2: share it where people already asked If you have a letter, one issue can carry the story, then the link. If you do not, the page plus the personal notes is enough. Do not start review two.

Weeks 3–6: do the other paths Cookie and trust lag live here. Keep sending the letter. Keep the package. Check the partner dashboard once a week, not once an hour.

Month 2: update the review Price, product, or your use — if something changed, change the page and the date. If nothing changed, add one paragraph about a new job you actually ran through the tool. Treat commissions as a sidecar until the list is real.

Do not add nineteen more tools because week 4 was quiet. Quiet is normal.

The quality bar

The stripped-link test is the bar. Remove every URL. Read the page. If a careful person could still decide, you are done. If the page collapses into “it is great, click here,” write it again.

The friend test Would you forward this to a friend who asked in good faith, even if they used a different link? If you would be embarrassed, it is an ad.

The failure test If you cannot name something that broke, you have not used it long enough, or you are hiding. Setup time that was worse than the marketing page counts. A feature you turned off counts. “Nothing failed” is a glowing list in one sentence.

The skip test Name who should not buy. Riley’s “do not paste raw output to a client” is a skip rule and a quality rule. A review with no skip is a brochure.

The screenshot test Your dashboard, if allowed. Never a fake. Never someone else’s crop with the URL bar missing in a suspicious way. If screenshots are banned, use words.

What to count

Count slow numbers. This path lies if you watch it daily.

Count these - Reviews published that would stand with the link removed. Target: one. - People who already asked, that you actually sent the page to. Six to ten is a real week 1. - Clicks, if the program shows them, without panicking at a quiet day. - Commissions that cleared, not pending. First cleared one is the milestone. - Updates per review per year. Zero updates and a price change on their site means your page is now wrong.

Ignore these for now Other people’s disclosed income. Rankings of “best affiliate offers.” How many tools you could join. A 50-tool content calendar.

If you have clicks and no commissions after 45 days, read the review again. Often the link is above the reasoning (people bounce) or you recommended a plan nobody in your audience needs. Fix the page. Do not add a roundup.

Objections you will hear

“Affiliate is sleazy.” Hidden affiliate is sleazy. A disclosure on top and a review that names failure is how people already recommend tools, with a cookie attached. If you are ashamed of the tool, do not join the program.

“I need traffic first.” Yes. This is not a traffic plan. Build the letter or the door. Then the page has somewhere to live.

“Programs take too long to approve.” Some do. Use the wait to write the review without the link, then add the URL. Do not apply to twelve programs to soothe the wait.

“What if they buy and the cookie misses?” It happens. The review still did the job. Do not add five tracking scripts to squeeze a maybe. You will look like a farm.

“Can I round up five tools I kind of used?” No. That is the glowing list. One tool you love. Tired people can smell a list.

“The commission is small.” It is a sidecar. Small plus honest plus a list that already trusts you beats large plus a name you cannot reuse.

What this path feeds — and what it needs

Affiliate rides the other paths. It does not replace them.

What it fills A Tuesday issue can tell the story of what failed, then point to the review. A playbook can mention the model you used, with the same disclosure. A package can include “here is the tool I draft in; you do not have to buy it.” The cut is extra. The trust is the asset.

What it needs People. Official programs. One page. A stack you still pay for. It needs you to leave twenty-link threads alone. If the letter dies, this path dies with it. Keep the letter.

Mistakes that rot the name

Fake, cancelled, and stuffed Fake screenshots. They will be caught, and even if they are not, you will know. Recommending a tool you cancelled. The page becomes a trap for people who trust you.

Stuffing every paragraph with links. That is how readers stop reading, and how some programs warn you.

Forgetting that some programs ban certain claims. Income promises, trademark misuse, “official” language you were not given. Read the rules in week 1, not after a warning.

The farm Twenty links in a thread. Tools you have never opened. A best-50 farm. Hiding the disclosure. Dating the page “updated” without a month. Those are the same mistake: you optimized for a click instead of a decision.

Also: building this before you have a place people already look. A perfect review in a forest is still a forest.

Tools (official, few, used)

Official partner programs, a simple page or newsletter, honest disclosure. That is the stack.

Official partner programs Join from the vendor’s own site. Two to start, both in your actual stack: a model, a host, an editor, a shop you already pay. Read trademark and disclosure rules before you write a headline.

A simple page or newsletter The letter you already send is enough. A single page with a date is enough. You do not need a review site template with 40 comparison tables.

Honest disclosure Plain sentences at the top. Not a legal poem. Not a tiny footnote. The same words you would want from a friend.

Leave link-in-bio mazes of twenty tools, unofficial coupon extensions, and any program that asks you to hide the relationship. If they are shy, you should be too — all the way out the door.

Checklist

Tick this before you add a second tool.

Before you publish - Tool is in your actual stack; you would keep paying without the program - Official partner account, rules read - Disclosure written at the top - Structure complete: who it is for, setup time, what you tried, what failed, who should not buy - Link below the reasoning - Screenshot is yours, or no screenshot if banned - Date on the page - No banned claims

First 72 hours - One review, not a roundup - Shared once with people who already asked - Letter issue optional, still one link

Ongoing - Update when price or product changes - Commissions counted when they clear - Sidecar, not the engine - Still no best-50 page

FAQ

Can I join a program for a tool I used once? No. Use it until you can fill the failure section without inventing. Once is a first date. A review is a relationship.

Do I have to say “affiliate” in the first line? Say you may earn a cut if they buy through the link, and that the review does not change. That is the honesty. Use the word “affiliate” if the program or the law you are under wants it. Quiet and early beats clever and late.

Newsletter or a page? Either. Riley did a page and mentioned it in the letter. If you only have the letter, the issue can be the review. Keep the disclosure at the top of that issue.

How many links on the page? One primary. A second if there is a cheaper plan you actually think they should consider. Not a forest.

What if the program rejects me? Write the review anyway without a link, or pick the second tool you already use. Rejection is not a prompt to join a shady network.

Can I use AI to write the review? You can outline. You still have to add the scar: setup time, what failed, who should skip it. A model will write a glowing list. That is the thing we are not doing.

When do I add a second review? After someone who is not you used the first one, and you have updated it once. Then one more tool from the stack. Still not fifty.

Is this enough to live on? Treat it as a sidecar. Some months will surprise you. Do not schedule rent on a surprise.

What if my audience asks for a tool I hate? Tell them you do not use it. No link. Your skip rule is allowed to be the whole email.

The vendor changed pricing and my page is wrong. Update this week. Change the date. A dated correction builds more trust than a perfect first draft you abandoned.

PATH 09

AI printables and small files

Solve a Tuesday problem. Mood boards are a hobby. Trackers are a product.

18 minute read · 4,068 words

Who this is for
You can design a clean one-page tool: tracker, checklist, wall print, planner page.
First sale
Someone prints the $9 file.
Time to first
2–10 days
Cash window
$7–$12 single, $19 bundle
Tools
Canva, a mockup, Stan or Etsy, AI for copy not for stolen art.
Leave this alone
Generic aesthetic planners with no job.

ON THIS PAGE

  1. 01 · What you are selling
  2. 02 · Who this is for — and who should skip it
  3. 03 · The offer, named in one sentence
  4. 04 · What done looks like
  5. 05 · Pricing that matches a printed page
  6. 06 · The first 72 hours
  7. 07 · The path, step by step
  8. 08 · Worked example: Harper and the hairstylist tracker
  9. 09 · Copy you can paste
  10. 10 · Week-by-week
  11. 11 · The quality bar
  12. 12 · What to measure
  13. 13 · Objections you will hear
  14. 14 · What this path feeds next
  15. 15 · Mistakes that kill this path
  16. 16 · Tools
  17. 17 · Checklist
  18. 18 · FAQ

What you are selling

A printable is a page someone will actually print, fill, and pin to a wall or drop in a binder. That is the whole product. Not a vibe. Not a mood board. A tool for a Tuesday problem: what to post this week, what to pack, what to check before a client walks in.

Search language is the product title. ‘Weekly content tracker for solo hairstylists’ beats ‘Cute planner 2026’ because the first one names a person and a job. The second one is wallpaper. People searching Etsy or Google at 10pm are not shopping for cute. They are shopping for a page that stops the guessing.

AI can draft the copy on the page: the labels, the tiny how-to in the footer, the listing description. You still design the layout so it prints without eating the margins. The model does not know your printer. You do, after one test page.

Your own site keeps the customer. Etsy rents the aisle. Use the aisle if you have no traffic. Move them to email when you can. The file is cheap. The list is the real asset.

What this is not

This is not a 40-page undated planner with empty circles and a quote about blooming. Those exist by the thousand. They photograph well and convert poorly unless you already have a following that buys your taste.

This is also not a wall print of someone else’s illustration or a trademarked character. Your layout, your words, your simple shapes. If you cannot draw, use type, lines, and a two-color palette. Clean beats illustrated when the buyer is a working stylist, not a Pinterest board.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you can sit in Canva for two hours and ship a one-page tool that a stranger can fill without a video. You do not need to be a designer. You need margins, a hierarchy (title, then fields, then a one-line instruction), and the patience to print a test copy on the cheapest paper in the house.

It also fits if you already live near a Tuesday problem. You cut hair. You run a classroom. You pack a kit for a trade. You have watched someone (including you) invent the same list every Sunday night. That list, cleaned up, is the product.

Skip this if

Skip this path if you only want to make pretty pages with no job on them. Generic aesthetic planners with no job are the thing to leave alone. The aisle is full.

Skip it if you cannot print a test. Selling a PDF you have never held is how you ship clipped margins and a furious one-star. Borrow a printer. Use the library. Do not guess.

Skip it if you need $2,000 this week. A $9 file can add up. It does not replace a service invoice. Pair this with a paid week of work if rent is the problem.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this before you open Canva: ‘A one-page [job] for [person], printed at home, filled in ten minutes, $9.’ If you cannot fill the brackets, you do not have a product. You have a hobby file.

The offer has four parts and none of them are a color palette.

  • Person: solo hairstylist, not ‘creatives.’
  • Job: weekly content tracker, not ‘planner.’
  • Format: one US Letter PDF, print-ready, with a filled example on page two.
  • Price: $9 for the single, $19 for three related pages.

A sentence you can put on the listing

‘Weekly content tracker for solo hairstylists — one page, print at home, fill it Sunday night, walk into Monday with the posts already chosen. $9. Bundle with monthly themes and caption starters for $19.’

That sentence is the product. If the page cannot keep that promise, redesign the page. Do not rewrite the sentence to hide a weak file.

What done looks like

Done is not a Canva link in your drafts. Done is a stranger who can buy, download, print, and fill the page without emailing you.

The done bar

  • The PDF is US Letter (or A4 if that is your market), 300 dpi, fonts outlined or embedded.
  • 0.25 inch bleed if you ever send it to a print shop. 0.5 inch safe margin so home printers do not eat the last checkbox.
  • Page one is the blank tool. Page two is the same tool filled with a real example. People buy the filled page. They use the blank one.
  • A table mockup photo, not a floating PNG on fake marble if you can help it. Phone photo of the printed page next to a mug is enough.
  • Listing title uses the job and the person. Tags match how they search, not how you brand.
  • Checkout works. You bought it from a second email. The file arrived. It opened. It printed.
  • License line on the file and the listing: personal use, or small-business use if you mean that. No reselling the PDF.

If any of those is missing, it is not done. A pretty layout with a clipped margin is a refund.

Pricing that matches a printed page

Cash window: $7–$12 for a single page, $19 for a bundle of three related pages. Stay in that lane until you have reviews and a second niche.

$9 is the default single. Low enough that a tired stylist does not need a committee. High enough that you are not training people to treat you like a free Canva account. $7 if the page is truly one checkbox list. $12 if it includes the filled example plus a short how-to on the same PDF.

The $19 bundle is the real offer. Three pages that belong together: weekly tracker, monthly theme map, caption-starter sheet. Do not bundle three unrelated aesthetics. Bundle one job, three angles.

What not to do with price

Do not list at $3 to ‘get reviews.’ You will get the buyers who want $3 things, and they will review you like a $3 thing. Do not list a one-page tracker at $27. That price wants a workshop or a video. You have a page.

Do not run a 70% off launch. A clean $9 and a clean $19, stated once, will outsell a fake $40 slashed to $9. If you need a reason to email later, add a fourth page to the bundle in month two and raise the bundle to $24. Grandfather the people who already paid.

On Etsy, fees take a bite. Price so you still clear about $6 on the single after fees, or skip ads until organic search works. On your own site with Stripe, $9 is almost all yours. That is why the aisle is temporary.

The first 72 hours

You do not need a shop with 400 listings. You need three related pages, one listing, one bundle, one post.

Hours 1–8

Pick the Tuesday problem and the person. Write the title in search language. Open Canva at US Letter, turn on rulers, set 0.5 inch margins. Build page one as the tool. Labels a tired person can read from two feet away. No 8-point script font.

Ask the model for the on-page labels and an 80-word how-to footer. Rewrite until it sounds like you. Fill page two with a real week. Export PDF.

Hours 9–24

Print it. If it clips, it is not done. Fix margins. Print again. Photograph the filled page on a table. That is the listing image.

Build two sibling pages in the same type and color. Same person, same job family. Export. Make a $19 zip: three PDFs plus a one-line readme (‘Print at 100%, actual size, do not fit to page’).

Hours 25–72

List the $9 single. List the $19 bundle. Title with the job and the person. Publish. One post showing the filled page, not the empty one. Send the link to ten people who do that job. Do not invent 400 listings. Do not design a logo.

The path, step by step

Step 1. Tuesday problem, one page. Write the person and the job on a sticky note. If it takes two notes, it is two products. Solo hairstylist, weekly content. Not ‘all beauty professionals, all planning needs.’

Step 2. Steal the search language, not the art. Type the job into Etsy and Google. Copy the words buyers already use: weekly, tracker, salon, hairstylist, content calendar. Your title is a sentence made of those words. Do not copy another seller’s layout.

Step 3. Paper first. Sketch the fields. Date. Posts 1–5. Photo needed? Caption started? Posted? Notes. If you cannot sketch it on a napkin, Canva will not save you.

Step 4. Build for print, not for a screenshot. US Letter or A4. 0.5 inch safe margin. High contrast. One accent color. Checkboxes large enough for a pen. Footer: ‘Print at 100%. Personal use.’

Step 5. AI drafts the words. You own the voice. Labels, subtitle, listing bullets. Then read them out loud. If you would not say it to a stylist in a break room, cut it.

Step 6. Fill the example. Write five posts a real solo stylist would actually make this week: before-and-after, a product they use, a booking reminder, a day-in-the-chair, a closed-Sunday note.

Step 7. Print test. Home printer, cheapest paper, actual size. If a checkbox is in the unprintable zone, move it. If gray type vanishes, go black. Repeat until a printed page looks like the screen.

Step 8. License: your layout, your words. Do not sell other people’s illustrations. Do not use trademarked characters. Type and lines are enough.

Step 9. Two sibling pages, then stop. Monthly theme map. Caption starters. Same fonts, same margins. That is the $19 bundle. Do not invent 400 listings in a week.

Step 10. Mockup on a table, not a floating PNG if you can help it. Print the filled page. Photograph it. One extra image: the three pages fanned for the bundle.

Step 11. List and tell ten people. $9 single, $19 bundle. Stan, your site, or Etsy if you have no traffic. Title with the job and the person. One post, filled page, price, link.

Step 12. A how-to page on your site that ranks for the job, then sells the file. ‘How solo hairstylists plan a week of posts without sitting on their phone for two hours.’ Teach the method. Put the $9 file at the bottom. This page is yours even if Etsy changes the rules.

Worked example: Harper and the hairstylist tracker

Harper cut hair for nine years. Every Sunday she watched other solo stylists in a group chat ask the same thing: what do I post this week. She already had a notebook page she used for herself. That notebook page is the product. She did not need a new personality. She needed margins.

What she shipped

Page 1: Weekly content tracker for solo hairstylists. Seven rows (Sunday plan plus six posting days, because she takes Monday off and wanted that to be allowed). Columns: date, post type (reel, photo, story, nothing), the idea in one line, photo already shot, caption started, posted. Footer: ‘Fill this Sunday. Shoot Tuesday. Post from the list. Do not invent at 9pm.’

Page 2: the same grid filled for a real week in her old chair: a color-correction before-and-after, a ‘three questions before a bang trim’ reel, a product she actually used, a last-minute Thursday opening, a closed-Sunday photo of the empty chair.

Bundle extras: a monthly theme map (education, proof, offer, personality) and 20 caption starters in the voice of a solo stylist. AI wrote a first pass. She rewrote 14 of the 20 because they sounded like a corporate salon.

She listed the tracker at $9. Bundle at $19. Canva, kitchen table mockup, Etsy for the aisle, a Stan link on a one-page site.

What happened

Day 1–2: layout and print tests. The first export ate the last column. She moved it. Day 3: listings live. Day 4: she posted the filled page in two stylist groups she already belonged to. Day 6: first stranger sale, $9. Day 11: a bundle. End of week 3: 14 singles and 6 bundles. About $240 before fees. Not rent. Proof.

She did not make 400 listings. In week 3 she wrote a how-to: ‘A Sunday-night content hour for one-chair salons.’ That post still sends two or three sales a week. The title did the work.

Copy you can paste

Listing title

Weekly content tracker for solo hairstylists — printable one-page planner (Sunday fill-in)

Listing first line

Print it. Fill it Sunday. Walk into Monday with the week’s posts already chosen. $9.

Listing bullets

  • One US Letter PDF, print at home at 100%
  • Built for one-chair stylists, not agencies
  • Columns for idea, photo, caption, posted
  • Includes a filled example so you are not staring at empty boxes
  • Personal use. Print as many copies as you need for your own chair

Bundle title

Hairstylist content kit: weekly tracker + monthly themes + 20 caption starters — $19

Post (filled page, not a mood)

I got tired of inventing Instagram in the chair between clients. This is the one-pager I fill on Sunday. $9. Bundle with themes and captions is $19. Link in bio. If you do not need it, ignore this.

DM to ten people who do the job

I made a one-page weekly content tracker for solo stylists. Instant PDF, $9. I use the filled example on page two. If that would save you a Sunday: [link]. If not, no need to reply.

Etsy tags (pick 13)

hairstylist planner, salon content calendar, weekly content tracker, instagram planner printable, hair stylist marketing, reel planner, one page tracker, sunday planning, small salon, booth renter, content checklist, social media for hair, printable tracker

Week-by-week

Week 1: three pages live. Day 1–2, the tracker and the print test. Day 3, two sibling pages. Day 4, listings and mockup. Day 5–7, ten DMs and one public post of the filled page. Do not start a second niche.

Week 2: the $19 bundle if you listed only the single in week 1. Same files, one zip, one new listing. Answer every question in under a day. If someone cannot print it, the problem is your export until proven otherwise.

Week 3: one how-to post aimed at the Tuesday problem. Teach the Sunday hour. Ten sentences and a photo of the filled page. Link once.

Week 4: seasonal only if the niche is seasonal. A stylist tracker is not seasonal. A holiday salon hours sign is. Add one seasonal sibling if it belongs. Do not invent 400 listings. Update the original if a buyer tripped on a label.

After week 4, pick a second person with the same shape: weekly tracker for solo house cleaners, or for one-person bakeries. Same grid, new language. That is a catalog. That is not a new business.

The quality bar

Print is unforgiving. A screen hides a 9-point font and a 0.1 inch margin. A cheap inkjet does not.

Specs that keep you out of one-star land

  • 300 dpi PDF. Not a compressed PNG pretending to be a PDF.
  • Black (or near-black) for all fill-in lines. Light gray disappears on paper.
  • Checkboxes at least 0.25 inch. Pens are thicker than cursors.
  • No text in the outer 0.5 inch. Home printers crop. ‘Fit to page’ shrinks your grid and people blame you.
  • Fonts you can embed. Script fonts for labels are a hobby. The job is readable at arm’s length.
  • For home print, full-bleed color blocks will come back with a white frame. Design for that frame or warn them.

The filled page is the quality

An empty grid is homework. A filled grid is a demonstration. If you are embarrassed to fill it with a real week, the tool is too complicated or you do not know the job. Simplify until a real week fits.

License quality: your layout, your words. Keep a folder of font licenses. If you cannot point to the license in 30 seconds, do not sell the file.

What to measure

Vanity is listing views. Money is conversion and refunds.

  • Sales: singles vs bundles. If you sell 10 singles and 0 bundles, the bundle is unclear, not unwanted. Fix the title and the table photo of all three pages.
  • Conversion: shop views to orders. On Etsy, under 1% means the thumbnail or the title is wrong. On your own site, count checkout starts.
  • Time to first sale after publish. Under 10 days is normal if you actually told ten people. At day 14 with zero sales and zero messages, the job in the title is mush.
  • Print complaints. One ‘it clipped’ is a revision. Three is a pattern. Fix the file and email the new PDF.
  • Email captures. If the Etsy sale never becomes an email, you rented the customer. Add a one-line ‘want the next page? tap here’ in the PDF footer.

Ignore follower counts and anyone telling you to post three Reels a day before you have a file that prints. The file is the unit. Measure the file.

Objections you will hear

‘Etsy is saturated.’ Saturated with cute undated planners. Not saturated with ‘weekly content tracker for solo hairstylists.’ If the first title is generic, that is your saturation, not the market.

‘People will just screenshot it.’ Some will. Price $9, not $90, and stop policing the ocean. The buyer who will pay is buying convenience and a clean grid.

‘I am not a designer.’ Good. Designers over-decorate trackers. You need margins, type, and a filled example. Canva plus a print test is the skill.

‘Should I make it a Google Sheet instead?’ That is a different path. A printable is for people who want paper next to the chair. If your buyer lives in a phone, sell a sheet. Do not merge them into one confusing zip on day one.

‘Can I use AI images on the cover?’ A phone photo of a real printed page still converts better. Use the model for labels and listing copy. Not for stolen art, and not for a fake marble flat-lay.

‘I need 400 listings to rank.’ You need one file that matches a search, then a handful of siblings. Shops with huge catalogs still win on the specific titles, not the pile.

What this path feeds next

Printables are a front door, not a dead end.

The how-to page that sells the $9 file is a long-form asset. The same Sunday-hour lesson becomes a short video with the filled page on camera. People who buy the tracker are a list. The list can hear about a $19 playbook, a $47 template, or a $9 membership that adds a new page each month.

If you are near real shops, the tracker is a sample in your bag. A salon owner who likes the page is a lead for a productized week: posts plus a landing draft. Do not pitch that on day one. Deliver the file. Then ask.

Notes from buyers — ‘I needed a column for who is covering the chair’ — become version 1.1 and also become chapters in a longer guide. The cheap file teaches you the language. Keep the catalog small enough that you can still name every file in one breath.

Mistakes that kill this path

Low-resolution files. A pretty Canva design exported as a fuzzy PNG, renamed .pdf. It will look fine on a phone and shameful on paper.

Selling trademarked characters. Famous cartoons, lyrics, league logos, a salon’s wordmark. You will sell a few and then lose the shop. Your layout, your words.

Empty pages. A grid with no filled example is homework. Mood boards are a hobby. Trackers are a product.

Ignoring print bleed and margins. If it clips, it is not done. ‘Fit to page’ is not a design strategy.

Inventing 400 listings in a week. Three related pages. Then a how-to. Then a second person.

Competing only on aesthetic. Pale beige with a bow vs pale beige with a different bow. The title with the job wins.

Hiding the price. Putting ‘DM for info’ on a $9 PDF. State $9. State $19. Let them buy while the tea is still hot.

Tools

Canva. US Letter or A4 custom size. Download as PDF Print, 300 dpi. You do not need Illustrator for a tracker.

A mockup. A real table and a phone beat a $12 mockup pack on day one. If you buy a pack later, pick one that looks like a kitchen, not a showroom.

Stan or Etsy. Etsy if you have no traffic and the search terms are obvious. Stan or a Stripe payment link on your own page if you can send ten people. Use the aisle. Keep the customer.

AI for copy, not for stolen art. Labels, listing, caption starters. Then rewrite. Do not generate ‘in the style of’ a living illustrator.

A home printer or a library printer. Non-negotiable. Plus cheap paper, a black pen, and a phone for the filled-page photo.

A simple folder. v1, v1.1, licenses, listing copy. Name files like a person searching: Weekly-Content-Tracker-Solo-Hairstylist.pdf — not FinalFinal3.pdf.

Checklist

  • Person and Tuesday problem written in one line
  • Title in search language, not a cute brand name
  • One-page tool in Canva with 0.5 inch safe margin
  • Filled example on page two with a real week
  • Print test at 100%, actual size, no clipping
  • Two sibling pages for the $19 bundle
  • License: your layout, your words, no trademarked characters
  • Table photo of the printed, filled page
  • $9 single listed, $19 bundle listed, price visible
  • Test-buy from a second email, file opens, file prints
  • Ten messages to people who do the job
  • One public post of the filled page, not the empty grid
  • Footer with personal-use line and an email you will answer
  • How-to page started, even as a draft, aimed at the same job

FAQ

Do I start on Etsy or on my own site?

If nobody knows you, Etsy is the aisle. List the $9 and the $19, and put the same files on a Stan or Stripe page. After the first twenty sales, invite them off Etsy with a ‘next page is on my list’ line inside the PDF.

US Letter or A4?

Sell the size your person prints. US-heavy jobs: Letter first, A4 as a second file in the same listing if you can keep the grid honest. Do not stretch Letter to A4. Rebuild the margin.

Can I sell a fillable PDF they type in?

Yes, as a later version. V1 is print. Fillable fields break on half the phones people open Etsy on. If you add fillable, keep a print version in the zip.

How many listings before I quit?

One job, three pages, two listings (single and bundle), ten asks, one how-to post. Give that 21 days. If nothing moved, the job in the title is wrong or you never showed the filled page.

What about commercial licenses for other sellers?

Not on day one. Personal use or ‘print for your own chair’ is enough. A commercial license is a different product at a different price. Skip it until someone asks twice.

Is a wall print a printable in this path?

Yes if it does a job: a salon opening-hours sign, a ‘what to do before color’ checklist a client can photograph. No if it is a quote in script. Job first.

Should I watermark the preview?

Watermark the listing mockup, not the paid file. A giant diagonal name across the product PDF is how you get refunds. Show the filled page in photos.

What do I do when a buyer wants a custom version?

One custom at a clear fee ($40–$80 for a one-page tweak) or a polite no. If three people ask for the same extra column, that is v1.1 for everyone.

Can I use Canva Pro photos in a file I sell?

Read the current Canva license for printables you resell. When unsure, use your photo of the printed page for marketing and keep the product itself type-and-line.

How do I handle refunds?

Refund the $9 without a speech if the file failed them. Ask for a print photo if they said it clipped — you need that to fix v1.1. Then fix the file for everyone. Arguing over $9 costs more than $9.

PATH 10

Creator ads with AI scripts

Brands need simple product videos. You do not need to be famous. You need to be clear.

19 minute read · 4,173 words

Who this is for
You are comfortable on camera. You can hold a product and talk.
First sale
One paid brief for two videos.
Time to first
7–21 days
Cash window
$150–$800 for a first two-video pack
Tools
Phone, CapCut, a rate card, AI for script outlines only.
Leave this alone
Working for exposure. Promising virality.
