What you are selling

UGC-style ads are not influencer fame. They are short product videos a brand can run as ads or organic posts. Demo, list, stitch, talking-head. Fifteen to thirty seconds. Vertical. You on camera, product in hand, a sentence a person would actually say in a store aisle.

The buyer is a small brand that cannot hire a studio and does not want another polished TV spot. They want a video that looks like a customer. You are that customer, on purpose, with a script you can say without blinking at a teleprompter.

AI drafts the script outline. You throw away anything you would not say out loud to a friend while chopping onions. If the model writes ‘this revolutionary garlic press will transform your weeknight ritual,’ that line dies. ‘I used to smash garlic with a knife and a prayer. This thing takes four seconds’ stays.

You do not need to be famous. You need to be clear. A sample ad for a product already on your shelf is your portfolio. Two samples beat a nine-page media kit with follower screenshots. Brands who pay $400 for two videos are not buying your audience. They are buying files they can load into Ads Manager this week.

What this is not

This is not a promise that the ad will go viral. You cannot promise that. Anyone who asks you to guarantee ROAS on day one is asking you to lie or to become their media buyer. You are the creator of the asset. Media buying is a second package, later, if you even want it.

This is not working for exposure, free product, or ‘we will tag you.’ Free product is not a brief. A paid pack is a brief. If you want the product, buy it and film a sample. Then send the invoice path, not the barter path.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you are comfortable on camera. Not ‘love being an influencer.’ Comfortable. You can hold a product, talk for twenty seconds, and not apologize to the lens. A kitchen, a window, a phone is enough. You do not need a ring light army.

It also fits if you already buy in a niche: kitchen tools, skincare you actually use, hardware in the garage, a software login you open every morning. The niche is the drawer you already have. Samples from that drawer are honest. Samples from a niche you are faking will sound like a press release.

Skip this if

Skip it if you hate being on camera and were hoping the model would generate a face. Faceless slideshow ads exist. They are a different product. This path is you, a product, a sentence.

Skip it if you want to wait for a marketplace to send you brands. Those platforms take a cut and train you to bid against people who work for $50. You will email five brands you already use.

Skip it if you will promise virality to close the deal. Sell the files, the deadline, and one revision. Not a lottery ticket.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this on the rate card before you film: ‘Two product videos, one revision, seven days, $400. Vertical files you can run. Samples on this page.’

That is the whole offer. Not a menu of 12 packages. Not a 20-video retainer. Two videos. One pack. A date.

The four parts

  • Deliverable: two 15–30 second vertical videos (9:16), captions burned in, raw clips optional if you want to upsell later.
  • Process: they send the product facts and claims they can actually make. You script, shoot, edit. They get one round of notes. You deliver files, not a login to your brain.
  • Timeline: seven days from product-in-hand (or from the day they ship it to you) to files in a folder.
  • Price: $400 for the first two-video pack in kitchen tools. The cash window for a first pack anywhere in this path is $150–$800. Start where you can say the number without flinching, then raise.

If you cannot name the niche in the sentence — ‘two kitchen-tool product videos’ — you do not have an offer yet. ‘I make UGC’ is a category. Categories do not get replies.

What done looks like

Done is a paid brief, not a sample sitting in your camera roll. The first sale is one brand saying yes and paying a deposit for two videos.

The done bar for a pack

  • Two finished vertical files, 15–30 seconds, captions a muted viewer can follow.
  • A 3-second hook that names the job (‘I stopped mincing garlic with a knife’).
  • No medical claims, no fake urgency you know is false, no ‘doctors hate this’ garbage the brand cannot defend.
  • One revision round, written, with a 48-hour window. After that, new work is a new invoice.
  • Files named like an adult: Brand-Product-A-hook1.mp4, Brand-Product-B-demo.mp4. Delivered in a folder they own.
  • Deposit taken before you shoot. Balance on delivery. You are not a bank.

Done for the business, week one, is smaller: two sample ads live on a page with the $400 pack spelled out, five emails sent. If those five are not sent, you do not have a business. You have homework.

Pricing the two-video pack

Cash window: $150–$800 for a first two-video pack. Alex’s kitchen-tool pack sits at $400. That number is high enough to filter hobby brands who want free content, and low enough that a six-person company can expense it without a board meeting.

How to pick your number

$150–$250 if you have zero samples and need a first paid case. Say it is a founder rate for the first two brands, then it becomes $400. Write the date you will stop discounting.

$400 is the default once two samples exist. Two videos, one revision, seven days. Rush in 72 hours: add $150. Extra revision: $75. Extra video in the same shoot: $150. Write those three add-ons on the rate card so they do not become a conversation.

$600–$800 when you have three paid brands in the same niche and a folder of ads that ran. Do not jump there on day one. You have no proof yet.

What you never do

You never work for exposure. You never take ‘we will send you the product and a coupon code’ as payment. You never promise a CPM, a ROAS, or a view count. You sell files. If they want you to run the ads too, that is a second package with a second price, and you should not sell it until you have delivered the files once.

Deposit 50% to hold the week. Balance on delivery, before the revision window starts if you want leverage, or after the one revision if you want to be easier to hire. Pick one. Put it on the page.

The first 72 hours

Film two sample ads. Edit. Put them on a simple page with a package: two videos, one revision, a deadline, a price. Send the page to five small brands you already use. Do not wait to be discovered on a marketplace.

Hours 1–8

Pick the niche from your own shelf. Kitchen tools if that is your drawer. Take two products you already own. Write three hook options each. Speak them into a voice memo. Keep the ones you would say in a store. Throw out the ones that sound like a banner ad.

Hours 9–24

Shoot by a window. Phone vertical. Product in frame in the first two seconds. One take that is good enough. Then a second angle: demo the tool actually working. Edit in CapCut: captions, a light crop, 20 seconds, no trending audio you do not have the rights to. Export.

Hours 25–72

A one-page site or a Notion page: two embedded samples, the rate card line, a deposit link, an email. Send five notes to brands you already buy from. ‘I made two kitchen-tool videos as samples. I can do two for [product] in seven days for $400, one revision. Page: [link].’ That is the 72 hours. No media kit. No waiting.

The path, step by step

Step 1. Rate card before more gear. Starter pack: two videos, one revision, seven days, $400. Rush fee. Extra revision. Extra video. Put it on one page. If you cannot say the number out loud, the number is wrong or you are not ready to send email.

Step 2. Niche you already buy in. Kitchen tools, software, skincare, hardware. One. Stay there until you have repeat clients. A sample from your own shelf is honest. A sample from a niche you Googled this morning will sound like it.

Step 3. Two sample ads, not a PDF about your ‘media kit.’ Film a demo and a talking-head for products you own. Brands hire from video. They do not hire from a bio.

Step 4. Brief intake. A form, not a 40-minute ‘discovery call’ that becomes free consulting. Product facts. Three claims they can legally make. Claims you will not make. Two example ads they like (not ‘make it viral’). Ship-to address if they are sending the product. Deadline.

Step 5. Script in your mouth. AI outline: hook, problem, demo, proof, ask. Then rewrite until you can say it while walking across the kitchen. Record a voice memo. If you stumble, the line is too long.

Step 6. Shoot. Window light. Phone at eye height. Product in the first two seconds. Do the job on camera — press the garlic, peel the orange, click the software. Talking about the product without showing it is a lecture.

Step 7. Edit. CapCut is enough. Captions. Cut the breath you took before the sentence. End on the product name and the one action (‘link in bio’ is their problem; your file can end on the product). Keep a 15-second and a 25-second cut if it is easy.

Step 8. Deliver vertical files they can run. 9:16, under 100 MB if you can help it, no watermark, no your-logo end card unless they paid for a brand frame. You are not their media buyer unless that is a second package.

Step 9. One revision window. Written notes, 48 hours, one round. ‘Make it pop’ is not a note. ‘Swap the first line for the one about the dishwasher’ is a note. After the window, new invoice.

Step 10. Ask for the next SKU and one referral. While they are happy. ‘If the juicer has a sibling product, I can shoot it in the same setup for $150 extra next week.’ Then: ‘Who else in kitchen tools should see the samples?’

Step 11. Stay in one niche. The second brand is easier when you can say ‘I already shot a press, a peeler, and a juicer.’ Crossing into dental ads because someone asked is how you restart from zero.

Step 12. Raise or productize. After three paid packs, $500. After a folder of work, a monthly ‘four videos, first Friday’ retainer. Not before you have delivered once without drama.

Worked example: Alex and the $400 kitchen-tool pack

Alex cooks most nights in a small apartment kitchen. No studio. A window over the sink. A drawer of tools bought over a few years. That drawer is the niche. Alex did not pick kitchen tools because of a trend report. Alex picked them because the garlic press was already there.

The two samples

Sample 1, garlic press: 18 seconds. Hook: ‘I used to mince garlic with a knife and it made the whole board smell for days.’ Demo: clove in, squeeze, peel the leftover skin, rinse. Last line: ‘Four seconds, and the board stays clean.’ No music fight. Captions on.

Sample 2, silicone spatula: 22 seconds. Hook: ‘If your spatula melts on the pan, that is not a personality, that is a bad tool.’ Demo: scramble, scrape, hold it against the edge. Last line: ‘This one does not smell like burnt rubber. I checked.’

Both shot in one evening. Edited the next morning in CapCut. On a one-page site with this line: ‘Two product videos, one revision, seven days. $400. Samples on this page.’ Deposit link under the sentence.

The five emails

Alex listed five small kitchen brands already in the cupboard — not Amazon house brands, not All-Clad. A citrus juicer company with 12,000 Instagram followers. A board-scraper shop. A mill seller. Two spatula companies. The email was six lines, the page link, and a still from the garlic-press ad. No media kit.

Day 9: the juicer brand replied. They had a new hand juicer. They wanted two ads: one demo, one ‘why I switched from a cheap press.’ They asked if Alex could ‘also run the ads and get us views.’ Alex said no to the media buying, yes to the $400 pack, 50% deposit. Product arrived on day 12.

The paid brief

Script outline from the model. Alex cut three fake-urgency lines and one claim about ‘clinically proven juice yield’ the brand could not stand behind. Shot on Saturday. Delivered Monday. One revision: they wanted the sink less messy in the background. Alex reshot the last six seconds. Balance paid. Total elapsed from first email to files: 16 days. Time to first paid brief: inside the 7–21 day window.

Alex did not become famous. Two more kitchen brands hired in the next six weeks because the juicer owner forwarded the page. Same pack. Same $400. Same niche.

Copy you can paste

Rate card line

Two product videos, one revision, seven days. $400. Samples on this page.

Add-ons, same card

  • 72-hour rush: +$150
  • Extra revision round: +$75
  • Extra video in the same shoot: +$150
  • Raw clips folder: +$50

Email to a brand you already buy from

Hi [name] — I already use [product]. I make short product videos brands can run as ads. Two samples here: [link]. I can do two videos for [their product] in seven days, one revision, $400. 50% to hold the week. If the timing is wrong, ignore this.

Page headline

Kitchen-tool product videos. You get files, not a follower pitch.

Brief questions (send as a form)

  • Product name and the URL
  • Three facts that are true
  • Any claim I must not make
  • Two ads you like (links)
  • Where to ship the product, or a date I can film what I have
  • Deadline, if it is not ‘seven days from in-hand’

Delivery note

Files are in this folder. Two vertical mp4s, captions on. One revision window, 48 hours, written notes. After that I am glad to do another round as a $75 add-on.

Week-by-week

Week 1: two samples. Day 1, pick the niche and the two products on your shelf. Day 2, shoot. Day 3, edit and put them on a page with the $400 pack. Days 4–7, five emails to brands you already buy. Do not build a marketplace profile instead of sending the five.

Week 2: follow-up and a sixth brand if needed. One follow-up, four days later, one line: ‘Looping this to the top in case it got buried. Samples still here: [link].’ If someone says ‘maybe next quarter,’ ask if they have a sibling SKU now. Do not wait.

Week 3: deliver the first paid brief. Deposit, intake, script in your mouth, shoot, edit, files. One revision. Ask for the next SKU and one name. Do not start a skincare sample ‘while you wait.’

Week 4: stay in the niche. Second pack at the same $400, or founder-rate closed. Write down what broke in the first shoot (audio, messy sink, a claim you had to cut) and make it a checklist. Four videos in your folder is a portfolio. Forty half-finished ideas is not.

The quality bar

Clear beats cinematic. A stable phone, a window, and a sentence you would say in a store will outperform a shaky dolly shot with a script you cannot pronounce.

Picture and sound

  • Product in the first two seconds. If they have to wait for the brand, they swipe.
  • Face or hands, plus the tool doing the job. Talking-head with the product off-screen is a podcast.
  • Audio you can hear on a phone speaker. A $20 lav or a quiet room. The dishwasher is off.
  • Captions. Most people watch muted. If the caption is the whole script in three giant lines, it is unreadable. One short line at a time.

Claims

You do not make medical claims. You do not make ‘this will change your life in 7 days’ claims you know are false. You do not invent a statistic. If the brand sends a claim you would not repeat to a friend, you send it back. The ad is their legal problem and your name on camera. Both matter.

Human

If you would not say it in a store aisle, it does not go in the video. AI outlines. You speak. A little mess in a real kitchen is trust. A fake marble island with a product still in the Amazon tape is not.

What to measure

You are selling packs, not views. Measure the business you control.

  • Emails sent this week. Five is the floor in week 1. Zero emails means zero briefs, no matter how good the garlic press ad is.
  • Replies and yeses. A 20–40% reply rate on warm brands you already buy from is normal. Cold marketplace bids will be worse. Stay warm until you have cases.
  • Days from yes to files. Target seven from product-in-hand. If it is 18, your revision process or your shoot block is sloppy.
  • Revision count. One is the package. Two means your brief missed the claims list. Three means you are training them to use you as an art department.
  • Repeat rate. The goal is a second SKU from the same brand, not a new niche. One repeat in the first five clients is a real business.

Do not let a brand make your metric ‘how the ad performed.’ You did not buy the traffic. You made the file. If they insist, you can ask them to send you a screenshot later for your page — as a favor, not as a warranty.

Objections you will hear

‘Can you do it for product only?’ No. Working for exposure is the thing to leave alone. You can buy the product and film a sample. That is marketing. Their product in the mail is not a salary.

‘We need this to go viral.’ You cannot promise that. You can promise two clear videos in seven days. If they need a virality clause, they need a different vendor, or they need to run paid ads, which is their job.

‘We already have a UGC platform.’ Good. Those platforms are full of $50 videos. Your page is two samples in their exact niche and a human email. Some brands still want that. If they do not, next email.

‘Can we hop on a call?’ Maybe a 15-minute walkthrough after the deposit. A 40-minute ‘let us explore’ call before money is the job. Send the brief form first.

‘Our last creator made medical claims and we got flagged.’ Put the claims-you-will-not-make box on the intake. Write it: ‘I will not say anything you cannot stand behind.’

‘$400 seems high for two TikToks.’ They are not TikToks. They are product videos a brand can run. Two samples are on the page. If $400 is wrong for them, $150 for exposure is wrong for you.

What this path feeds next

A finished pack is a case. Cases feed a $19 playbook and a skill pack of the outlines you actually used. Do not sell those until you have delivered paid work.

The same niche becomes a retainer: four videos a month, first Friday, one price. Local brands in your town who sell kitchen tools will hire you faster than a national name. Walk in with the two samples on your phone.

What this does not feed: a personal brand built on promising you can make anything viral. Keep the page boring and specific. Kitchen-tool product videos. Files in seven days.

Mistakes that kill this path

Medical claims. Fake urgency you know is false. ‘Clinically proven’ on a garlic press. You will get a brand flagged and you will not get the second SKU.

Unlimited rounds. One revision is a package. Open-ended ‘until we love it’ is a second job you forgot to price.

Waiting for a platform to send you brands. Marketplaces train you to race to $75. Five emails to brands you already use is the channel.

Working for exposure. Free product is not a brief. A tag is not a deposit.

Reading the AI script like a hostage. If you cannot say it in the kitchen, it does not go in the ad.

Promising virality. You do not control distribution. Sell the files.

Building a 12-item menu before two samples exist. Two videos, one price, one niche. Then a rate card add-on. Then a retainer. In that order.

Tools

Phone. Vertical. Clean the lens. 4K if it will still export a reasonable file; 1080p is fine for ads. You do not need a cinema camera for a spatula.

CapCut. Captions, cuts, export. Stay off music you cannot license. Silence plus the room plus your voice is legal and human.

A rate card. One page. Pack, rush, extra revision, extra video. Not a Figma palace.

AI for script outlines only. Hook, demo beats, a list of claims to avoid. Then your mouth. Then the throw-away pass.

A simple page. Carrd, Notion, Stan, a Google Doc with two unlisted YouTube or Vimeo embeds. Deposit via Stripe. Email you actually check.

A cheap lav or a quiet room. Optional tripod. Window. That is the studio.

Leave the marketplace apps alone until five organic emails have had a chance. Leave the ring-light shopping cart alone until a brand has paid.

Checklist

  • Niche picked from a drawer you already have
  • Two sample ads shot and edited, 15–30 seconds, captions on
  • Rate card: two videos, one revision, seven days, $400
  • Rush, extra revision, extra video written as add-ons
  • One-page with samples, price, deposit link
  • Brief form: facts, claims you will not make, examples they like
  • Five emails to brands you already buy
  • One follow-up four days later
  • Deposit before you shoot
  • Script said out loud, AI lines you would not say are gone
  • Vertical files delivered, named, no watermark
  • One revision window, 48 hours, written notes
  • Ask for the next SKU and one referral
  • No virality promise anywhere on the page

FAQ

Do I need followers?

No. Brands paying $400 for two videos are buying files they can run. Followers are a bonus if you have them. They are not the product. Two samples in the niche beat 40,000 followers in a different niche.

Should I join a UGC marketplace?

You can later. Do not wait on one. Those platforms are useful when you already have a pack and a rate. They are a trap when they are your only channel and the bids start at $50. Five emails first.

Who pays for the product?

For samples, you do. You already own it. For a paid brief, they ship it or you invoice a product budget. Do not buy $200 of inventory on a maybe. Deposit first, then shipping.

How long should each video be?

15–30 seconds for v1. Some ad placements want 6 seconds later. You can cut a 6-second hook from a 20-second file if you shot a clean first line. Do not deliver a 90-second story and call it an ad.

What if I freeze on camera?

Talk to the product, not the lens, for the first sample. Demo-heavy videos hide a shy face. You still need to be willing to be seen. If you cannot, this is the wrong path.

Can I use AI to generate the spokesperson?

Not for this path. Brands hiring UGC want a person. AI is for outlines. You are the mouth.

What about music?

Skip it unless the brand sends a track they own. Trending audio can get an ad pulled. Voice, captions, the sound of the tool — enough.

Do I need an LLC and a contract?

A one-page agreement is enough for pack one: deliverable, timeline, price, revision, claims, who owns the files (they do, on payment). Deposit via Stripe. The agreement cannot wait.

How do I handle a brand that wants 10 videos this week?

That is a different package. Quote it: 10 videos, two revision rounds, 14 days, $1,600, 50% deposit. Do not say yes at $400. Do not say yes at all if you cannot shoot that volume without wrecking the seven-day promise to someone else.

What if the first five emails go nowhere?

Send five more in the same niche. Change the subject line to the product you already use. Check that the page loads on a phone. Do not switch to skincare on day eight. The niche is the asset. The emails are the work.

PATH 11

Long-form explainers

Eight honest minutes on the question they already type. Soft ask at the end.

17 minute read · 3,798 words

Who this is for
You can explain a job without rushing. You can sit still for a recording.
First sale
A video that produces a click to your offer.
Time to first
30–60 days of one video a week
Cash window
Supports the $19–$47 file
Tools
YouTube, a cheap mic, a doc outline, AI for research notes you will verify.
Leave this alone
Daily uploads with no point. Copying someone else’s series.

What you are selling

Short-form borrows attention. Long-form can keep it. A video that fully answers a search question earns the right to mention your playbook at the end. That is the path: eight honest minutes on the question they already type, a soft ask at the last 45 seconds, one link in the description.

You are not selling the video. YouTube’s ad money on a new channel is a rounding error. You are selling the click to a $19–$47 file that does the job the video just taught. The video is the proof. The file is the cash window.

Thumbnails and titles are the product the algorithm and the human both see. The video keeps the promise those two things made. If the title says ‘how to plan a week of posts for a one-person cleaning company’ and the video is a 14-minute origin story about your brand, you lied. They leave. You do not get the click. You do not get the $19.

AI is useful for the outline and the counter-arguments (‘what would a skeptic say at minute four’). You verify every fact. You tell the story. If the model invented a statistic, it does not go in the video. A made-up number on camera is how you teach people not to trust the file either.

What this is not

This is not a daily vlog. This is not a second channel ‘for fun.’ This is not copying someone else’s series frame for frame. One video a week, one question, one offer. Boring on purpose. Boring ships.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you can explain a job without rushing. You can sit still for a recording. You already have, or can finish this month, a $19–$47 file that the video can point at. Without the file, long-form is a hobby with a microphone.

It also fits if you have patience measured in weeks, not hours. Time to first is 30–60 days of one video a week. Search is slow. A video that answers a real question can send clicks for years. It will not send them on Tuesday because you posted on Monday.

Skip this if

Skip it if you cannot sit through eight minutes of your own voice. Record a test. If you hate it so much you will not publish, pick short-form or a written how-to. The camera is not mandatory for a living. It is mandatory for this path.

Skip it if you want daily uploads with no point. Daily with no offer is a second job that pays in comments. Skip it if you planned to copy someone else’s series because theirs has views. Their series is their scar tissue. Yours has to be a job you can teach.

Skip it if you need cash this week. Use a service path. Come back to long-form when the $19 file exists and you can spare one recording block a week.

The offer, named in one sentence

The on-camera offer is one sentence, same every video: ‘If you want the one-page version plus the prompts, the $19 playbook is in the description.’ Soft. Once. After you have actually taught the job.

The real offer is the file. The video is a free, complete answer to one search question. You do not withhold the steps to force the click. You teach the whole small job. The file is for people who want the checklist, the prompts, and the 10-day sequence without rewinding you.

Write this before you outline video one

‘I answer [search question] in eight minutes for [person], then I point at [file] for $19.’ Example: ‘I answer how a one-person bookkeeper closes the month in Claude, then I point at the $19 month-end playbook.’

If you cannot name the search question, you do not have a video. If you cannot name the file, you are building a channel with no cash register. Do both on paper before you buy a light.

What done looks like

Done for a single video: a stranger can search the question, click your title, stay past 20 seconds, finish the job you promised, and see one clear next step. Chapters in the description. Tools listed. The offer once. An end screen with one link, not eight.

Done for the path: four videos in four weeks, same offer, titles written like searches. Then ten videos, and you double down on the three that earned saves or comments. A click that bought the $19 file is the first sale. Views without a click are a nice crowd. They are not the first sale.

The done bar on the file side

The playbook must exist and the link must work on a phone. Test it. If the description link is a Linktree with twelve buttons, you do not have a soft ask. You have a junk drawer. One link. The $19 file.

What the video is allowed to sell

Cash window: this path supports the $19–$47 file. It is not a $2,000 course funnel in month one. The video teaches a small job. The file is the job written down with templates.

$19 is the default ask at the end of an eight-minute explainer. $47 if the file includes the prompts, a filled example, and a 14-day plan. Do not pitch a $297 cohort from video three. You have no series yet. They have no reason.

YouTube ad revenue is a sidecar, not the plan. Do not wait for 1,000 subscribers to ‘monetize.’ You monetize from video one with the link. The platform’s ads are gravy if they ever arrive.

What you do not sell in the video

You do not sell a second channel, a merch line, or a vague ‘work with me.’ You do not stack five affiliate links under an eight-minute tutorial. One offer. If an affiliate tool is truly used on screen, disclose it and keep it below the playbook link. The playbook is the reason you sat down.

The first 72 hours

Search the question your buyer already types. Outline eight minutes. Record even if the lighting is boring. Publish. End with one link. Write the title like a search, not a brand campaign. Make a thumbnail that reads at phone size.

Hours 1–8

Open YouTube and Google. Type the job. Read the titles that already rank. Write five questions in their words, not yours. Pick one you can answer without a week of research. Outline: result in 20 seconds, three steps, one mistake, one recap, the $19 ask.

Ask the model for counter-arguments and a chapter list. Verify anything that looks like a fact. Delete invented numbers.

Hours 9–24

Cheap mic. Face the window. Notes on the desk, not a teleprompter novel. Record the eight minutes in one or two takes. You are allowed to be boring. You are not allowed to be false.

Hours 25–72

Edit only what you must: dead air, the cough, the 40-second ramble. Thumbnail: five words, readable on a phone, your face or the result, not a collage. Title = the search question. Description = chapters, tools, one link. Publish. Do not start a second video until this one is live.

The path, step by step

Step 1. One video a week beats five abandoned channels. Put the recording block on the calendar like a shift. Same day, same length. If you miss a week, you record two the next. You do not open a second channel to ‘reset.’

Step 2. Title is the search question. ‘How to close month-end in Claude as a solo bookkeeper’ beats ‘AI Tips That Will Change Your Practice.’ You are not a brand campaign. You are an answer.

Step 3. First 20 seconds: the result they get by staying. ‘By the end of this you will have a close checklist you can run tomorrow, including the prompt that asks for missing numbers instead of inventing them.’ Then start. No 90-second bio.

Step 4. Teach the whole small job. Do not tease a part two for a job that fits in one video. If it does not fit in eight to twelve minutes, the job is two videos with two titles, not a cliffhanger.

Step 5. Outline on a doc, not in your head. Chapters: promise, step 1, step 2, step 3, the mistake, recap, ask. AI can draft this. You reorder it so a tired person can follow.

Step 6. Verify every fact. If the model invented a statistic, it does not go in the video. If you have not run the prompt on screen, you do not claim it works.

Step 7. Record like a person at a table. Cheap mic, sit still, talk. Reading a script like a hostage is a mistake this path does not survive. Notes are fine. A novel is not.

Step 8. Thumbnail that reads at phone size. Five words. High contrast. One idea. If you cannot read it with the phone at arm’s length, it is decoration.

Step 9. Description: chapters, tools, the offer. Timestamps. The $19 link once, near the top and again at the end. Tools you actually used.

Step 10. End screen is one link. Not a grid of eight old videos and a subscribe animation that eats the ask. One button, the playbook.

Step 11. After ten videos, double down on the three that earned saves or comments. Repeat those shapes. Same structure, new question. Kill the formats that died in silence.

Step 12. Do not start a second channel. And do not start a second offer. Four weeks, one file. Then another four. Search compounds on a name, not on a network of empty accounts.

Worked example: Morgan and the $19 playbook at the end

Morgan is a solo bookkeeper who already sold a $19 playbook: a month-end close sequence for one-person bookkeepers using Claude, with the prompts and a checklist. Sales were friends-and-friends. Search was not sending anyone. Morgan could explain the job without rushing and could sit still for a recording. That is the whole qualification.

The weekly machine

One video a week, eight to ten minutes, cheap mic, kitchen table, window on the left. Every video ended on the same sentence: ‘If you want the checklist and the prompts in one file, the $19 playbook is in the description.’ No countdown. No fake scarcity.

Video 1 title: ‘How to close month-end in Claude without it inventing numbers.’ Thumbnail: ‘STOP fake totals’ in four words plus Morgan’s face. First 20 seconds: what ‘done’ looks like — a close checklist with gaps flagged, not filled with guesses. Then the three-step job. Soft ask at 8:10.

Video 2: ‘What to paste into Claude when the client’s receipts are a mess.’ Video 3: ‘The month-end prompt that asks one question at a time.’ Video 4: ‘Why your AI close keeps drifting off the trial balance.’ Each one a search question. Each one a complete small job. None of them a trailer for a course.

What happened

Week 1: 90 views, two description clicks, zero sales. Morgan did not start a second channel. Week 2: a comment from a bookkeeper who argued about GST. That argument became video 4. Week 5: 400 views on video 1 from search, 11 clicks, two $19 sales. First stranger sale from a video: day 36. Inside the 30–60 day window.

By video 10, three titles had earned comments and watch time: the inventing-numbers video, the receipts video, and the trial-balance video. Morgan repeated those shapes for the next month instead of inventing a travel vlog. The playbook did not change. The titles did the work. Daily uploads never entered the chat.

Copy you can paste

Title formulas (search, not campaign)

  • How to [job] in [tool] without [pain they already have]
  • What to paste into [tool] when [messy input]
  • Why your [result] keeps [failure], and the fix in 8 minutes

First 20 seconds

By the end of this you will have [named artifact] you can use [timeframe]. I will show the three steps, the one mistake I still see, and then a file if you want the checklist. If you only want the video, that is enough. Stay for the steps.

Soft ask at the end

If you want the one-page version plus the prompts, the $19 playbook is the first link in the description. It is the same sequence I just walked through, written down so you do not have to rewind me. If the video was enough, good. Use it.

Description block

0:00 What ‘done’ looks like
0:40 Step 1
3:00 Step 2
5:20 The mistake
7:10 Recap
8:00 The $19 file

Playbook: [one link]
Tools on screen: [names]
I use [tool]. If I mention an affiliate later it will be disclosed. Today the only ask is the file.

Thumbnail text (five words or fewer)

STOP invented numbers
Paste the messy receipts
One question at a time

Week-by-week

Week 1. Pick the question. Outline eight minutes. Record even if the lighting is boring. Publish. End with one link. Do not record four videos ‘to batch a month’ before you have seen whether you will actually upload.

Week 2. Same offer, new search question in the same job family. Read the comments from video 1. One of them is the outline. Follow up on video 1 in the community tab with a single line and the same link. Once.

Week 3. Third video. Improve the thumbnail on video 1 if the click-through is sad (under 2% after impressions exist). Do not rewrite the whole channel name.

Week 4. Fourth video. You now have a month. Look at comments and average view duration, not vanity views. Repeat the shape that earned the most saves or comments next week.

Then: one video a week for four more weeks. After ten, double down on the three that earned a response. Do not start a second channel. Do not add a second file until the first one is the only link you are willing to say out loud.

The quality bar

Honest minutes beat produced minutes. A kitchen table, a cheap mic, and a kept promise will outlast a studio intro you cannot sustain every week.

Picture and sound

Sit still. Face a window. Mic within a foot of your mouth. If the fridge is loud, unplug it for ten minutes. You do not need four-camera switching. You need words a tired bookkeeper can follow.

The promise

Title, thumbnail, and first 20 seconds agree. The video pays them off. Clickbait the video cannot pay off is the fastest way to train YouTube and humans to skip you.

Facts

You verify. The model drafts an outline and lists counter-arguments. If a number did not come from you, a primary source, or a screen you can show, it stays out. Saying ‘I do not know that number’ on camera is quality. Inventing one is not.

The ask

Soft, once, after the job is taught. Hard sells at minute one, or a missing ask at minute eight, both fail. Teach. Then point.

What to measure

A video that produces a click to your offer is the first sale unit. Views are the weather.

  • Click-through on the title and thumbnail. If impressions exist and CTR is under 2%, the search language is weak or the thumbnail is wallpaper.
  • Average view duration vs length. If they leave at 25 seconds, the first 20 seconds did not name a result. If they stay and bounce at the ask, the ask is too hard or the file is unclear.
  • Description clicks. YouTube Studio shows them. Zero clicks and a thousand views means the offer is invisible or untrusted.
  • $19 sales attributed to the week a video went up. Crude, good enough. UTM on the link if you can.
  • Comments that argue with the method. Those are video outlines. Count them. Answer them on camera, not in a 40-reply thread.

Do not measure daily upload streaks. This path is one a week. A streak of empty videos is the thing to leave alone.

Objections you will hear

‘Long-form is dead. Only Shorts work.’ Shorts borrow attention. They can send people here. They are not a replacement for eight minutes that answer a search question. Search still pays the file. Shorts without an offer pay in noise.

‘I need better gear first.’ You need a cheap mic and a question. Lighting can be a window. Gear shopping is a delay costume.

‘I should post every day to feed the algorithm.’ Daily uploads with no point are the leave-alone. One honest video a week, done, beats seven sketches you are ashamed of.

‘What if someone copies my series?’ They might copy the titles. They cannot copy the way you close a month or plan a week. Teach the whole job anyway. The file still has the prompts.

‘I do not have a playbook yet.’ Then this path is early. Write the $19 file first, or in the same 72 hours as video 1. A channel with no register is a diary.

‘My voice sounds weird.’ Everyone thinks that. Sit still. Talk. Publish video one. You will hate it. Video four is less painful. Giving up at video three is the mistake this path is famous for.

What this path feeds next

The ten videos are a library. Three of them become Shorts with the same offer. The comments become newsletter issues. The outlines become chapters in a $47 version of the file.

A video that ranks is a front door you do not have to stand in. People will still arrive in six months. That is why you do not delete video one because it only got 90 views in week one.

If a local owner comments, that is a lead for a productized week. If a brand asks you to make product videos, that is a different path — do not pivot the channel into UGC ads. Keep the explainers. Sell the other pack from a different page.

What this feeds badly: five more channels. One name. One file. One weekly block. Let the rest of the suite take the overflow.

Mistakes that kill this path

Clickbait the video cannot pay off. A title about a complete job and a video that is a teaser. They will not buy the $19 file from someone who already cheated the eight minutes.

Reading a script like a hostage. Notes, not a novel. If you cannot say it to a person across a table, do not say it to a lens.

No offer. A beautiful explainer with nowhere to go. Soft ask, one link.

Giving up at video three. Time to first is 30–60 days of one a week. Video three is still the hallway.

Daily uploads with no point. Copying someone else’s series. Starting a second channel because the first one ‘feels tainted.’ Inventing a statistic because the model sounded confident.

Teasing part two for a job that fits in one video. Teach the job. If there is a bigger job, it gets its own search title next week.

Tools

YouTube. One channel. Search titles. Studio for CTR, duration, description clicks. Do not spread the same eight minutes across four platforms in week 1. You can upload the file elsewhere later.

A cheap mic. A wired lav or a simple USB mic. Phone mics in a quiet room can work for video one. Echoey kitchens cannot.

A doc outline. Google Doc or paper. Chapters with timestamps after you edit. AI for research notes you will verify, for counter-arguments, for a title list. Not for facts you cannot show.

The $19–$47 file and a working link. Stan, Stripe, your site. Test on a phone. One link.

A window and a chair. Optional cheap light. Optional screen recording if the job is a tool. CapCut or the YouTube editor for cuts. You do not need a studio subscription to explain month-end.

Checklist

  • $19–$47 file exists and the link works on a phone
  • One search question written in the buyer’s words
  • Eight-minute outline: result, three steps, mistake, recap, ask
  • AI notes verified, invented statistics deleted
  • First 20 seconds names the result of staying
  • Recorded at a table, cheap mic, sit still
  • Title = the search question
  • Thumbnail reads at phone size, five words or fewer
  • Description: chapters, tools, one offer link
  • End screen is one link
  • Published. Not sitting in drafts
  • One video a week on the calendar
  • After ten, three winners marked to repeat
  • No second channel, no daily empty uploads

FAQ

How long should the video be?

Eight honest minutes is the default. Twelve if the job needs a screen recording. Twenty only if you are actually teaching twenty minutes of work, not stalling. People searching a how-to will stay if you pay off the title. They will not stay for a tour of your setup.

Do I need to show my face?

It helps trust. It is not required if the job is a screen and your voice is clear. A talking head at a table is the simplest version of this path. Pick one and keep it for ten videos.

Can I batch a month in a day?

You can record two on a good day. Do not batch eight before you have uploaded one. You will learn from comments. Batching a dead format is efficient waste.

Shorts as well?

After you have four long videos. Cut a 30-second piece from the mistake section. Same offer in the pinned comment. Do not replace the weekly explainer with only Shorts. Search lives on the long one.

What if I have no playbook?

Write it this week, even ugly. The video can sell a simple PDF: checklist plus prompts. That is enough for $19. Do not build a 12-week course to justify sitting down.

Should I tell a long origin story?

No. First 20 seconds are the result. Origin can be one sentence. They typed a question. Answer it.

How do I pick the next title?

Search plus comments. The question people ask under video 1 is video 2. The failure they confess is video 3. After ten, repeat the three that earned a response.

What about copyright music and clips?

Skip them. Your voice, your screen, your cheap mic. A stolen track can kill the video that was about to rank.

When do I change the offer?

When the $19 file has buyers who ask for the next layer. Then a $47 version, same channel, same weekly video, new link. Not a new channel. Not five files in the description.

I published three videos and nothing happened. Now what?

That is the hallway. Time to first is 30–60 days of one a week. Check titles against real searches. Check that the link works. Record video four on the same job. Giving up at video three is how this path dies, not how the algorithm ‘rejects’ you.

PATH 12

Local businesses + AI

Nearby owners still need pages, replies, and simple videos. Cash while digital compounds.

18 minute read · 3,870 words

Who this is for
You live near businesses that still get customers from Google, word of mouth, or a dusty site.
First sale
One local yes and a deposit.
Time to first
3–14 days of walking and writing
Cash window
$400–$2,000 packages
Tools
A one-page PDF of the package, Stripe or e-transfer, your phone.
Leave this alone
City-wide ‘we do all AI’ agencies.

What you are selling

A named week of work for a nearby owner. Not a 12-month ‘AI transformation.’ Not a city-wide agency. A package with a date on it: an AI-drafted page plus five posts, delivered by Friday. You edit every draft before they see it. You are the quality, not the model.

Nearby owners still need pages, replies, and simple videos. They get customers from Google, word of mouth, or a dusty site that still says ‘opening soon’ from 2019. They do not need your philosophy about large language models. They need a page that tells a human what the gym costs and five posts that do not sound like a robot moved to town.

You walk in or email with a one-page PDF. Price so one yes covers the week. Cash while digital compounds: this path pays now. The notes from the job become a playbook you can sell later to people not in your town. Do not reverse that order. Do not build the playbook instead of walking.

What this is not

This is not ‘we do all AI’ for every shop from the highway to the river. That sentence makes you look like a vendor who will vanish. One trade at a time. One package. One week. The first yes is allowed to be a small job. A 12-month retainer is a conversation after they have Friday’s files in hand.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you live near businesses that still get customers from Google, word of mouth, or a dusty site. You can walk into a gym or a cleaner’s office without melting. You can write a clear page in English a tired owner will recognize as their shop, not as a template from a different city.

You do not need to be a marketer of record. You need a package, a printer or a phone PDF, and the nerve to follow up the next day. Introverts can email. The work is the same: ten names, five asks, one follow-up.

Skip this if

Skip it if you want to be a city-wide ‘we do all AI’ agency. That is the thing to leave alone. You will spend a month on a pitch deck and zero days on a hallway.

Skip it if you cannot tell the owner no when they ask you to invent a claim they cannot legally make. Dentists, gyms, and cleaners all have lines. You will not be the person who writes ‘guaranteed 20 lb loss’ or ‘hospital-grade kill of every pathogen’ because the model sounded confident.

Skip it if you need to hide behind DMs with strangers in other countries this week. This path is local on purpose. The bus fare is part of the method.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write it on the PDF in a font they can read without glasses: ‘Page plus five posts this week. Deposit today, files Friday. $800.’

That is Taylor’s pack. Yours can be the same shape at a different number inside the $400–$2,000 window. The shape is the product: a named week, a page, five posts (or five videos), a Friday, a deposit.

What is in

  • One page: home or services, written from their facts, edited by you, in a doc or CMS they already use.
  • Five posts: captions plus a photo brief, or five simple videos from your phone if that is the pack you named.
  • One round of notes, 24 hours, written.
  • A 15-minute walkthrough on Friday so they know which button to press.

What is not in

Ads spend. A new logo. A 12-month content calendar. A new website platform unless they already asked and you priced it as a different job. You do not sell a retainer until you have delivered once.

What done looks like

Done for the path: one local yes and a deposit. Not a maybe. Not ‘send me something.’ Money in, date on the calendar.

Done for the week: Friday files they can use without you in the room. The page is in their voice, with their hours, their price range if they allow it, their real service list. The five posts are scheduled or sitting in a folder with the image brief. You did not paste raw model output. You cut the claims they cannot make.

The done bar

  • Deposit received before you draft.
  • Kickoff: 20 minutes or a form. Facts, photos they can send, phrases they hate, examples of posts they do not hate.
  • Drafts they never see until you have edited.
  • Friday delivery in a folder they own, plus the walkthrough.
  • A referral ask the same day: ‘Who else on this street should hear this?’

If you ghost after the posts go up, it is not done. It is a one-star story the next owner will hear at the coffee shop.

Price so one yes covers the week

Cash window: $400–$2,000 packages. Taylor’s ‘page plus five posts this week’ sits at $800. That number should cover your hours, your walking, and the fact that you cannot sell Friday twice.

$400–$500 is a founder pack if you need the first case and you will write that on the PDF as a first-seat price with a date it expires. $800 is the default once you have one sample page made from public info. $1,200–$2,000 when the page is a full services rewrite plus five videos, or when the owner has two locations.

Deposit

50% to start, 50% Friday before the walkthrough, or 50/50 with the balance on delivery. E-transfer, Stripe, whatever they already use. Do not start a draft on a handshake. Local does not mean casual about money. Local means you might see them at the grocery store, which is why a clean PDF and a clean invoice matter more, not less.

Do not discount to ‘win the street.’ One $800 yes is the week. Two $300 maybes is a mess. Do not sell a 12-month retainer at a mystery rate until they have seen Friday once. A retainer, later, can be $1,200 a month for four posts a week. That is a different PDF. After.

The first 72 hours

Write the one-pager. List ten businesses. Visit or email five. Follow up the next day. The first yes is allowed to be a small job.

Hours 1–8

One trade. Taylor starts with cleaners, then walks gyms with the same pack. You start with one. Learn ten phrases they use: ‘booth,’ ‘membership freeze,’ ‘move-out clean,’ ‘drop-in.’ Write the PDF: headline, what is in, Friday, $800, deposit, your phone, two sentences of proof if you have any (a sample page from public info counts).

Build one sample from a real local site that is dusty. Rewrite their home section in a doc. Watermark SAMPLE. You will show it, then offer to do theirs properly with their facts.

Hours 9–48

List ten. Visit or email five. Walk-in: ‘I am not selling a 12-month thing. One page, five posts, this week, $800. PDF is one page. Can I leave it with you?’ Email: six lines, the PDF, the sample, a Friday date you can actually hit.

Hours 49–72

Follow up the next day. In person or one line: ‘I can still start this week if the deposit lands today. Friday files either way.’ Do not add a second package. Do not redesign the PDF. Walk the next five if the first five were silent.

The path, step by step

Step 1. One trade. Learn their language. Cleaners, gyms, dentists, trades. Read five of their Google reviews. The words customers use are the words on the page. ‘They actually showed up’ is a better headline than ‘synergized cleaning solutions.’

Step 2. The named week on one PDF. Page plus five posts this week. Deposit today, files Friday. $800. Print ten copies. Phone PDF for the ones who will not take paper.

Step 3. A sample made from public info. Their dusty site, rewritten in a doc, SAMPLE across it. Then: ‘I can do yours properly with your facts.’ Do not pretend you already work there.

Step 4. Ten names, five visits or emails. Hallway, front desk, or the contact form they ignore. You are not a city-wide blast. You are a person on this street.

Step 5. Follow up the next day. One time, clearly. Owners are busy, not coy. If they say no, ask who else on the block would want Friday files. If they say later, put a date.

Step 6. Deposit. Kickoff. Money, then a 20-minute fact pull: hours, services, prices they will print, photos, phrases to ban, claims they must not make. Write the bans down.

Step 7. Draft with the model. Edit like you live here. AI can draft. They never see the raw pass. Cut fake urgency. Cut medical or chemical claims. Put the real cross-street in the copy. Put the real parking note.

Step 8. Deliver Friday. Folder they own. Page in a format they can paste. Five posts with the photo brief. Fifteen-minute walkthrough. Balance paid.

Step 9. Ask for a referral to the next owner on the same street. Same day. ‘If this saved you a headache, who else should I walk to before I go home?’

Step 10. Do not ghost. A text on Monday: ‘Any post you want swapped?’ One check-in. Then stop hovering. You are not their staff.

Step 11. Notes from the job become a playbook. The phrases, the claims you refused, the kickoff questions. That file can sell to cleaners in other towns. Later. After cash this week.

Step 12. Only then, a second week or a retainer. Same pack for the gym two doors down, or a monthly at a written rate. Not a 12-month contract sold on day one. Not a new trade on the other side of the city until this street has seen you deliver.

Worked example: Taylor walking gyms and cleaners

Taylor lives in a town where the highway gyms have new paint and the independent ones have a Facebook page last updated for a New Year promo. The cleaners are busier than they look. Taylor is not an agency. Taylor has a phone, a one-page PDF, and a week to fill.

The pack

‘Page plus five posts this week. Deposit today, files Friday. $800.’ In: one services or home page, five posts (caption plus what to photograph), one round of notes. Out: ads, logos, a new website builder. Sample attached: a rewrite of a cleaner’s public home page, SAMPLE watermark, with the fake claims stripped.

Taylor printed twelve PDFs at the library. Listed ten cleaners and eight gyms. Day 1 was the PDF. Days 2–4 were walking. Cleaners in the morning (owners actually in). Gyms at 10:30, between classes, not at 5pm.

The first yes

A two-van cleaning company. The owner had been meaning to ‘fix the site’ for a year. The sample looked like her shop instead of a national franchise. She asked if Taylor could also promise ‘hospital-grade’ in the copy. Taylor said no: ‘I will not write a claim you cannot stand behind. I will write that you are bonded, that move-outs take a day, and that you text an ETA. $800. 50% today.’ E-transfer on the van hood. Kickoff in the office: 18 minutes, a list of services, three photos from her camera roll, a ban list.

Taylor drafted that night. Edited the next morning — the model had added ‘eco-certified’ which they were not. Five posts: a before-and-after (with permission), a move-out checklist, a ‘what we will not do’ post that built trust, a hiring post for a third van, a Friday availability. Delivered Thursday night, walkthrough Friday. Asked for a referral before leaving. The owner named a gym two streets over whose manager also cleaned for her once.

What happened

Elapsed from first print to deposit: 6 days. Inside 3–14. Taylor walked the gym the same Friday with the cleaner as the intro, not as a secret. Second pack, same $800, gym language swapped in (‘drop-in,’ ‘freeze,’ ‘no contract shame’). Did not sell a 12-month retainer. Did not open a city-wide AI shop. Notes went into a doc that later became a $19 playbook for cleaners in other towns. The week’s cash was the $800. The playbook was the compound.

Copy you can paste

One-pager headline

Page plus five posts this week. Deposit today, files Friday. $800.

One-pager body

You get a services page written from your facts, plus five posts you can publish this week. I draft with AI. I edit every line before you see it. You get one round of notes. You do not get a 12-month contract, a new logo, or claims you cannot stand behind.

50% to start. Files Friday. Walkthrough Friday. Balance on delivery.

Walk-in line

I am not selling a 12-month thing. One page, five posts, this week, $800. The PDF is one page. Can I leave it with you?

Email

Hi [name] — I rewrote a sample from your public page (attached, marked sample). I can do yours properly with your facts this week: one page, five posts, $800, deposit today, files Friday. If the timing is wrong, ignore this. If a neighbor should see it, tell me who.

Kickoff questions

  • Hours, service list, anything you will not do
  • Prices that are allowed on a page
  • Claims I must not make
  • Three photos I can use
  • Phrases you hate
  • Where the page actually lives (WordPress, Squarespace, Facebook only)

Friday ask

If this saved you a headache, who else on this street should I walk to before I go home?

Week-by-week

Day 1: PDF. Sample page from public info. Ten names. Price on the paper.

Days 2–4: ten visits (or five visits and five emails). Follow up the next day. Do not redesign the offer.

Week 2: deliver and ask the next owner on the same street. Deposit, kickoff, draft, edit, Friday files. Referral the same day. If the first yes was a cleaner, the second walk is a gym or the next cleaner, not a dentist in another town.

Week 3: same pack, full price if you discounted. Write the SOP: kickoff questions, ban list, delivery folder template. A Monday check-in text, then you stop hovering.

Week 4: notes into a playbook draft for people not in your town, or a monthly PDF for the owner who already has Friday files. Do not sell the retainer to a stranger who has not seen you deliver. Do not hire two subcontractors and call yourself an agency.

The quality bar

You are the quality, not the model. If they can tell a chatbot wrote the page, you undercharged their trust and overcharged their Friday.

Edit like you live here

Real hours. Real cross-street. Real parking. Real name of the owner if they want it. No ‘revolutionizing the wellness space.’ No stock photo of a city that is not this one.

Claims

Gyms: no guaranteed body fat numbers. Cleaners: no medical kill-claims unless they have the certificate in a drawer and want it cited. Dentists (if you ever go there): you are not their clinical copywriter. When unsure, cut the line. Write what they do, when they do it, what it costs, how to book.

Delivery

A folder they own. File names a human can read. Posts in the order of the week. Photo briefs that a person with a phone can shoot in ten minutes (‘van in the driveway, 3pm light, no logo overlay’). A walkthrough. Then space.

Ghosting after the posts go up is a quality failure. So is dumping five raw AI captions into their Messenger at 11pm Thursday.

What to measure

The first sale is one local yes and a deposit. Measure movement toward that, then toward Friday.

  • Names on the list vs visits or emails this week. Ten and five is the 72-hour bar.
  • Follow-ups sent the next day. Zero follow-ups is how nice PDFs die on a counter.
  • Days from first walk to deposit. 3–14 is the window. At day 15 with no yes, the PDF is mush or you only visited friends.
  • Hours from kickoff to Friday files. If it took 18 hours of drafting, your edit pass is too precious or the kickoff missed the facts.
  • Referral asked, referral given. One name from a happy Friday is worth ten cold gyms.
  • Claims you refused. Log them. That log is how you stay out of trouble and how the playbook later teaches.

Do not measure ‘brand awareness on the street.’ Do not measure likes on their posts as your scorecard unless they paid you to run ads, which they did not.

Objections you will hear

‘My nephew does Facebook.’ Good. This is one page and five posts this week, then you are gone. Nephew can keep the page. You are not replacing their family. You are clearing a backlog.

‘Can you just show me a free sample on our account?’ No. Doing the work free as a trial is a mistake this path does not survive. The SAMPLE rewrite from public info is the sample. Deposit starts theirs.

‘We need to go viral.’ You are not promising that. You are promising Friday files. If they want ads, that is a different pack and a different number.

‘$800 is a lot.’ One yes covers the week. A cheap website someone never finished already cost them more. If $800 is wrong, $400 founder-rate with an expiry date is allowed once. $0 is not a price.

‘Put that we are the best in the city / hospital-grade / guaranteed results.’ No. You will not make claims the owner cannot legally make. Offer three true lines instead.

‘Can you start and we will pay Friday if we like it?’ No. Deposit today. Files Friday. Like-it is what the revision round is for, not what payroll is for.

What this path feeds next

Cash this week is the point. Compound is the notes.

The kickoff questions and the claims you refused become a $19 playbook for cleaners or gym owners you will never drive to. The five-post structure becomes a template you can sell. A local yes who is happy is a case study (with permission, real name, real town).

The same street becomes a retainer after one delivered week. The same pack becomes five videos instead of five captions if they have a phone and a doorway. Do not turn it into a city-wide agency. The next path is a product, not a headcount.

If you also make printables, the cleaner’s ‘move-out checklist’ can be a $9 page. If you also do long-form, ‘how independent gyms explain drop-ins’ is a video. Feed those after Friday, not instead of walking.

Mistakes that kill this path

Doing the work free as a ‘trial.’ They now have your Friday. You have a maybe.

Making claims the owner cannot legally make. You will both wear that copy.

Ghosting after the posts go up. The coffee-shop review writes itself.

Selling a 12-month retainer before you have delivered once. You do not know their approval speed. They do not know your Friday.

City-wide ‘we do all AI’ positioning. One trade, one pack, one street.

Starting the draft before the deposit. Starting a second trade in another town while five local names sit unvisited.

Letting them see raw model output. Your name is on the walk-in. The model does not walk in.

Tools

A one-page PDF of the package. Headline, in/out, Friday, $800, deposit, phone. Printed and on your phone.

Stripe or e-transfer. Whatever the owner already trusts. Invoice with the week’s date on it.

Your phone. Camera for samples, maps for the ten names, email, a notes app for the ban list. Five videos, if that is the pack, are also the phone.

A doc for drafts. You write. They receive a clean file, not a chat log.

The model, privately. Drafts only. Never a login you share with the owner as the product.

You do not need a CRM in week one. A list of ten in a notebook is a CRM. You do not need a team. You need shoes that can do four visits in an afternoon.

Checklist

  • One trade chosen, ten phrases learned from reviews
  • One-pager PDF: page plus five posts this week, $800, deposit today, files Friday
  • Sample rewrite from public info, marked SAMPLE
  • Ten names on a list
  • Five visits or emails
  • Follow-up the next day
  • Deposit before any draft
  • Kickoff facts and a written ban list for claims
  • AI draft edited by you; they never see the raw pass
  • Friday folder, walkthrough, balance
  • Referral ask: next owner on the same street
  • Monday check-in text, then no ghosting and no hovering
  • Notes saved for a later playbook
  • No 12-month retainer sold before one delivered week

FAQ

Gyms or cleaners first?

One trade first. Taylor walked cleaners in the morning because owners were on site, then used a referral into a gym. If you have a membership or a mop, start where you already know the words. Same PDF shape. Different nouns.

What if they have no website, only Facebook?

The ‘page’ is a Facebook services post or a Google Business profile rewrite. Name it on the PDF so they are not expecting a custom domain. Still $800 if the work is the same. If it is truly smaller, $400 and still a Friday.

Do I need insurance or an LLC?

A one-page agreement: deliverable, date, price, claims, who owns the copy (they do, on payment). Deposit. Local rules on business licenses vary. Do not let paperwork delay the first five visits. Do not skip the agreement.

Can I do this by email only?

Yes if you will actually send five and follow up. Walking still wins in small towns. Mix it. The path is ‘visit or email,’ not ‘wait to be discovered.’

What about dentists and medical trades?

Same pack, tighter ban list. You do not write clinical promises. If you are not willing to cut lines, skip that trade.

How do I handle an owner who wants endless rewrites?

One round, 24 hours, written notes. Then $150 for another round. Put it on the PDF. Unlimited is how Friday never comes.

Should I hire someone to walk for me?

No. Not until you have delivered three Fridays. The walk is how you learn the language. A hired body with your PDF is the agency you were told to leave alone.

Can the five posts be videos?

Yes if the PDF says so. Five simple phone videos, same $800 or $1,200 if you are also on camera. Do not surprise them with captions when they expected video, or the reverse.

What if the first ten say no?

Follow up once. Ask for a name. Walk the next ten in the same trade. Change the sample, not the whole business. Day 14 with zero deposits means the ask is fuzzy or you never mentioned the price on the paper.

When do I sell the playbook?

After one paid week, when the notes are real. The playbook is for owners you will not drive to. It does not replace the walk this month. Cash while digital compounds — in that order.

PATH 13

AI design and stock

A library, not a lottery ticket. One style, many variations.

16 minute read · 3,489 words

Who this is for
You can produce a coherent set of icons, patterns, or simple graphics.
First sale
A first payout, however small, that proves the account works.
Time to first
30–90 days to meaningful payout
Cash window
Slow then compounding
Tools
Your design tool, one marketplace, a license you actually read.
Leave this alone
One upload and waiting. Unlicensed copies. Raw unedited dumps if the marketplace forbids them.

ON THIS PAGE
