What this actually is

You already do AI work for money. Someone asks what you charge. You shrug an hourly number, or you write a custom proposal that eats an evening, or you say yes to a job that does not look like the last one because variety feels like growth. This path is the decision to stop reinventing the job.

Take the last job you did well. Write the steps as a checklist the night you deliver. After three similar jobs, the package writes itself. That package is path 7 — a named service with a date and a price. The checklist is path 1 — a playbook a stranger can run without you.

Charge the rate now. Build the product from the notes. You do not pause income to ‘work on the business.’ You take similar work on purpose, you write it down, and you send a package name the next time someone asks what you charge.

Variety feels like growth. It is usually stalling. AI made it faster. That is what they are buying. Do not undercharge because the draft arrived in twelve minutes. Speed is the offer, not a reason to discount.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if invoices already exist. You have done AI-assisted work for other people — posts, listings, research briefs, internal SOPs — and you can open a folder of paid jobs. You are tired of quoting from scratch. You may already be here.

It fits if you can tell the truth about which jobs were similar. Same kind of input, same kind of output, roughly the same number of days. A realtor listing pack and a dentist listing pack can be similar. A realtor listing pack and a custom chatbot are not.

Skip this if

Skip it if you have never been paid. You cannot productize a fantasy. Skip it if you refuse to write notes. The notes are the whole conversion. Skip it if you want to stop service this month and ‘only sell digital products.’ The honest road is three similar jobs, a named package, then a cheaper file of the job without you in the middle.

If you already run a productized package, this path is still yours: raise when the waitlist appears, and spin the checklist into a file. You are on step 9.

The offer, named in one sentence

When they ask your rate, name the package, not an hourly shrug.

The sentence: [Named result] in [N days] for [price]. You get [files]. One revision. You provide [inputs]. ‘I can help with AI’ is not an offer.

Offers that come from real invoices

  • 12 posts and one landing draft in 7 days for $600. You get files, not a login to my brain.
  • 8 listing descriptions plus a Google Business week in 5 days for $450.
  • Inbox to 15 follow-up emails in 10 days for $750, built in your voice.

You do not invent this on a branding retreat. You circle the similar invoices, write the shared steps, and name what you already delivered. The service package is you in the middle. The product version is the job without you, priced lower. Do not refuse paid work while you ‘wait to productize.’ Charge now. Notes tonight.

What done looks like

Done for the first 72 hours: last three invoices open, similar ones circled, shared steps written, package named, and that name sitting in a note you will paste the next time someone asks what you charge.

Done for the first sale of this path: a second similar job at a cleaner scope. Not a bigger job. A cleaner one. Same shape as the last, written scope, deposit, one revision, a date. If you still said ‘sure, I can also do the chatbot and the brand voice and the ads,’ you smiled. You did not productize.

A job is done when the night-of-delivery notes exist in the same doc you always use: steps, prompts you reused, what they failed to provide, how long it actually took, what you will refuse next time.

A package is done when you can send it without opening a blank proposal. Name, deliverable, timeline, price, inputs, revision rule. One page. The product version is later: the checklist a stranger can run without you. This path’s finish line is the named package and the habit of notes.

Pricing and the cash window

The cash window is your current rate, then a package. You do not cut your rate to ‘make it productized.’ You stop pretending every job is unique.

If you already charge $75/hour, and a job eats 8 hours including the smiling and the revisions, the package is not $200 because AI drafted fast. The package is $600–$900 with a date, or whatever math matches the last invoices plus a little for the cleaner scope. Speed is what they buy. You keep the surplus.

If you have been undercharging, do not leap to $5,000 because a thread told you to. Average the last three similar invoices. Round up to a number you can say without coughing. That is v1. Raise when a waitlist appears — meaning someone has to wait a week or you are turning people away.

First packages in this suite’s service path often live at $300–$1,500. A 3-day listing pack can be $400. A 10-day follow-up system can be $900. A 14-day content-plus-page can be $1,200. Write what is in and what is out so the number has a spine. Deposit before you open the chat. 50% to start.

When the checklist is boring because you have run it three times, price the file at $19–$47. Cheaper than hiring you. That is the point. It does not replace the $600 until you want it to.

The first 72 hours

You are not building a new brand. You are opening invoices.

Hours 1–3: circle

Open the last three invoices, or the last five if three is too thin. Circle the similar ones. Similar = same kind of input, same kind of output, same-ish days. If none are similar, circle the one you would be willing to do again, and treat the next yes as the second data point. Write the names of the jobs on a sticky: ‘listing packs,’ not ‘misc AI help.’

Hours 4–8: shared steps

From the circled jobs, write the shared steps in a single doc. Intake, inputs you needed, the AI draft pass, the edit pass, the delivery folder, the walkthrough, the revision window. Put the prompts you reused under the step where you used them. This doc is the company.

Day 2–3: name, one-pager, paste

Name the package like a result: ‘7-day listing pack.’ Write what’s in, what’s not, timeline, price (from the invoices, rounded up), what they must provide, one revision. Put the name in a note on your phone. The next time someone asks ‘what do you charge?’ you send the package, not an hourly shrug. If nobody is asking this week, send the one-pager to two past clients who already liked the work.

  • Last invoices open, similar ones circled
  • Shared steps in one doc
  • Package named, one page
  • Paste ready for the next ‘what do you charge?’

The path, step by step

Step 1. Open the last three invoices and circle the similar ones. If you cannot find similarity, you have been collecting gigs. The next yes should look like the best of those jobs, not a new species.

Step 2. Write the steps the night you deliver. Same day, while the mess is still in your head. Tomorrow you will only remember the win. The product lives in the missing inputs, the prompt that worked, the extra round they talked you into.

Step 3. Notes in the same doc after every job. One running file. Dated entries. Do not start a new Notion palace per client.

Step 4. Prompts you reused go in a stack. If you typed it twice, it belongs in a block you can paste. That stack becomes part of the package and, later, part of a file.

Step 5. Name the package. Result, days, price. Stop the hourly shrug. Practice saying it out loud once so you do not fold on a call.

Step 6. Write what’s in, what’s out, the date, the deposit. One revision is enough. Unlimited revisions is how packages die. They must provide voice, examples, and access on day one or the clock does not start.

Step 7. Send that name the next time someone asks what you charge. Even if the one-pager is ugly. A named package is cleaner than a charming email.

Step 8. Stop saying yes to work that does not teach the product. A flattering weird gig will pay once and steal the notes week. Refer it or decline.

Step 9. After three similar jobs, the package writes itself. You are not guessing scope. You have dates and hours. Tighten the one-pager. That package is path 7. The checklist is path 1.

Step 10. Raise the package price when the waitlist appears. Waitlist means delay or a no because you are full. Add 15–30%, keep the scope. Do not add more deliverables to justify the raise.

Step 11. Make the product version: the job without you in the middle. Price it lower. Let it sell while you sleep. Do this after the checklist is boring, not instead of the next paid job.

Step 12. Do not undercharge because AI made it fast. They bought speed and judgment. If a job that used to take a day now takes three hours, you did not become cheaper. You became a better package. Keep the price. Shrink the timeline if you want, on purpose, as part of the offer.

A worked example: Drew names the package

Drew has been ‘doing AI stuff’ for six months. Invoices: $400 to rewrite a dentist’s Google posts, $650 to draft listing copy for a realtor friend, $300 to ‘just help with ChatGPT’ for a cousin’s bakery, $800 for another realtor’s listings plus a landing page. Drew quotes from scratch every time.

Tuesday night, Drew circles the two realtor jobs and, after a pause, the dentist posts. Same shape: messy facts in, a week of public-facing copy out. The bakery cousin is not circled. Sticky note: ‘do not take cousin work.’

Shared steps go in one doc that night: intake, folder of inputs, draft pass, human edit, delivery in a Google doc they own, 15-minute walkthrough, one revision window of 5 days. Reused prompts get pasted under the draft step.

Wednesday, Drew names it: 8 listings and one page draft in 7 days for $750. Out: ads, chatbots, unlimited rounds. Deposit 50%. The one-pager is a Google doc. Ugly. Fine.

Thursday, an acquaintance asks what Drew would charge ‘to help with AI.’ Drew pastes the package name instead of ‘I usually do $75 an hour.’ The acquaintance is not a realtor. Drew says this package is for listings and a page. Two hours saved.

The next realtor who asks, week 2, gets the same paste. They say yes. Deposit lands before the chat. Drew delivers in 6 days, writes the notes that night (they always forget parking details; add that to intake). That is the first sale of this path: a second similar job at a cleaner scope.

After the third listing-pack job, Drew raises to $900 when a fourth has to wait a week. The checklist is boring enough to become a $27 playbook. The file sells twice in a month while Drew is inside a paid week. The hourly shrug is gone. The cousin still wants ‘a little help.’ Drew sends the playbook link.

Copy you can paste

When they ask what you charge

I do not bill a pile of hours for this anymore. It is a named package: [result] in [N] days for [price]. You get [files], one revision, and a short walkthrough. You provide [inputs] on day one. Deposit holds the week. Here is the one-pager.

One-pager headline

[Result] in [N] days. You get files, not a login to my brain.

Scope block

In: [deliverables]. Out: [the usual extras — extra rounds, extra channels, strategy decks, sitting in their Slack]. Timeline: [start when inputs arrive, end on date]. Price: [amount]. Deposit: 50% before I open the work. Revisions: one round within 5 days of delivery.

Notes template, the night you deliver

Date. Client type. What they provided / what they skipped. Steps I actually ran. Prompts I reused. Hours start to finish. What I will refuse next time. One line I could sell as a file.

Decline that teaches the product

This sounds like a custom build, and I am not taking those. If you want [the named package], I have a seat in [week]. If you want to run it without me, the [playbook / stack] is [price] and it is the same steps.

Raise, when they wait

The package is [new price] starting [date]. Same scope. I am booking [N] weeks out. Deposit still holds the slot.

Week-by-week

This week. Circle similar invoices. Write the shared steps. Name the package. Put the paste on your phone. Do not rebuild your website.

Next job. Send the package name. Take a deposit. Run the job. Notes in the same doc the night you deliver. Prompts you reused go in the stack.

After two similar jobs. Tighten what’s in and what’s out. If you got burned by extra rounds, the one-revision line gets bolder, not politer.

After three similar jobs. The package is real. Stop custom proposals for this shape of work. Start saying no to work that does not teach it. Sketch the product version from the checklist — but do not disappear from paid weeks to design a cover.

When a waitlist appears. Raise 15–30%. Keep the scope. Do not celebrate by adding deliverables.

When the checklist is boring. Ship the cheaper file. Mention it after delivery and to people who cannot afford the package. Keep taking packages until you choose not to.

Every week you deliver anything. Notes that night. If you skip the notes, you skipped the path. The invoice is the job. The notes are the company.

Quality bar

They never see the raw model output. That is the floor. AI drafts. You edit. Quality is your name, not the model’s.

Floor for a package job

  • Written scope before work. Deposit before chat.
  • Inputs listed. Clock starts when they arrive.
  • Human pass on every deliverable. Facts checked. Voice matches their examples, not ‘an AI that heard of their industry.’
  • Delivery in a folder they own. A 15-minute walkthrough. One revision window with a date.
  • Notes the night you deliver, in the same doc.

Floor for the conversion

  • Similarity is real, not branded. You can say in one sentence what matches across the circled jobs.
  • The package name is a result plus a date plus a price. If it needs a paragraph of hedging, it is still a shrug.
  • You declined at least one job that would have felt like growth and would have taught nothing.

Cleaner scope means the second similar job has fewer side quests than the first. Fewer extra rounds. Fewer ‘while you’re in there.’ If the second job is bigger and fuzzier, you went the wrong direction.

What to measure

  • Similar jobs in a row. Two is the first-sale line. Three writes the package. Randomness is the enemy; count streaks.
  • Proposal time. Minutes from ‘what do you charge’ to a sent package. If this is still two hours, you do not have a package. You have a ritual.
  • Hours per package vs price. Know the number. Raise or tighten scope if the surplus disappears.
  • Revision count. More than one as a habit means your definition of done is soft or your intake is weak.
  • Notes completed the night of delivery. Binary. This is the leading indicator of a future file.
  • Nos to off-shape work. If this is zero, you are still collecting gigs.
  • Waitlist days. When people wait, raise. When they do not, do not panic-discount. Improve the ask list.
  • Product-version sales, later. Separate from package revenue. Do not kill a $900 week because a $27 file feels more ‘scalable’ on a slide.

Do not measure how many different industries you have touched. That is a stalling trophy.

Objections you will hear — including from yourself

‘Every client is unique.’ Their business is unique. The job is not. Eight listings and a page is eight listings and a page. Unique belongs in their facts, not in your process.

‘I will productize after I quit clients.’ You will have no notes, no proof, and no cash. Charge now. Notes tonight. File later.

‘AI made it fast, so I should charge less.’ They are buying the speed. Keep the price. Offer a shorter timeline if you want the speed visible.

‘A package will make me miss interesting work.’ It will. That is the trade. Interesting work that never repeats is a class you pay for with your calendar. Take one on purpose once a quarter if you must.

‘I need a new website first.’ You need a one-pager and a paste.

‘What if they want hourly?’ You can say no. If a trusted client insists, cap the hours and still write the steps. Do not live there.

‘I only have one invoice.’ Circle that one. Aim the next yes at the same shape. The first sale is the second similar job.

What feeds this path

The last jobs. Invoices, emails, folders, Slack threads. Mine them before you invent a niche.

The night-of-delivery doc. Every entry feeds the one-pager and, later, the file. Skip a night and the feed dries up.

People who already paid you. They are the easiest second similar job. ‘If you need this again, it has a name now.’

The questions inside the job. ‘Can you also…’ is how you learn what to put in ‘what’s out.’ Intake gets sharper. The package gets kinder to you.

Prompts you reused. They are a stack waiting to happen. Do not leave them in chat history.

What does not feed it. Job boards that want a new species every time. ‘Happy to help with anything AI.’ Those feeds train the shrug. When a week has no freelance work, the feed is still the doc. Clean it. Send two past clients the one-pager. Do not fill the week with a random gig so you feel busy.

Mistakes

Random gigs forever. No notes. You will be as good at quoting from scratch in December as you were in March, only more tired. The notes are the path.

Custom work that never repeats. It feels like a portfolio. It is a stall. If it does not teach the package, it is a no.

Undercharging because AI ‘made it fast.’ Speed is the product. Keep the rate. Tighten the days if you want. Put the days on the package. Be done when you are done.

Working before the deposit. The ‘quick call’ becomes the job. Deposit first.

Unlimited revisions. Then there is no definition of done, and the checklist cannot be written because the job never ends.

Building the file instead of taking the second similar job. The file will be theoretical. Take the job. Write it down. Then productize.

A menu of 12 services. If you have twelve, you have none. One package. Not a cafeteria.

Tools

  • Whatever you already invoice with. Stripe, HoneyBook, an invoice PDF, Venmo with a paper trail. Do not switch billing tools this week.
  • A growing checklist in one doc. Google Doc, Notion, a notes app. One. Dated. Ugly.
  • A one-page agreement. Scope, date, price, deposit, one revision, what they provide. You can reuse this 20 times.
  • A deposit link. Same as the invoice tool. 50% before the chat.
  • The prompt stack you actually reused, sitting under the step where it belongs.

You do not need a new CRM to productize. You do not need a scheduler until people are actually booking. You do not need a course platform to turn the checklist into a $27 file — a PDF and a payment link will do when the time comes. The bottleneck is not software. It is writing the steps the night you deliver and sending a name instead of a shrug.

Checklist

This week

  • Last three invoices open. Similar ones circled.
  • Shared steps in one doc.
  • Package named: result, days, price.
  • What’s in / what’s out / deposit / one revision.
  • Paste saved for ‘what do you charge?’

Every job

  • Deposit before the chat. Clock starts when inputs arrive.
  • They never see raw model output.
  • Deliver in a folder they own. Walkthrough. One revision window.
  • Notes in the same doc the night you deliver.
  • Reused prompts copied into the stack.

After three similar jobs

  • Stop custom proposals for this shape.
  • No to work that does not teach the product.
  • Raise when people wait.
  • Outline the cheaper file from the boring checklist. Ship it without quitting the paid package.

FAQ

I only have messy one-off jobs. Can I still start?

Yes. Circle the one you would repeat. Make the next yes match it. The first sale of this path is the second similar job, not a perfect history.

What if I am not sure I want to keep doing service?

Keep doing it long enough to write the notes. Three similar jobs. Then the file can carry more of the weight. Quitting before the notes exist leaves you with neither cash nor a product.

How is this different from the productized service path?

That path assumes you can name the package this week and sell it. This path is for people already in the messy middle of freelance. You convert what you have. The destination — a named package — is the same room.

Should I put the package on a fancy page?

A one-pager is enough until strangers you do not know are buying. Past clients will read a Google doc. Do not stall on Webflow.

How many revisions is one?

One round of grouped notes inside a dated window, usually 5 days. Not a drip of ‘quick tweaks’ for a month. Put that in the one-pager.

What goes in the product version vs the package?

The product version is the steps, prompts, examples, and definition of done — the job without you. The package is you running those steps on their mess, with judgment and a date.

The client wants hourly and I am scared to lose them.

Name the package once. If they still want hourly, cap the hours and the extras, or let them go. A client who only exists when the scope is fog is not a client you can productize around.

When do I raise the price?

When someone waits, or when you are full, or when the notes show the job is heavier than the old invoices implied. Add 15–30%. Do not add three new deliverables so you feel polite.

Can I productize more than one job?

Later. One package until the paste is automatic and the notes are boring. A second package is how you end up quoting from scratch again.

AI did 80% of the draft. Is charging $750 honest?

If you edited, checked facts, matched voice, owned the result, and saved them a week in the blank window, yes. You are not selling keystrokes. You are selling a dated result they can use. If you are shipping unedited model text, fix the quality bar before you worry about the number.

PATH 17

Live drops of AI packs

A time, a file, a link in the bio. Scarcity must be real.

19 minute read · 4,196 words

Who this is for
You have a finished pack and a time you can go live without freezing.
First sale
A live session that produces at least one checkout.
Time to first
Same day as the live if the file is ready
Cash window
Burst sales of the $19–$47 file
Tools
TikTok Live, Instagram Live, Stan, a rehearsed first two minutes.
Leave this alone
Fake countdown timers. Going live with nothing to sell.

What a live drop actually is

A live drop is a date, a file, and a checkout. You announce a time. You go live for about twenty minutes. You show the pack on screen. People buy while you are still talking. That is the whole machine.

It is not a webinar. It is not a masterclass. It is not an hour of origin story before a slide that says “link in bio.” You are not teaching a course. You are holding up a finished thing and letting people take it.

Why live still converts a file A $29 pack on a quiet page asks the buyer to trust a stranger and a screenshot. A live drop lets them watch you open the file, click around, and answer one question out loud. Trust happens in public. That is why a plain pack can outsell a prettier one on a dead landing page — the live is the proof.

What scarcity is allowed to mean Scarcity is a date, a cohort, or a version. “I walk through this file Thursday at 7. After that you can still buy the pack on the page, but you miss the walkthrough.” That is honest. “Only three left” on a digital file you can copy forever is a lie. People who buy from you once will remember the lie.

A version is also real. “This is the April pack. The next rewrite ships in six weeks.” A cohort is real. “Twelve people get the live Q&A with this purchase. After twelve, the file is still for sale, the room is closed.” Pick one form of scarcity. Write it down. Keep it.

If the pack is not finished, you do not have a drop. You have a promise, and promises do not check out.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you already have a finished pack from an earlier path: a playbook, a skill pack, a prompt stack, a template bundle. You can talk for twenty minutes without freezing. You have one platform where at least a few dozen people already watch or follow you. You do not need a huge audience. You need a time, a file, and the nerve to press Go Live.

You can do this if - You have sold the file quietly, or you could, and you want a burst instead of a drip. - You can demo the product with your screen, not just describe it. - You can keep a Thursday. If you cancel lives, this path will embarrass you.

Skip this path if Skip it if the pack is not done. Skip it if you cannot be on a screen share without your voice leaving. Skip it if you only have a fake countdown and no real date. Skip it if you have never tested the checkout from a second email.

If you need cash this week and the file is ready, this is one of the fastest paths in the suite. If the file is not ready, finish path 1, 2, 3, or 6 first. Then come back and drop it.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this before you pick a thumbnail: “Thursday 7pm I show the [named pack]. Twenty minutes. Link in bio. $[price]. After the live the walkthrough is gone; the file stays on the page.”

That sentence is the offer. Everything else is decoration. If you cannot say it in one breath, you are stacking too many bonuses.

What sits in the sentence - A day and a clock time in the buyer’s timezone, or a timezone named out loud. - A file with a job in the title, not “my AI pack.” - A price they can hear once and remember: $19, $29, $39, or $47. - A reason to show up live that is not fake stock.

What does not sit in the sentence Do not add a 40-minute bonus training. Do not add a private podcast. Do not add “and I’ll also critique your account.” The drop is the pack plus the twenty-minute show. If you want a higher ticket, that is a different path. Keep this one boring and buyable.

What done looks like

Done is a live that produces at least one checkout from someone who is not you. The replay or the static page is up within two hours of ending. The link still works the next morning. You wrote down what people asked, what they bought, and what confused them.

The live itself, done You started within two minutes of the announced time. You showed the file in the first three minutes. You said the price and the URL out loud at least four times. Twenty minutes, maybe twenty-five if questions are real. Then you leave.

After the live, done - Checkout still delivers the file. - A thank-you comment or story: “Pack is on the page. Walkthrough was tonight.” - Notes: three questions you got, one thing you fumbled, the next date or the decision that there is no next date yet.

If zero people bought, it is still a finished live if you showed the product and the link worked. You do not declare the path dead after one quiet Thursday. You also do not go live again tomorrow to chase the feeling.

Pricing

The cash window is a burst of the $19–$47 file. Live does not magically make a $197 pack feel cheap. Live makes a small file feel urgent and clear.

The numbers that hold up - $19 if the pack is thin and this is your first public drop. - $29 if it is a complete job: prompts plus examples plus a one-page how-to. This is the default. Sage sells here. - $39 if you include a filled sample the buyer can copy. - $47 if the pack already sold quietly and you are using the live as a version event, not a rescue.

Do not discount live. “Live special $9” trains people to wait for you to panic. If you want a live-only extra, make it the walkthrough, not a cheaper file. The file price stays. The show expires.

You never run a fake “only 3 left at this price.” You never drop the price in minute eighteen because the room is small. Hold the number.

The first 72 hours

The first 72 hours assume the pack is already a file you can attach. If it is not, stop and finish the pack. Do not announce a live for a folder of notes.

Hour 1–4: freeze the product Export the final PDF or zip. Name the file like a human: “Realtor listing pack v1.pdf,” not “FINAL_final2.” Test-open it on your phone. Write the one-sentence offer. Pick the price. Confirm the Stripe or Stan checkout. Buy it yourself from a second email. If that fails, nothing else in this chapter matters.

Hour 5–8: the first two minutes on a card Write, on paper you can glance at: hello and how long you will be here; what the file does in one job-shaped sentence; share screen, open the first page, click two things; price and where the link is, said slowly.

Rehearse that out loud twice. Standing up. Do not memorize a speech for minute three through twenty. Human is allowed after the open. The open is not allowed to ramble.

Day 2: announce twice, not twelve Post the time once in the morning, once in the evening. Example: “Thursday 7pm. I show the file. Link in bio. Twenty minutes.” Put the checkout in the bio now. People who cannot attend still need a door.

Tell five people directly. “I’m walking through the listing pack Thursday at 7. Twenty minutes. $29. If that’s useful, I’ll drop the link when I go live.” Direct beats a story sticker that expires while they are at dinner.

Day 3: the live and the landing Hour before: water, charger, Do Not Disturb, link copied, screen ready. Go live on time. Demo. Repeat the link. End. Within two hours: a static page or a pinned post. The live is the event. The page is the store.

  • Finished file, named like a human
  • Checkout test-bought from a second email
  • First two minutes on a card, rehearsed twice
  • Two public announcements, five direct asks
  • Live of twenty minutes, then a store page the same night

The path, step by step

Do these in order. Skipping the checkout test is how you host a room and then apologize.

  1. Finish one pack. One file, one job, one price. Export, open on a phone, fix the page that looks broken at that size.
  2. Set a start time you can keep. Thursday 7pm is a classic. If you work evenings, pick Saturday 10am. Write the timezone. Lock the hour on a calendar.
  3. Build the checkout. Title matches the pack. Price is visible. File delivers. Test-buy. Watch the file arrive.
  4. Write the first two minutes. Card, not a script deck. Include the screen share in minute two, not minute twelve.
  5. Put the link in bio and in a note you can paste. If you fumble the URL on live, the moment dies. Have a short link written on paper.
  6. Announce twice in public and five times in private. Forty-eight hours out, then two hours out. The two-hour reminder fills a small room.
  7. Stage the desk. Charged device. A second device on the checkout. Notifications off except the platform you are on.
  8. Go live on time. If three people are there, you still start. Waiting ten minutes for a crowd teaches the three that you do not respect the clock.
  9. Demo, do not lecture. Open the file. Scroll. Fill one example live if you can do it in three minutes. Answer questions about the pack, not your life story.
  10. Repeat the link and the price out loud every three minutes. Pin it. Say it like a person: “Link is in the bio, it’s $29, it delivers as soon as you pay.”
  11. Close on purpose. At minute twenty, recap what they get, where to buy, and that you are ending. Stay two minutes for last questions. Then leave.
  12. Same night, stand up the store. Replay if you have one. Otherwise a static page: what the pack is, who it is for, price, buy button, “the live walkthrough was [date].” Write the three questions you got. Those become your next post.

A worked example: Sage on Thursday at 7

Sage finished a $29 pack on Tuesday night. It is a listing-description stack for solo realtors: twenty-five prompts, three before-and-after listings, a one-page install. PDF plus a short text file. Not a course. A pack.

The date is the product Sage picks Thursday 7pm Eastern. Twenty minutes. Instagram Live, because that is where the realtor acquaintances already watch Stories. Stan checkout already works — Sage test-bought it last month when the pack sat on a quiet page and sold two copies. Those two copies prove the file delivers. The live is for the rest.

Scarcity is the date. Sage writes: “I walk through the listing pack Thursday at 7. After that, the pack stays $29 on the page. The walkthrough does not.” No fake stock. No “only 5 left.” The version is v1. Sage will rewrite when realtors actually use it, not because a timer hit zero.

The card and the asks Tuesday, Sage writes the first two minutes on a yellow pad: “Hey — it’s Thursday, I’m here twenty minutes. This is the listing pack I use when a new property hits the MLS and I have twelve minutes before the client wants copy. I’ll share my screen, open the PDF, and run one address through the first prompt. Link is in the bio. $29. It delivers immediately.”

Sage rehearses that twice in the kitchen. Wednesday: morning Story and evening feed post, same core sentence. Five DMs to agents Sage has actually helped. One person asks for the link now. Sage sends Stan. That buyer pays $29 Wednesday night. The live is not the only door. It is the event.

Thursday 7pm Phone on a cheap tripod. Charger in. Do Not Disturb except Instagram. Stan open on a tablet. Bio already has the checkout. Pinned comment ready.

At 7:00 Sage goes live with eleven people. Not eleven thousand. Sage does not wait. The card gets through minute two. Screen share. A real listing Sage has permission to show, address blurred. First prompt. Ninety seconds of output. Sage reads two sentences, cuts the fluffy one, shows that edit. That is the demo.

Every three minutes: “Bio has the link. $29. You get the PDF tonight.” Someone asks ChatGPT or Claude. Sage answers: both, the install page says how. Someone asks for a discount. Sage says no, nicely. At minute nineteen Sage recaps and ends.

Four checkouts during the live. One more overnight. Wednesday’s early buyer makes six packs in two days. $174. Sage puts a static post up at 7:40. Friday Sage does not go live again.

Sage refused a countdown sticker that said “3 left.” Sage refused to stay live until 8:15 because a friend was driving home. Sage refused a $9 upsell on the fly. One file. A real date. Copy the shape, not the niche, unless you also write listings.

Copy you can actually send

Use these as they are. Swap the pack name, the price, the day. Do not add three paragraphs of your origin.

The announcement, public Thursday 7pm. I show the file. Link in bio. Twenty minutes.

Longer caption: Thursday 7pm Eastern. I walk through the [job] pack on my screen. Twenty minutes, then I leave. $29, instant download. After the live the walkthrough is over; the pack stays on the page.

The two-hour reminder Going live at 7. Twenty minutes. I show the pack, not a slide deck. Link will be in the bio the whole time.

The DM I’m walking through the [job] pack Thursday at 7. Twenty minutes, $29. If that’s useful, I’ll have the link in my bio when I go live. If not, no need to reply.

The pinned comment Pack is $[price] — link in bio — delivers the file as soon as you pay. This walkthrough is tonight only.

After the live The walkthrough was tonight. The pack is still $[price] on the page: [link]. Next live is not tomorrow. I’ll date the next one when there is a new version worth showing.

What you do not post Do not post “ONLY 2 SPOTS.” Do not post a fake timer. Do not write a 600-word caption that hides the time.

Week-by-week if you have a job

You do not need a free month. You need three evenings and a Thursday you will not cancel.

Week 1 Day 1: finish the pack. Export. Phone-check. Checkout. Test-buy. Write the one-sentence offer and the two-minute card.

Day 2: announce in the morning and the evening. Five DMs. Bio link live.

Day 3: go live at the time you promised. Twenty minutes. Static page the same night.

Days 4–7: do not drop again. Answer buyer questions. Fix the one page of the PDF that two people could not find. Ship a quiet v1.1 if it is a real fix.

Week 2 One post that teaches a piece of the pack without giving the whole file away. Soft link to the store page. Count sales from the page versus the live. If the live carried it, date the next drop four to six weeks out, not next Thursday.

Week 3–4 Only schedule a second live if you have a new version, a new sample, or a new audience. Repeating the same twenty minutes every week trains people to ignore you. A drop is an event because it is rare.

The quality bar

The bar is simple. A tired person who joins at minute four can still tell what the file is, what it costs, and where to buy it.

Before you go live - The file opens. The first page states the job. - The checkout works on a phone. - You can demo without hunting through eight folders. - The first two minutes are rehearsed. The rest can be human.

During You show the product more than you show your face talking about the product. You answer product questions. You do not fake a crowd. You do not read comments for ten minutes while the screen is your wallpaper.

After Buyers get the file. If someone pays and the delivery fails, you fix it before you sleep. If you cancel dates as a habit, this path will cost you more trust than it makes. Keep the calendar or pick another path.

What to count

Count a few numbers. Do not build a dashboard.

The only numbers that matter on drop day - Peak concurrent viewers. For a first drop, 8–40 is a real room. Do not compare this to people with a million followers. - Checkouts during the live plus two hours after. That is the burst. - Checkouts the rest of the week from the static page. That tells you if you have a store or only an event. - Questions asked more than once. That is the next caption, or a fix in the file.

What to ignore Ignore likes on the announcement. Ignore whether a stranger from a Facebook group said they would come. Ignore your heart rate. Ignore a view count that includes people who stayed for four seconds.

After three drops Write down: average live checkouts, average page checkouts in the following seven days, and whether you kept the date. If you cannot keep dates, stop this path. If live checkouts are zero three times and the page also sells zero, the problem is the pack or the audience, not the lighting.

Objections you will hear — including your own

“Nobody will show up.” Maybe. You still go live at the time you announced if even one person is there. A cancelled live teaches your small audience that your times are decoration.

“I hate being on camera.” Share the screen. Pin the product. You can be a voice and a cursor. Rehearse the two minutes so you are not staring at your own face looking for a personality.

“Live selling is slimy.” Fake stock is slimy. A date and a demo are a shop hours sign. You are allowed to say the price out loud. You are not allowed to invent a shortage.

“I’ll go live when I have more followers.” Eight people who already trust you will buy more than eight hundred cold viewers. Get the pack right. Date the drop.

“Someone asked for a discount on the live.” Say the price again. Offer the static page if they need to think. Do not negotiate in public. If you made a real error in the file, refund, do not haggle.

“What if they buy and want a course?” You sold a pack. Point them to the page. A setup session is path 20, priced separately, not a surprise at minute sixteen.

What this feeds — and what feeds it

Live drops are not a first path. They are an amplifier.

What must exist first You need a pack from path 1, 2, 3, 6, or 9. A live with nothing to sell is a talk show. Finish the file. Then drop it.

What the drop feeds - The static store. People who missed Thursday still need a button. - Your list, if you collect emails on the checkout. Do not build a 12-email sequence before you have a live that works. - Proof. A screen recording of you opening your own product is more useful than a designed ad.

What not to chain on night one Do not launch a membership from the live. Do not pitch an automation setup. Do not read a menu of twelve offers. One file. After the burst, you can point a buyer who asks for more toward a service path — in the DMs, not in minute twelve.

Mistakes that kill a drop

Lying about stock. Reading comments and never showing the product. Going live daily until nobody cares. Those three from the short version still kill it. Here is the rest.

Product mistakes Going live with a draft. Promising a zip you have not exported. Changing the price mid-live. Adding a second product because the room went quiet.

Performance mistakes Starting late. Waiting for “more people.” Lecturing for twelve minutes before the screen share. Fumbling the URL because it lived only in your head. Staying on until the last person leaves so the night feels like a hangout, not a shop.

Scarcity mistakes Fake countdown timers. “Only 3 left” on a PDF. “This price dies at midnight” when you will sell the same file at the same price on Saturday. Real dates only.

Cadence mistakes A live every day. A live that you cancel twice. A live with no store page afterward, so anyone who hears about it on Friday has nowhere to go.

If you catch yourself designing a talking-head intro sequence instead of testing the checkout, you have already slipped. Come back to the file and the time.

Tools — only what you need

You need a place to go live, a place to get paid, and a card for the first two minutes.

Live TikTok Live or Instagram Live. Pick the one where your people already are. Do not dual-stream a first drop. One room, one comment thread, one bio link. YouTube Live is fine if that is your actual home. Zoom is a meeting, not a drop, unless you already sell into a private list and sent a calendar hold.

Checkout Stan, Stripe Payment Link, or Gumroad. One product. The link must be short enough to say out loud. Test on cellular, not only wifi.

Staging A charger. A second screen or a tablet for orders. A notes app with the URL and the price. Optional: a $20 tripod so your demo is not a ceiling fan.

Do not add yet Do not add a webinar platform, a registration funnel, a wait-room video, or an on-air producer. Those are for people who already sell out rooms. You are dating a Thursday.

Checklist

Print this or copy it into a note. Check boxes in order.

  • Pack is exported and opens on a phone
  • Filename is human
  • One price, written: $19, $29, $39, or $47
  • Checkout test-bought from a second email; file arrived
  • One-sentence offer written
  • First two minutes on a card, rehearsed twice out loud
  • Day, clock time, and timezone on a calendar
  • Bio link live at least 24 hours before
  • Short URL pasted where you can pin it
  • Two public announcements: 48 hours out, 2 hours out
  • Five direct messages to people who already know the job
  • Device charged, Do Not Disturb on, second device on the order screen
  • Start on time even if the room is small
  • Screen share in the first three minutes
  • Price and link said every three minutes
  • End around minute twenty
  • Static page or pinned post up within two hours
  • Delivery issues fixed before you sleep
  • Three questions written down; no second live scheduled on adrenaline

FAQ

How long should I stay live? Twenty minutes. Twenty-five if questions are about the file. Not an hour.

What if only three people show up? You demo for three. You keep the date. You still put the store page up. Sage’s first useful room was eleven. Yours can be smaller.

TikTok or Instagram? Whichever already has your people. If both are empty, this is not your first path. Build a pack and a small audience on one platform, then drop.

Do I have to show my face? No. Show the file. A stable screen and a clear voice beat a nervous talking head that never shares the product.

Can I use a fake timer if “everyone does it”? No. Scarcity is a date, a cohort, or a version. Fake stock on a digital file is how you teach buyers to ignore you.

Should I go live every day to feed the algorithm? No. Date the next one when you have a reason: a new version, a new sample, a real cohort.

What if the checkout breaks during the live? Have the URL on paper. Have a backup payment link. If both fail, stop selling, take names, deliver by hand that night. Fix the pipe before you announce another Thursday.

Do I need a replay? Nice if the platform gives you one. Not required. The static page is required. The live is the event. The page is the store.

Can I drop a pack I did not finish because “the live will hold me accountable”? No. Finish the pack first. Accountability theater is how you go live with nothing to sell.

What should I charge if my pack already sells at $19 on the page? Keep $19, or use the live to introduce a $29 version that actually added examples. Do not raise the price only because you turned a camera on.

PATH 18

Research and brief service

A messy question in. A two-page brief out. You verify what the model cannot know.

16 minute read · 3,546 words

Who this is for
You can turn a pile of tabs into a decision someone can use.
First sale
One paid brief delivered on time.
Time to first
3–10 days
Cash window
$150–$600 per brief
Tools
Docs, a simple order form, sources you will and will not use.
Leave this alone
Claiming ‘AI knows everything.’ Publishing unreviewed facts.

ON THIS PAGE

What this actually is

Anyone can paste a question into a model. What they get back is a confident first draft with a unknown number of invented citations. You sell something narrower: a 48-hour brief. Two pages. What you found, what you verified, what is still unknown, and a recommended next step. One revision included.

The product is judgment. The model is a junior researcher who works fast and lies when bored. You are the person who says which numbers can stand in a meeting.

What a brief is not It is not a white paper. It is not a twelve-page literature review. It is not “I’ll go deep on your whole market.” Executives do not read twelve pages. Operators do not need a seminar. They need a decision, on a date, with the holes labeled.

Why two pages Two pages forces you to choose. If a finding cannot earn a sentence, it does not belong. The “do not know” section is not a cop-out. It is the part that makes the rest believable. People who have been burned by a hallucinated source will pay for someone who will write “I could not verify this” in plain type.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if people already ask you “what should I do about X?” and you already open twenty tabs before you answer. You can tell a primary source from a blog that copied a blog. You can say “I do not know” without feeling like you failed.

You can do this if - You have a niche where you already know the landscape: a trade, a software category, a local market, a regulation you have had to live with. - You can write two tight pages in a working day after the sweep. - You will check the citations. Not some of them. The ones you present as fact.

Skip this path if Skip it if you want to publish unreviewed model output under your name. Skip it if you freeze when a number cannot be confirmed. Skip it if you would rather build the whole project than recommend a next step. Scope creep into doing the work is how a $300 brief becomes a $3,000 job you forgot to price.

If you cannot yet name three people who already ask you for this kind of help, this is not your first path. Do a productized service or a playbook in a job you know, then offer briefs to the same people.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this on the order form: “48-hour brief. Two pages. What I verified, what I still do not know, next step. $[price]. One revision included.”

That is the whole offer. If you cannot fit it on a sticky note, you are selling a retainer.

What is in - Intake: the decision they must make, the deadline, what they already tried. - A sweep with a model. - Your verification pass against sources you named in advance. - Two pages delivered in 48 hours from a complete intake, not from the first vague email. - One revision window of 48 hours after delivery. Revision means clarify or correct, not “now also write the strategy deck.”

What is out Building the thing. Sitting in their Slack. Monitoring a topic for a month. Interviews you did not quote in the scope. If they need those, that is a different package with a different number.

What done looks like

Done is a two-page file the buyer can forward without you on the call. It has a recommended next step that a human could take this week. It has a “do not know” section that is specific, not theatrical. Sources that are stated as fact have been opened by you. The clock was kept.

The page-one test Hand the brief to someone who does not know the buyer. In three minutes they should be able to say: the decision, the recommendation, the biggest uncertainty. If they cannot, you wrote an essay.

Delivery, done - PDF or Doc they own, in their inbox, inside 48 hours of a complete intake. - Filename with the topic and the date, not “brief_final_v3.” - One revision if they ask inside the window. Then the job is closed unless they buy another brief.

Pricing

The cash window is $150–$600 per brief. Robin sells at $300. That is the number to copy until you have proof you are slow or uniquely sourced.

How to pick the number - $150 if this is your first paid brief, the topic is one you already know, and you need a case. Discount once. Then lock the fee. - $300 for a standard 48-hour, two-page, one-revision brief with named sources. Default. - $450–$600 if they need you to include a small set of primary documents they provide (filings, a competitor’s pricing page, a contract clause) and the decision is time-sensitive inside their week.

Do not charge hourly. A slow reader should not earn more than a fast one for the same two pages. Do not charge $50. That price buys a paste, and they will treat it like one.

Rush and extras Rush under 24 hours: add 50 percent, and only if you actually have the hours. A second topic is a second brief, not a longer first one. A monthly “keep an eye on this” is a retainer you should not sell until five briefs have landed on time.

The first 72 hours

You do not need a website. You need a template, a fee, and three people who already ask you what to do.

Hour 1–4: the brief template Open a two-page Doc with locked headings:

  1. Decision on the table
  2. What I found
  3. What I verified (with sources)
  4. What I do not know
  5. Recommended next step
  6. One thing I would not do

Write a one-paragraph sources policy under the template: sites you will use, sites you will not, and the rule that an unchecked number is not a fact. If you will not use Reddit as a source, say so. If a trade journal is in, say so.

Hour 5–8: the order form Five fields only: name, email, the decision they must make, the deadline, what they already tried. Price on the form. $300. Payment before you start the clock. 48 hours starts when intake is complete and the payment is in, whichever is later.

Day 2–3: three offers Send the sentence to three people who already lean on you. Not a cold list. “I turned the way I already help you into a 48-hour brief. Two pages. What I verified, what I still do not know, next step. $300. If you have a decision this month, I’ll take one.”

Deliver the first even if you discount it to $150 to get a case. Deliver on time. Then lock the price. A late free brief teaches the wrong lesson.

The path, step by step

Do these in order. The clock is part of the product.

  1. Write the two-page template and the sources policy. If you cannot name sources you refuse, you are not ready to charge for verification.
  2. Put $300 on a one-page offer. Intake fields, turnaround, one revision, what is out. Payment link above the fold.
  3. Ask three people who already ask you “what should I do about X?” Offer one discounted first seat if you need a sample, then never discount without a reason you can say out loud.
  4. On a yes: collect payment, then the intake. Do not start on a voice note. Incomplete intake does not start the 48 hours.
  5. Sweep with a model. Prompt it for a map: claims, names, numbers, competing views. Dump that into a working doc you will not send.
  6. Verify. Open the sources. Check the numbers. Kill anything you cannot confirm. Move the rest to “do not know” or cut it.
  7. Write two pages in your voice. Short sentences. The recommendation sits near the top, not after a tour of the landscape.
  8. Fill “do not know” with specifics: “I could not find 2025 market share from a primary filing. Vendor blogs claim 18 percent. Treat as unverified.”
  9. Deliver inside 48 hours as a file they own. Restate the revision window in the email.
  10. If they revise, you clarify or correct. You do not add a second question. If they want the second question, sell brief two.
  11. After delivery, ask one thing: which sentence they used. That sentence becomes your next outbound line.
  12. After three on-time briefs, raise from $150 (if you discounted) to $300, or from $300 to $450 if demand is real and your calendar is full. Do not raise because you feel fancy.

A worked example: Robin’s $300 brief

Robin helps independent clinic owners who keep asking whether they should buy a new scheduling tool. Robin is not a software vendor. Robin has sat through four of these decisions in two years and is tired of rewriting the same email.

The offer Robin sent Wednesday morning, Robin messages three clinic owners: “48-hour brief. Two pages. What I verified, what I still do not know, next step. $300. One revision. Clock starts when I have the decision, the deadline, and payment.”

One replies. Decision: stay on the current scheduler or move to Tool B before October 1, because two front-desk people just gave notice. They already tried a vendor demo and a Facebook group thread. They pay $300 Thursday at 9:12am. Intake is complete at 9:40. Clock ends Saturday 9:40am.

How Robin actually works Thursday, Robin uses a model to sweep: feature lists, pricing pages, two complaint patterns, HIPAA claims. That dump is ugly and stays private. Then Robin opens sources. Pricing is confirmed on both vendor pages, screenshots dated. A “HIPAA compliant” badge on Tool B’s homepage is marketing copy; Robin cannot find a BAA sample without a sales call, so that goes in “do not know.” A Reddit thread claiming Tool B lost appointments for a 200-provider group is one anonymous post — not a fact. Cut.

Robin writes two pages, not seven. Page one: the decision, the recommendation (stay through October, run a 14-day parallel on Tool B with one provider, do not cut over during staffing loss), the three verified differences that matter for a two-person front desk. Page two: sources, the “do not know” block, one thing Robin would not do (sign an annual on a sales call this week).

The “do not know” section, copied - Cannot verify Tool B’s uptime for 2025 from a primary status page; vendor says “99.9 percent” on a marketing URL only. - Cannot confirm BAA language without a sales call. If HIPAA is the reason to switch, that call is the next step, not a cutover. - No independent review of Tool B’s SMS reminders in this state. Vendor blog only.

Saturday 8:10am, the PDF lands. Filename: “Scheduler-stay-or-move_brief_18Apr.pdf.” Revision window until Monday 8:10. The owner asks one clarifying question: what “parallel” means in hours. Robin adds four sentences. Job closed. $300. Time on the clock: about five hours of real work, two of them verification.

Robin does not offer to implement the parallel. That would be a project. Robin notes the case, locks the fee at $300, and does not write a twelve-page version “to be thorough.”

Copy you can actually send

Use these. Swap the topic. Do not add a biography.

The outbound line 48-hour brief. Two pages. What I verified, what I still do not know, next step. $300.

The DM or email You keep asking me what to do about [X]. I turned that into a fixed job: 48-hour brief, two pages, sources checked, a “do not know” section so we do not pretend. $300, one revision. If you have a decision this month, I’ll take one.

The order-form headline A messy question in. A two-page brief out. Clock starts when intake and payment are both in.

The delivery email Attached: two-page brief on [decision]. I verified the items in section 3. Section 4 is what I still do not know — please do not treat those as facts. Recommended next step is on page one. One revision if you send it within 48 hours. After that, a new brief is a new order.

What you do not write “AI knows everything.” “Comprehensive report.” “I’ll keep looking until you are happy.” Those sentences are how you work for free and ship fiction.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1 Day 1: template plus sources policy plus $300 form. Days 2–3: three offers. Day 4–7: deliver one on time, even if the first is $150. Then lock the fee. Do not take a second brief until the first has landed.

Week 2 If the first buyer used a sentence, put that sentence in your next three asks. If they did not, ask what they needed that was missing — usually it is a clearer next step, not more pages. Ship that as a template change, not as unpaid extra work.

Week 3–4 Two more briefs at full price, or stop and pick a different path. Three on-time deliveries is enough proof. A monthly retainer is not the goal this month. Speed and a spine in the “do not know” section are the goal.

The quality bar

A brief is good when a skeptical reader can see the seam between verified and guessed.

Hard rules - If a number cannot be checked, it does not go in as fact. - If you will not use a source, say so in the policy, not after they complain. - Citations you did not open do not appear. Hallucinated citations are a firing offense you give yourself. - The recommendation is a verb plus an object plus a time: “Stay on Tool A through October 1. Run a 14-day parallel.” Not “consider exploring options.”

Length Two pages. A third page is a failure of editing unless they paid for a document dump they provided. Do not “add value” with appendices they will not read.

Voice Kitchen table. No hype. You may be wrong in the recommendation. You may not be fuzzy. Wrong and clear can be revised. Vague cannot.

What to count

Per brief - Hours from complete intake to send. Target: under 48, usually 4–8 hours of work inside that window. - Sources opened versus sources the model proposed. If you opened fewer than half, you are not verifying. - Whether they used the next step inside two weeks. A yes is a case. A no is a template problem or a bad-fit buyer.

After five briefs On-time rate. Aim for 5/5. One late brief is a process bug; three is you selling a clock you do not keep. Average fee. If you are still at $150 after five, you are discounting as a personality trait. Revision count. If every job uses the revision to add a second question, your intake is soft. Tighten the form.

Ignore how clever the model sounded in the sweep. Nobody paid for that dump.

Objections you will hear — including your own

“Can’t they just ask ChatGPT?” They can. They will still have to check it. You are selling the check, the cut, and a next step they can defend in a meeting. If they want the paste, they should not hire you.

“Two pages feels thin for $300.” Twelve pages they will not read is thinner. Price the decision, not the paper weight. Robin’s clinic owner forwarded one page-one paragraph to a partner. That is use.

“I might miss something.” You will. That is why “do not know” exists. Missing something and labeling it is professional. Missing something and stating it as fact is how this path dies.

“They want me to just hop on a call.” A call without a brief becomes the whole job, unpaid. Offer the brief. If they want a 20-minute walkthrough after delivery, price it as $75 or include once, in writing. Do not let the call replace the file.

“What if they want the whole project?” Say no, or sell a different package. A brief that turns into implementation without a new quote is how you train them to ignore your scope.

What this feeds — and what feeds it

Briefs sit next to other operator paths. They are not a funnel slide.

What feeds a brief practice People who already ask you for judgment: clients from a productized service, readers of a newsletter, buyers of a playbook who now have a live decision. Cold traffic can work later. First sales come from three names you can text.

What a brief feeds - A productized service, if the next step is work they want you to do. New quote. New scope. - A playbook, if three briefs repeat the same structure. The template becomes a $19–$47 file. - Custom project setups, if the recommendation is “configure the tool,” not “I will configure it this afternoon for free.” - Trust for a later retainer you still should not sell in week one.

Do not use a brief as a bait-and-switch into a $5,000 project they did not ask for. Recommend. Then wait to be asked.

Mistakes that kill this path

Hallucinated citations. Briefs that refuse to recommend. Scope creep into doing the whole project. Those three still kill it.

The rest - Starting the clock on a vague email. - Delivering twelve pages to look serious. - Leaving “do not know” empty because it felt like weakness. - Publishing the brief as a public post with the client’s numbers in it. - Claiming the model “knows everything.” - A second revision, then a third, because you did not close the window. - Undercharging at $49 so you feel like you can be sloppy.

If you catch yourself pasting a URL you did not open into a footnotes list, stop and delete the line. That is the whole ethic in one reflex.

Tools — only what you need

Writing and delivery Google Docs or Word. Export PDF. Their inbox. That is enough. Notion is optional and often slower.

Intake and pay A Tally or Google Form. Stripe Payment Link. $300. You do not need a proposal tool for a two-page product.

Research The model you already pay for, used as a sweep. A browser. The actual source pages. A folder of dated screenshots for numbers that might move. A simple rule list of sources you will and will not use.

Do not add yet Do not buy a “research agent” stack, a scraping farm, or a CRM. Three clients do not need a pipeline. They need you to open the links.

Checklist

  • Two-page template with the six headings, including “do not know”
  • Sources policy written: in, out, unchecked numbers are not facts
  • Offer sentence on one page: 48 hours, two pages, $300, one revision
  • Intake: decision, deadline, what they already tried
  • Payment before the clock
  • Clock starts only when intake is complete and payment is in
  • Model for sweep, private dump, never sent
  • Every fact opened at the source
  • Recommendation is a verb, an object, and a time
  • Filename has topic and date
  • Delivered inside 48 hours
  • Revision window restated in the email
  • Second question = second brief
  • After delivery: one sentence they used, written down
  • First discount, if any, is once. Then the fee is locked

FAQ

Why 48 hours and not a week? A week becomes a research project. 48 hours forces a decision-sized file. If they need a week of interviews, that is not this product.

What if intake arrives at 6pm Friday? State your working days on the form. “48 hours on business days” is allowed if you write it before they pay. Do not surprise them with Monday math after a Friday charge unless the form said so.

Do I show my working? No. They get two pages. The sweep dump stays yours. Showing the raw model output trains them to skip you next time.

Can I use the brief as public content? Not with their numbers, names, or quotes, unless they said yes in writing. You may rewrite a generic version weeks later if nothing identifying remains.

What if I cannot verify the key number? Put it in “do not know.” Change the recommendation so it does not depend on a fake number. That section is why they trust you.

Is $300 too high if I only spend four hours? No. They are buying the turnaround, the cut, and the nerve to recommend. If four hours feels guilty, look at the value of the decision, not your typing speed.

Should I offer unlimited email follow-up? No. One revision. Then a new brief or a different path.

What if they want citations in academic form? Give enough to reopen the source: title, publisher, date, URL. You are not submitting a journal article. You are making a decision defensible.

Can I do this without a niche? You can try. You will be slower and worse. Start where you already know which sources lie.

When do I raise the price? After three on-time briefs at the current fee, if you are turning work away or the decisions are heavier. Not after one compliment.

PATH 19

Automation setups

A named setup that still runs a week later. Not ‘I’ll automate your company.’

15 minute read · 3,368 words

Who this is for
You can connect a model to email, a sheet, or a form without setting their building on fire.
First sale
One paid setup that still runs a week later.
Time to first
7–21 days
Cash window
$400–$2,500 named setups
Tools
Make, Zapier, n8n, or native Claude/ChatGPT connectors.
Leave this alone
Open-ended retainers with no named job.

ON THIS PAGE

  1. 01 · What this actually is
