What this actually is

You sell a named machine. Lead form to draft reply. Weekly report to Slack. Invoice reminder that waits for a human. The name is the product. “I’ll automate your company” is how you vanish into their operations and never send a second invoice.

A setup is done when it still runs a week later without you in the Slack. You hand off a one-page runbook. You support it for 14 days. Then it is theirs, or they buy a new named package.

Why the name matters “Form to draft reply with your approval” is a thing a tired owner can say yes to. “AI operations overhaul” is a thing they will ghost. If you cannot write the before and after on one line, you do not have an offer. You have a hobby with logins.

Trust is the actual product If the machine can send mail without a human check, put a check in anyway until they ask to remove it. You are selling trust. The first time an automated message goes to the wrong person, they will not remember your cleverness. They will remember that you had no off switch.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you can connect a model to email, a sheet, or a form without setting their building on fire. You have built at least one of these for yourself. You can draw the flow on one page. You know what least privilege means, even if you learned it the embarrassing way.

You can do this if - You already use Make, Zapier, n8n, or a native Claude or ChatGPT connector for a real job, not a tutorial. - You can explain an off switch in two sentences. - You will write a runbook. People who hate documentation should pick a different path.

Skip this path if Skip it if you want an open-ended retainer with no named job. Skip it if you have never tested with fake data. Skip it if you need god-mode logins to feel useful. Skip it if the process has never been done manually — automating a fantasy workflow is how you spend three weeks debugging a job nobody actually does.

If you need cash this week, a brief or a productized service is faster. This path takes 7–21 days to a first paid setup because access, testing, and the runbook are the work.

The offer, named in one sentence

Write this before you open Make: “Named setup: form to draft reply with your approval. Runbook included. 14-day support. $750.”

Kai’s sentence is the default. Yours can swap the machine. Keep the shape.

What is in - One named flow, scoped to named tools, in writing. - A one-page diagram before you build. - Tests with fake data. - Human approval on anything that sends, posts, or charges, until they ask in writing to remove it. - A one-page runbook: how to pause, how to know it broke, who to ping during the 14 days. - Fourteen days of support after handoff. Then a new package if they want more.

What is out Rewiring their whole company. Unlimited extra zaps. Sitting in their inbox as a junior assistant. Keeping their passwords in your password manager forever. If they want a second machine, it is a second quote.

What done looks like

Done is a setup that still runs on day 8 without you poking it. The client can pause it without calling you. The runbook is in a folder they own. Your access is the least they needed, and you have a calendar reminder to remove yourself after the support window if they do not need you.

The day-8 test You do not log in “just to check.” They send you a screenshot or a sample output from the week. If it failed silently, the runbook failed. You fix that inside the 14 days as support, then you write the failure into the runbook so it does not stay tribal knowledge.

Handoff, done - Diagram, runbook, and tool logins they control. - Off switch labeled in the first paragraph of the runbook. - Support end date in writing. Not “I’m around.”

Pricing

The cash window is $400–$2,500 for named setups. Kai charges $750 for form-to-draft-reply with approval, runbook, and 14-day support. Copy that until you have a reason not to.

How to pick the number - $400–$500 if the flow is one trigger, one model call, one destination, and you already built it for yourself. First paid job only. Then move up. - $750 for the default: form or inbox in, draft out, human approval, runbook, 14 days. This is Kai. This is you unless the tools are uglier. - $1,200–$2,500 if there are three tools, a messy existing sheet, or a compliance reason you have to move slowly. Still one named machine. Not a department.

What you do not sell Hourly “I’ll hop in the workspace.” A retainer that says “automations as needed.” A $99 special that cannot pay for the runbook. Support after day 14 is a new package at a written rate, or they own the risk. Do not leave yourself on the hook because it feels kind.

The first 72 hours

Do not hunt a client on day one. Demo on your own accounts first.

Hour 1–6: pick one setup Form to draft reply is the cleanest first machine. You already understand email. The approval step is obvious. Build it on your own form, your own inbox, a dummy customer name like “Test River.” Record your screen for eight minutes: form submit, draft appears, you approve, mail would send. Stop the recording before you philosophize.

Hour 7–10: price and a one-pager Write the sentence. $750. Tools named: for example “Typeform or Tally, Gmail or Outlook, Claude or ChatGPT, Make or Zapier.” One page: in, out, approval, runbook, 14 days, what is not included. Payment: 50 percent to start, 50 percent on handoff.

Day 2–3: two offers Send it to two people with messy inboxes. Not ten. Two who have complained in your hearing. “You said the lead form dumps into a void. I set up form-to-draft-reply with your approval. Runbook included. 14-day support. $750. I can demo on my accounts this week.”

If they want a different machine, you may swap the name only if you can demo that one too. Do not invent a custom empire because they sounded excited.

The path, step by step

  1. Name the machine in one line. If it needs a paragraph, split it into two products and sell one.
  2. Diagram on one page before you build. Trigger, tools, approval, destination, off switch. If you cannot draw the off switch, do not collect a deposit.
  3. Scope the tools in writing. “Make plus Gmail plus Claude” is a scope. “Whatever we need” is how invoices die.
  4. Take a deposit. Then request access. Least privilege: the form, the one inbox or label, the one sheet. Not their Google admin.
  5. Build in a test environment. Fake names, fake emails you own, a dummy Slack channel. Never test by mailing their real customers.
  6. Put a human check on anything that sends, posts, or charges. They can ask to remove it later, in writing, after they have seen a week of drafts.
  7. Write the runbook: how to pause, how to know it broke, where the logs live, what “looks wrong” means in one example, who you are until day 14.
  8. Walk them through it live, twenty minutes, not a lecture. They pause it once with you watching. If they cannot pause it, you are not done.
  9. Handoff in a folder they own. Invoice the rest. Calendar the 14-day end date.
  10. Support for 14 days is break-fix and small clarifications, not new features. A new branch in the flow is a new quote.
  11. Day 8: they confirm it ran. Day 14: you ask if they want a second named setup. If not, remove extra access you no longer need.
  12. Only then write an SOP for yourself so the second client is faster. Do not productize a setup you have never kept alive for a week.

A worked example: Kai’s $750 form-to-draft

Kai helps a three-person studio that takes project inquiries through a Tally form. The form currently emails everyone, and replies happen two days later, or not. The owner asked Kai to “put AI on our intake.” Kai refused that sentence and sold a named setup instead.

The one-pager Kai sent Named setup: form to draft reply with your approval. Tally to Gmail draft (not send) via Make. Claude writes the draft from a 12-line studio voice doc. You click Send. Runbook included. 14-day support. $750. Half to start.

The owner pays $375. Kai asks for: Tally editor access, a Gmail label “Leads-drafts,” and a shared Doc with three good replies they liked last year. Not the whole Google org. Not the client’s personal inbox.

Build week Monday: diagram. One page. Form submit → Make → Claude (voice doc + form fields) → Gmail draft in the label → human sends. Off switch: a Make scenario toggle, circled in red, screenshot in the runbook.

Tuesday: fake data. Kai submits “River Test / never-use-this@kai.example” four times. First drafts are too long. Voice doc gets a line: “Six sentences max. No ‘excited to potentially explore.’” Third test is boring and correct. That is the goal.

Wednesday: walkthrough, twenty minutes. Owner pauses the scenario and unpauses it. Kai watches. Owner files a real inquiry from a friend’s form. Draft lands. Owner edits one sentence and sends. Kai invoices the remaining $375.

The runbook, what it actually says How to pause: Make, scenario “Lead drafts,” toggle off. Screenshot. How to know it broke: no draft in the label within ten minutes of a test submit. How to test: a form password Kai set, used only for tests. Who to ping until [date 14 days out]. What Kai will not do after that date without a new package: add Slack, add a second language, wire it to the CRM.

Day 8 and day 14 Day 8 the owner forwards three drafts that went out. One was too stiff; Kai tightens the voice doc inside support, not as a new product. Day 14 Kai removes Tally access, leaves the owner as Make admin, and asks if they want invoice reminders as a second named setup at $750. Owner says later. Kai does not stay in the workspace “in case.” The machine still runs. That is the sale.

Copy you can actually send

The one-liner Named setup: form to draft reply with your approval. Runbook included. 14-day support.

The ask You said the lead form dumps into a void. I set up form-to-draft-reply with your approval so nothing sends until you tap it. Runbook included. 14-day support. $750. I can demo on my accounts this week.

The scope line, paste into the invoice In: one form, one draft destination, one model, human approval, one-page runbook, 14 days of break-fix. Out: extra tools, extra flows, sending without approval, retainers.

The runbook title How to pause [machine name]. How to know it broke. Support ends [date].

What you do not write “I’ll automate your ops.” “Unlimited zaps.” “Set and forget forever.” “I just need admin access to everything.” Those sentences are how this path becomes a second unpaid job.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1 Demo on your own accounts. Record it. Write the one-pager at $750. Offer it to two people with messy inboxes. Do not skip the off switch on your own demo. If you cannot pause your own scenario, you have nothing to sell.

Week 2 First paid setup. Diagram before you build. Fake data. Least privilege. Runbook. Twenty-minute walkthrough. Calendar day 8 and day 14. Do not start a second client in the same week unless the first machine is already drafting.

Week 3 Support window. Break-fix only. Write down every change you made; that list is either a runbook update or a quote for setup two. Remove extra access at day 14.

Week 4 If the first machine still runs, you may offer a second named setup to the same person or repeat the offer to two more. If it does not run, you do not sell a second one. You fix your process.

The quality bar

The bar is a machine a non-technical owner can pause, and a week of drafts that did not embarrass them.

Before you call it shipped - Diagram exists and matches what you built. - Fake-data tests are saved. - Approval is on. - Runbook is one page, in their folder, with screenshots of the off switch. - They paused it once in front of you.

After Silent failure is a bug in your product, not a quirk of Zapier. If the flow can die without anyone noticing, add a simple alert: an email to them when a run errors, or a daily count in a sheet. Fancy observability is optional. “We would know by tomorrow” is not optional.

Do not ship a clever branch you cannot explain. Clever is how you get a 11pm text in week three.

What to count

Per setup - Did it run on day 8 without you? Yes or no. This is the first sale definition. - How many real drafts or actions in week one. Zero means the trigger is wrong or they stopped feeding the form. - Support touches in 14 days. More than five usually means the runbook is thin or you sold a process they do not actually run.

After three setups On-time handoff rate. Machines still running at day 30 — ask once, do not babysit. Average fee. If you are stuck at $400 after three, you are discounting the runbook away.

Ignore how many modules the scenario uses. Clients do not buy module counts. They buy a named job that does not catch fire.

Objections you will hear — including your own

“Can’t they just turn on ChatGPT in the inbox?” They can turn on a lot of things. They hired you so it drafts in their voice, waits for approval, and can be paused. Native buttons without a runbook are how a junior sends a wrong quote at 6am.

“$750 feels high for a zap.” They are not buying a zap. They are buying design, least privilege, tests, a runbook, and two weeks of you on the hook. A $20/month tool with no owner is cheaper and worse.

“They want it to send automatically.” Not on day one. Run a week of approvals. If they still want send-without-check, get it in writing, add a holdout (never mail a list they did not just submit), and keep the off switch obvious.

“I need admin access to do it right.” You need the least access that makes the named machine work. Admin access is how you become the person who “also” resets everyone’s 2FA. Refuse god-mode.

“What if it breaks after day 14?” They have the runbook. They can pay for a new package. You may sell a $150 check-in. You may not pretend you are on call forever because you feel responsible for software they own.

What this feeds — and what feeds it

What feeds a first automation job A productized service client who is drowning in a form. A local business you already wrote posts for. Yourself — the demo on your accounts is the sales asset. Cold “automation agency” ads are not how a kitchen-table operator gets job one.

What a living setup feeds - A second named machine for the same client, quoted separately. - A custom project setup (path 20) if they also need the model configured, not just the pipes. - Notes that become a playbook or a template for a narrower audience, later, after you have done it three times. - Definitely not an open retainer in week two.

Keep the machine named. The minute you become “our automation person,” you are on a salary they forget to pay.

Mistakes that kill this path

God-mode logins. No off switch. Automating a process that has never been done manually. Leaving secrets in a shared doc. Those still kill it.

The rest - Starting to build before the diagram and the deposit. - Testing on real customers. - Skipping the approval step to look advanced. - A runbook that is a 19-page novel. One page. Screenshots of the toggle. - Support that quietly becomes new features. - Staying in their workspace after day 14 “just in case.” - Quoting one price for “all the zaps they might want.”

If a password lives in a Google Doc titled “logins,” you have already failed the quality bar. Use a password manager they own, or sit with them while they type it.

Tools — only what you need

Pick one automation tool and get good at it. Make, Zapier, or n8n. Native Claude or ChatGPT connectors are enough for some jobs and better when the client already pays for them.

The rest of the stack A form (Tally, Typeform, native). An email box or Slack channel they already live in. A Doc for voice and the runbook. Loom or a phone recording for the walkthrough. Stripe for the deposit.

Access Least privilege. Shared folders they own. No personal Gmail of yours in the middle of their lead flow — if you leave, the machine should not.

Do not add yet Do not sell them a new CRM as a prerequisite. Do not rebuild their website to “capture leads properly” unless they paid for that as a different job. The named setup uses what they already click.

Checklist

  • Machine named in one line
  • One-page diagram includes the off switch
  • Tools listed in the quote
  • 50 percent deposit before access
  • Least privilege, not admin
  • Fake data tests saved
  • Human approval on send/post/charge
  • Voice or rules doc they can edit
  • Runbook: pause, broken, logs, support end date
  • They pause it once in front of you
  • Handoff folder they own
  • Day 8 check: it still ran
  • Day 14: extra access removed
  • New features = new quote
  • No secrets in a shared Doc

FAQ

Make, Zapier, or n8n? Whichever you can demo this week on your own accounts. Do not learn a new tool on their dime. If they already pay for one, prefer that.

What if they do not have a form? Then the named setup is not form-to-draft. Sell a different named machine, or a smaller job to stand up a Tally form as part of the $750 if you write it in the scope. Do not wander.

Do I include the Zapier bill in my fee? No. They pay their tool bills. You say that on the one-pager. Your $750 is design, build, runbook, 14 days.

Can I keep access so I can “watch it”? Not after day 14 unless they buy a check-in package. Watching it is how you become unpaid ops.

What if the model writes a bad draft? That is why approval exists. Tighten the voice doc inside the 14 days. If they want a whole new tone for a second brand, that is a new quote.

Is $750 too much for a first client who is a friend? Charge $500 once if you need a case, then $750. Free is how friends get a machine you cannot afford to support.

Should I sell a monthly retainer? Not until several named setups have survived day 30. Even then, name the hours and the machines. “Retainer for automations” is the leave-this-alone line.

What if they want Slack, email, and the CRM in week one? That is three machines or a $2,500 scope with a written diagram. Default to one flow. You can stack later.

How do I handle API keys? In their account. Not in yours. Not in a chat log. If a key leaked into a screenshot, rotate it before you go to bed.

When is the first sale “real”? When the setup still runs a week later. A demo on your laptop is not a first sale. Kai’s first sale was the studio’s drafts on day 8.

PATH 20

Custom project setups

They want Claude Projects or Custom GPTs configured, not a lecture.

14 minute read · 3,186 words

Who this is for
Someone drowning in a blank project who will pay for a working one.
First sale
One setup a stranger can use without you.
Time to first
3–10 days
Cash window
$97–$297 per setup
Tools
Claude Projects or Custom GPTs, a 5-minute walkthrough recording.
Leave this alone
Selling access to a model you do not own.

What they actually buy

They buy instructions, files, and three starter chats that already work. You intake their actual job. You install it in an account they own. You test it. You record a five-minute walkthrough. You leave. They never need your login.

You are not reselling Claude. You are not reselling ChatGPT. You are selling configuration. Say that out loud so nobody is confused, including you. If your business requires them to keep using a project inside your account, you have built a hostage, not a product.

What “configured” means A blank project is a blinking cursor with extra steps. A configured one knows the job, the tone, the files it may use, and the three first prompts that produce something they recognize. The buyer should be able to open it on Thursday night and get a usable draft without writing a system prompt from scratch.

What it is not It is not a course on prompt engineering. It is not a second brain with forty folders they will never open. It is not you wrapping someone else’s model in a thin login screen and charging rent. Anthropic and OpenAI already sell the model. You sell the setup.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you have already made a project or a custom GPT for yourself that you actually reuse. People around you have said some version of “I never know what to put in the custom instructions.” You can sit in someone else’s job for an hour without needing to become their employee.

You can do this if - You can write instructions a tired person can read, not a wall of role-play. - You will hand the project over. Your name does not need to stay in the admin field. - You can record five minutes on a screen without turning it into a webinar.

Skip this path if Skip it if you planned to sell access to a model you do not own. Skip it if you want to keep their files in your account so they “have to stay.” Skip it if you will load confidential client files into a model against their policy because it is easier. Skip it if you have never finished a project for your own job — do that first, then sell the shape.

If you freeze at the idea of asking five people, this still works, but the first 72 hours will feel like the whole job. That is normal. The page and the five asks are the path.

The offer, named in one sentence

“I configure the project for your job. You own it. Walkthrough included. $197.”

Jules uses that sentence. Keep it. The number can move inside $97–$297. The ownership cannot.

What is in - A 20-minute intake: the jobs, the files they are allowed to add, the tone. - Custom instructions they can read and edit. - Up to a small set of files they provide and approve. - Three starter threads that already return a decent first draft. - A five-minute walkthrough recording. - One tweak window of seven days. Tweak means instructions and starters, not a second job.

What is out Your Claude login. A monthly “I host this for you.” Training their whole staff. Building a forty-file knowledge base they will not maintain. Connecting ten tools. If they want pipes into email, that is path 19, quoted separately.

What done looks like

Done is a setup a stranger can use without you. They open their own project. They click starter one. They get a draft they could send after a human edit. They know how to add a file and how to tweak a line of instructions because the walkthrough showed both.

The hand-off test Log out. Have them log in. Watch them run starter two without you typing. If they cannot, the walkthrough failed or the starters are cute instead of useful. Fix it inside the tweak window.

Ownership, done The project lives in their account. You are not the owner. If they want you as an invited viewer for seven days, fine. On day eight you leave. Files they consider confidential follow their policy, not your curiosity.

Pricing

The cash window is $97–$297 per setup. Jules charges $197. That is the default button.

How to pick the number - $97 if you need the first case and the job is one you could configure in an hour. Once. - $197 for a standard job: intake, instructions, three starters, five-minute walkthrough, one tweak window. Copy Jules. - $297 if they have several file types, a tone that is actually picky, or two related jobs in one project (for example “listing draft” and “email follow-up”) still inside one setup.

Do not charge $20. That is a prompt, not a configuration. Do not charge $1,000 unless you are also doing automations and a staff workshop, which is a different quote with a different name.

Hosting is not a price If they ask you to keep it in your account and “just give them access,” the answer is no. That is selling access to a model you do not own, plus a liability you cannot explain to a lawyer. Configure theirs. Leave.

The first 72 hours

Day 1: a sample for a job you know Build a sample project as if you were the client. Pick a job you have done: weekly posts, listing copy, invoice reminders, a study plan. Write instructions in plain language. Add one sample file you are allowed to use. Create three starter chats that already work. Record a five-minute walkthrough: open, pick a starter, add a file, change one instruction line. Do not record forty minutes.

Day 2: the $197 button One page. Sentence at the top. What they get. What they must provide (job, tone samples, files they are allowed to upload). What they own. Stripe or Stan at $197. Buy it yourself from a second email so you know the confirmation works even if the “product” is a scheduled setup, not a zip.

Days 3–5: five asks Ask five people who complained about custom instructions. Direct messages, not a launch. “I configure the project for your job. You own it. Walkthrough included. $197. If you want one, I’ll send the intake.” Do not explain five other services. Hand off so they never need your login.

The path, step by step

  1. Pick the platform they already pay for. Claude Project or Custom GPT, not both, not a new app you invented.
  2. Intake: the jobs (one primary), the files they are allowed to add, the tone in three samples they like, and any policy about data. Write it down. If they cannot name a job, sell them a cheaper pack instead.
  3. Create the project in their account, or create it in yours only as a draft and copy the text over while they watch. They click the buttons. They own it.
  4. Write instructions they can read. Role, inputs, output shape, what to ask if information is missing, what never to invent. Short. They should be able to edit a line without breaking the rest.
  5. Add only the files they approved. Do not scrape their Drive. Do not upload a client list they whispered about.
  6. Build three starter threads that already work: the three jobs they will actually open this week. Name the threads in human language.
  7. Test with a real example they provided. If the output is fluff, cut the instructions, do not add a fourth file.
  8. Record a five-minute walkthrough: where it lives, how to run a starter, how to add a file, how to tweak one instruction, who owns it.
  9. Hand off. Confirm they can run it without you. Start the seven-day tweak window in writing.
  10. One tweak, if they ask. Not a redesign. Not a second project.
  11. Leave their account. Delete any copies of their files that landed on your machine.
  12. After the first stranger uses it without you, write the intake as a form so job two is faster. Do not add a membership around a setup you have done once.

A worked example: Jules at $197

Jules keeps hearing the same sentence from independent bookkeepers: “I opened a Claude Project and then I just… stared at it.” Jules is not selling bookkeeping. Jules is selling a configured project for client follow-up emails.

The sample, built on Monday Jules makes a project titled “Follow-up emails — sample.” Instructions: you write collection and scheduling emails for a solo bookkeeper; you never invent a balance; you ask for the amount if it is missing; tone is plain, no “just circling back” stacks; max eight sentences. One sample file: a fake aged-receivables list with made-up names. Three starters: (1) 14-day reminder, (2) 30-day firmer note, (3) scheduling next month’s close. Jules records five minutes. The $197 button goes on a page Tuesday.

The five asks Jules messages five bookkeepers who already complained. Two ignore. One says “I don’t pay for Claude.” Jules does not argue. One wants Jules to “just write the emails each week.” That is a service, path 7, different number. One says yes: Mara, two days a week in her own books, already pays for Claude, hates the blank project.

Intake, Wednesday, twenty minutes Jobs: follow-up on unpaid invoices, and a short “what I need from you for month-end” note. Files Mara is allowed to add: her template phrases, not her real client spreadsheet. Policy: no client names in the project until Mara pastes them per chat. Tone: Mara forwards three emails she was proud of. Jules writes instructions from those, not from a persona Jules invented.

Install, in Mara’s account Screen share. Mara creates the project. Jules dictates, Mara pastes. Three starter chats Jules types while Mara watches, then Mara re-runs starter one on a fake invoice. It asks for the amount because Jules left it out on purpose. That is the quality check. Walkthrough recording is the same five minutes Jules already had, plus a 40-second ending: “this is yours, I’m not in the account.”

Mara pays $197. Tweak window through the next Wednesday. She asks to soften starter two. Jules changes three lines. Jules deletes the fake sample file from Jules’s machine. Mara owns the project. Jules does not keep a copy “for the portfolio” with Mara’s phrases in it.

Copy you can actually send

The line I configure the project for your job. You own it. Walkthrough included.

The DM You said you never know what to put in the custom instructions. I’ll set up the project in your account for [job], with three starter chats that already work, and a five-minute walkthrough. $197. You own it. I don’t keep a login.

The page headline A working Claude Project (or Custom GPT) for one job. Not a lecture. Not my account.

The intake ask Send: the job, three examples of a tone you like, and any files you are allowed to upload. Do not send confidential files you would not want in the model.

After hand-off It’s in your account. Starters are named. Walkthrough is here: [link]. You have seven days for one tweak on instructions or starters. After that, a new job is a new setup.

What you do not write “Access my GPT, $19/month.” “I’ll host your brain.” “Upload everything you have.” Those are the leave-this-alone line and a policy problem.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1 Day 1: sample project for a job you know, plus the five-minute walkthrough. Day 2: page with a $197 button. Days 3–5: five asks. Do not design a catalog of twelve industries. Hand off so they never need your login.

Week 2 Deliver the first paid setup. Keep the tweak window. Delete their files from your machine. Write the intake as a form. Do not start a “hosted GPT” idea because someone asked.

Week 3–4 Two more asks per week, or stop. If nobody buys, the job is too vague or you asked strangers who do not already pay for the model. Sell to people who already have the subscription. You are not also selling them Claude.

The quality bar

The bar is use-without-you. Pretty instructions that nobody runs are a brochure.

Instructions Readable. Editable. No 2,000-word role-play. A missing-info rule. A do-not-invent rule. Output shape in a few lines.

Starters Three, named for jobs, already producing a draft a human would edit, not a manifesto. If starter one needs a paragraph of extra prompting, you failed the configuration.

Files Only what they approved. Dated. Small. A second brain they will never open is a mistake, not a bonus.

Walkthrough Five minutes. If you need twelve, you built too much. Show ownership. Show the off-ramp: how they edit you out of the instructions later.

What to count

Per setup - Can they run a starter without you in the room? That is the first-sale definition. - Tweak requests that are actually a second job. Those mean intake was soft. - Time from intake to hand-off. Target: inside a week, often one sitting plus the recording.

After five setups How many still open the project two weeks later — ask once. If they never opened it, you overbuilt or you sold to someone who wanted a course. Average fee. If you are stuck at $97 after five, press the $197 button and live with fewer yeses.

Ignore how many knowledge files you uploaded. Volume is not quality. Jules’s bookkeeper project used phrases, not a dump of five years of returns.

Objections you will hear — including your own

“They could watch a YouTube video.” They could. They have not. You are selling a configured job in their account in a week, not a lecture.

“Isn’t this just writing a prompt?” A prompt they have to paste every time is not a project. Instructions plus files plus three working threads is a small appliance. Price the appliance.

“They want it in my account so they don’t have to pay for Claude.” No. That is selling access to a model you do not own. They need their own subscription. You configure it. If they will not pay the model, they will not pay you fairly either.

“What about confidential files?” Follow their policy. When in doubt, do not upload. Paste per chat. Write that in the intake. Loading their client list into a project “to be helpful” is how you get a story you do not want.

“Can I productize one project and sell access to many buyers?” That is a skill pack (path 2), with generic files, not their confidential ones. Different product. Do not mix a custom setup with a shared GPT you rent out.

What this feeds — and what feeds it

What feeds a $197 setup People who already pay for Claude or ChatGPT and stall on the blank project. Buyers of a skill pack who want it installed. Service clients who keep asking you to “just set up the GPT.” Your own sample and walkthrough.

What a setup feeds - Skill packs, if three clients needed the same shape. Generic version, no client files, sold as a file. - Automations, if they then want the project to draft from a form. New quote. Path 19. - A productized service, if they want you to run the job, not own the tool. - Not a hosted model business. That is the thing you leave alone.

Hand off. The best compliment is a week of silence because they are using it.

Mistakes that kill this path

Keeping the project in your account. Loading their confidential files into a model against their policy. Overbuilding a second brain they will never open. Those three still kill it.

The rest - Writing instructions they are afraid to edit. - Starters that only work if you are typing. - A 40-minute “training” instead of a five-minute walkthrough. - Selling access to Claude as if you were the vendor. - Bundling automations into the $197 so you work a week for a setup price. - Keeping copies of their files “for examples.”

If you need their login after day eight, you did not hand off. Go back and finish.

Tools — only what you need

Claude Projects or Custom GPTs. One. The five-minute walkthrough: QuickTime, Loom, or your phone pointed at the screen, as long as they can hear you and see the clicks.

The rest A page with a $197 button. An intake form: job, tone samples, files allowed, policy notes. Stripe or Stan. A calendar slot of 45 minutes if you do intake live, or an async form if they write clearly.

Do not add yet Do not build a native app. Do not resell API access. Do not stand up a team workspace they do not need. Configure the tool they already open.

Checklist

  • Sample project for a job you know, tested
  • Five-minute walkthrough recorded
  • $197 button on a page, test-confirmed
  • Five asks to people who already pay for the model
  • Intake: job, tone, allowed files, data policy
  • Project created in their account
  • Instructions they can read and edit
  • Three named starter threads that already work
  • No confidential files they did not approve
  • They run a starter without you typing
  • Walkthrough delivered
  • Seven-day tweak window in writing
  • Your access removed
  • Their files deleted from your machine
  • You said out loud: you are not reselling the model

FAQ

Claude Project or Custom GPT? Whichever they already pay for and open. If they use both, pick the one for this job. Do not configure two for $197.

What if they do not have a paid account? They need one. You do not sell them yours. Send the signup link. Schedule the setup after they can log in.

Can I do this over email only? Yes, if they write a clear intake. Live screen share is faster for hand-off. Jules uses twenty minutes live plus the recording.

How long should instructions be? Short enough they will edit them. Usually under a page. If you need four pages, you are writing a playbook (path 1) and calling it a project.

What goes in the three starters? The three jobs they will actually run this week. Not edge cases. Not “tell me about yourself.” Named, boring, useful.

Is $197 too low? It is a setup, not a month of service. If they want weekly-run work, sell path 7. If the setup is truly two jobs and picky files, $297.

What is the tweak window for? Instruction and starter fixes. Not “also build my content engine.” Write that down.

Can I show their project as a case study? Only with permission, and never with their files. Show your sample instead. Jules shows the fake receivables list.

Do I guarantee output quality forever? No. Models change. You guarantee a working configuration at hand-off and one tweak week. After that, they own the maintenance, or they buy another setup.

What if they ask me to stay as admin? No. Viewer for seven days if it helps them sleep. Then you leave. Ownership is the product.

PATH 21

Your front door

A useful diagnostic. Email required. The follow-up sells path 1. Strangers become a list.

15 minute read · 3,398 words

You finished any earlier path. You need people you have never met.

The first email from someone you have never met.
Time to first
Same week you publish the form
Cash window
Feeds every other path
Tools
A form, a PDF scorecard, MailerLite or Beehiiv.

A ‘free’ file that is only a sales letter. Passing this book off as if you wrote it.

What the door actually is

A front door is a useful diagnostic. Seven questions. They get a score and one next step. You get an email. The free file is useful even if they never buy. That is the deal you keep.

You may brand your own scorecard. You may not copy this book and sell it as yours. Build your door. Point it at your playbook.

What it is not It is not a 14-page “free guide” that is a sales letter with a checklist slapped on page 13. It is not this suite with your name on the cover. It is not a 40-field form that feels like a job application. It is not a fake score that always says “you need my $47 file.”

If they can tell, in two minutes, that the PDF only exists to sell, you built a flyer. Flyers do not earn replies from strangers. Diagnostics do.

Why email is required A stranger who takes a score and disappears was never going to buy this week. That is fine. The door still has to be fair: they get the file instantly. You get a way to teach for a few notes, then ask. No hostage PDF. No “we’ll send it someday.” Instant, then a sequence that earns the ask.

Who this is for — and who should skip it

This path fits if you finished any earlier path. You have something to point at — usually path 1, a playbook — and you need people you have never met. Friends and DMs got you the first sales. The door is how strangers join that line without you hunting each one.

You can do this if - You can write seven questions that change the advice. If every answer leads to “buy my file,” the questions are fake. - You can write a two-page PDF that still helps if they never pay you. - You will set up a 5–7 note sequence before you buy a single ad.

Skip this path if Skip it if you have nothing to sell yet. A door into an empty house is a hobby. Skip it if you planned to republish this suite as your lead magnet. Skip it if you will not include an unsubscribe. Skip it if you want a list more than you want to be useful — that motive leaks onto page one.

If you still cannot name the paid next step, go back to path 1 and finish the playbook. Then build the door that points at it.

The offer, named in one sentence

“Seven questions. A score. One next step. Free even if you never buy.”

That is the public offer. The private truth: the sequence teaches, then sells path 1. Both can be true if the PDF is real.

What they get - Seven questions, scored honestly. - A PDF that explains the score in plain language and gives one next step they can do this week without paying you. - Instant delivery. The file is in the thank-you email or the next screen, not in a queue you will “get to.”

What you get An email, permission to send a short sequence, and a score you may use to pick which next step to recommend. You do not get a right to spam daily. You do not get a right to sell them twelve things in note one.

What done looks like

Done is the first email from someone you have never met, sitting in a tool you control, with a score attached, and a PDF they already received. The sequence exists. Unsubscribe works. The paid playbook is linked in a later note, not in the first paragraph of the free file.

The fairness test Give the PDF to a friend and tell them not to buy anything. If they can still take one useful action this week, the door is honest. If they cannot, you wrote a sales letter and called it a scorecard. Rewrite until the friend has a next step that does not require a card.

Technical done Form live. PDF attached or linked immediately. Confirmation they expect. Sequence of 5–7 notes drafted, not “I’ll write them when people join.”

Pricing — the door is free, the house is not

The cash window is not a price on the PDF. The door feeds every other path. You do not charge for the seven questions.

Where money shows up - Path 1 playbook, usually $19–$47, asked for in note 5, 6, or 7 after you have taught. - Later: a service, a setup, a drop. Not in the first email.

What you never charge for here Do not charge $9 for the scorecard and also call it free. Do not put the diagnostic behind a “tripwire” that is just the same sales letter. Free means free. Useful means useful without a receipt.

If someone asks to pay you for a live walkthrough of their results, that is a $97–$197 setup or a brief, sold as itself, after they already have the PDF. Do not hide that inside the magnet.

The first 72 hours

Day 1: questions plus PDF Write seven questions that change the advice. Not flattery. Each answer should send them toward a different next step: a playbook, a skill pack, “do not buy anything yet, finish one job,” or “talk to a person, not a file.” Build a two-to-four page PDF: how scoring works, what each band means, one action per band that does not require paying you. Put your email in the footer as a human, not as a funnel.

Day 2: form live MailerLite, Beehiiv, or Tally into either. Fields: first name optional, email required, the seven questions. Thank-you page includes the PDF now. Test as a stranger from a second email. Confirm the file arrives. Confirm unsubscribe is one click.

Put the form above the paid button on your page. The door is the first thing a stranger sees. The playbook is the second.

Day 3: share once Share it once in a place you already help people. One post, one comment, one email to people who already know you. Do not buy ads. Do not spray five platforms. Watch whether anyone finishes the seven questions. If they abandon at question two, the questions are too cute or too many.

The path, step by step

  1. Name the paid thing the door will eventually sell. Usually path 1. Write that on a sticky note so the sequence does not wander into a buffet.
  2. Write seven questions that diagnose, not flatter. If two answers never change the PDF, delete one question and merge them.
  3. Write a scoring key you could defend to a skeptical friend. Bands, not fake precision. “12–18: you need a pack, not a tool binge.”
  4. Write the PDF so the free next step is real. Example: “Run one recurring job in a saved project this week. Do not buy a second subscription.”
  5. Build the form. Email required. Instant file. No 40 fields. No “company size, budget, phone, mailing address.”
  6. Hook the form to MailerLite or Beehiiv. Tag the score band if you can do it without a science project. If you cannot, send one sequence to everyone and mention bands in the PDF only.
  7. Write 5–7 notes before anyone joins. Teach, then ask. Note 1 is the file and what to read. Notes 2–4 teach. Notes 5–6 ask for the playbook. Note 7 is a quiet close or a single service mention if that is truly the better fit.
  8. Test-buy the path 1 checkout from a second email. The sequence must not point at a dead button.
  9. Put the form above the paid button. Share once where you already help.
  10. Reply to the first stranger like a person. One sentence. Not a six-link kit.
  11. After ten subscribers, read where they drop. Fix questions, not your branding.
  12. Only then consider a small ad budget. Sequence first. Ads on an empty sequence buy you unsubscribes you paid for.

A worked example: Noor’s 7-question door

Noor finished a $29 playbook: a 10-day content system for solo trades. Friends bought it. Strangers did not know Noor existed. Noor needed a door, not a second book.

The seven questions Noor used Each is multiple choice. Each changes the band.

  1. In the last 30 days, how many times did you actually finish a job with AI, not just open a chat? (0, 1–3, 4+)
  2. Do you have one offer a stranger can buy this week without a call? (yes / not yet)
  3. Where does a new customer usually find you? (word of mouth, Google, Instagram or TikTok, they don’t)
  4. What happens after someone messages you? (you reply yourself, a form, nothing for days)
  5. Have you saved a prompt or a project you reuse, or do you start from a blank chat every time? (saved / blank)
  6. How many hours a week can you give this, honestly, with the job you already have? (under 2, 2–5, 6+)
  7. If a file told you the next step, would you rather have a pack to run yourself, or a person to set it up? (pack / person)

Noor scores 0–2 points per question. Bands: 0–5 still-setup, 6–10 ready-for-a-pack, 11–14 ready-but-inconsistent. The PDF gives one action per band. Still-setup: write the offer sentence this week, do not buy a course. Ready-for-a-pack: run one saved project on a real job by Friday. Ready-but-inconsistent: pick one platform for 14 days. None of those actions require $29. That is the deal Noor keeps.

The form and the sequence Email required. PDF on the thank-you page and in note 1. Noor brands it “Trades AI scorecard — Noor” — a scorecard, not this book. Notes: (1) here’s your file, read the band, do the action; (2) a one-job example from the playbook, no pitch; (3) why blank chat is expensive, still no pitch; (4) a mistake: posting on five platforms; (5) the playbook exists, $29, link; (6) a customer sentence from a friend who used day 3; (7) last time the link will be in the sequence, plus unsubscribe reminder.

A stranger named Dell finishes the form on a Thursday. Score 8. Note 1 goes out at once. Dell does the “saved project” action. Note 5 sells. Dell buys the $29 file Saturday. Noor never ran an ad. First email from someone Noor had never met: Thursday 4:12pm. That is the first sale of this path — the address, not the $29. The $29 is path 1 working through the door.

Copy you can actually send

The public line Seven questions. A score. One next step. Free even if you never buy.

The post I made a short AI-readiness scorecard for [your people]. Seven questions, a score, one thing to do this week. It is useful if you never buy from me. Link: [form].

Note 1 (instant) Your scorecard is here: [PDF]. Read the band with your score. Do the one action this week. I’ll send a few short notes. You can leave any time — unsubscribe is at the bottom.

Notes 2–4, shape not script One job, one example, one mistake to avoid. No link to the paid file yet unless they ask.

Note 5, the ask If you want the full sequence I use for [job], it’s a $29 playbook: [link]. If the free action in the PDF is enough, stay with that. Either is fine.

Note 7, close Last note in this set. Playbook is still here: [link]. If you want a human setup instead, reply with the word SETUP and I’ll tell you the price. If not, I’ll write when I have something useful. Unsubscribe anytime.

What you do not write Do not paste chapters of this suite into your PDF. Do not put a fake 94/100 at the top. Do not forget the unsubscribe. The free file is a diagnostic, not a brochure that happens to have a score.

Week-by-week if you have a job

Week 1 Day 1: questions plus PDF. Day 2: form live, test as a stranger. Then write the 5–7 notes before any ads and before a second share. Share once in a place you already help people.

Week 2 Reply to humans. Fix the question people skip. Do not add a webinar. Count emails from strangers, not likes on the post.

Week 3–4 If ten people finished the form and the sequence is live, you may share again or spend a small amount on ads to the form — not to the paid button. If fewer than ten, the share was weak or the questions feel like a trap. Tighten the PDF’s free action. Do not buy traffic to a flyer.

The quality bar

The bar is a file that still helps if they never pay, and a score that would change if they answered honestly.

Questions They change the advice. “Are you an ambitious entrepreneur?” does not. “Do you have one offer a stranger can buy this week?” does.

PDF Two to four pages. Band meanings. One free action each. Your name. No pasted chapters from someone else’s book. You may brand your own scorecard. You may not copy this book and sell it as yours.

Sequence Teaches first. Asks later. Unsubscribe always. Instant file, always.

Ethics you do not get to skip Passing this book off as if you wrote it is off limits. Selling this book as your own is off limits. Building a door that points at your playbook is the work.

What to count

First week - Form starts versus finishes. If half leave, you have too many questions or they smell a quiz trap. - Emails from people you have never met. That is the first sale of this path. - Whether the PDF link is clicked. If not, delivery is buried.

After 30 subscribers - Unsubscribe rate. Over 5 percent on the sequence means you asked too early or you were boring. Under 1 percent and zero clicks can mean nobody is reading — check the from-name. - Playbook click from notes 5–7. A few percent is real. Fifty percent means you only invited fans. - Replies. One genuine reply is worth twenty silent “opens.”

Do not buy a dashboard before you have thirty people. A spreadsheet with date, source, score band, bought-or-not is enough.

Objections you will hear — including your own

“People won’t give an email.” Some will not. They were not going to buy this month. The people who want the score will. Do not open the PDF to the whole internet and then wonder why you have no list. Email required is the deal.

“A free file should be a 40-page guide.” No. Long and useless is still useless. Seven questions and a tight PDF beat a stolen book with your logo.

“Can I just upload this suite as the magnet?” No. You may not copy this book and sell it as yours. You may not give it away as if you wrote it. Write a scorecard for your people. Point at your playbook.

“I should run ads on day one.” Not before the sequence exists. Paid traffic into silence is how you learn to hate lists.

“Fake scores convert better.” They convert once. Then they tell friends. Your name is small. Keep it clean.

“What if they only want the free thing?” Good. You kept the deal. Some of them buy in three months. Some never do. That is allowed.

What this feeds — and what feeds it

The door is last for a reason. It feeds every other path and it needs one of them first.

What must exist A paid next step, almost always path 1. Optionally a service or a setup if the diagnostic band says “person, not file.” The sequence should not invent a product you have not shipped.

What the list then feeds - Playbook sales without you DMing. - Later drops (path 17): you can tell a list about a Thursday live without begging the algorithm. - Research briefs, automations, custom projects — only as a reply offer, not as note 1. - A newsletter if you want one. The door can be the same form.

What the door must never feed A replica of this suite with your name on it. A “free” file that is only a sales letter. A purchased list. Those rot.

Mistakes that kill the door

Buying ads before the sequence exists. A 40-field form. Fake scores. Forgetting the unsubscribe. Those still kill it.

The rest - A PDF that is only a sales letter. - Passing this book off as if you wrote it, or selling this book as your own. - “We’ll send the file in 24 hours” as a drip trick. - Asking for the sale in paragraph one of the free file. - Daily blasts. - Questions that always output the same band. - Sharing on five platforms before you tested delivery from a second email.

If you catch yourself rewriting the PDF to mention the paid file more than the free action, you slipped. Put the paid file back in notes 5–7.

Tools — only what you need

A form, a PDF scorecard, MailerLite or Beehiiv. That is the stack.

Form Tally, the Beehiiv form, or MailerLite’s. Email required. Seven questions. Thank-you with the file.

PDF Google Docs export. Two to four pages. Your name, the year, a sentence that the file is free even if they never buy.

Sequence 5–7 notes in the same tool. Plain text is fine. No twenty-pixel buttons in every paragraph.

Do not add yet Do not add a webinar platform, a quiz app with 14 branches, or a CRM. Thirty strangers do not need HubSpot. They need a file and a way out.

Checklist

  • You finished an earlier path; the paid next step exists
  • Seven questions that change the advice
  • Scoring key you can defend
  • PDF with one free action per band
  • You did not copy this book into the PDF
  • Email required, not 40 fields
  • Instant delivery, tested from a second inbox
  • Unsubscribe works
  • 5–7 notes written before you share widely
  • Teach first, ask later (notes 5–7)
  • Form sits above the paid button
  • Shared once where you already help
  • No ads until the sequence exists
  • First stranger email treated like a person

FAQ

Do I have to use seven questions? Seven is enough to change advice and short enough to finish on a phone. Five can work. Forty cannot.

What if I do not have a playbook yet? Do not build the door. Finish path 1, or another path with a named next step. An empty house does not need a nicer porch.

Can I brand the scorecard with my business name? Yes. You may brand your own scorecard. You may not copy this book and sell it as yours, and you may not give this book away as if you wrote it.

MailerLite or Beehiiv? Whichever you will actually open. Beehiiv if you also want a public letter. MailerLite if you want simple forms. Both handle unsubscribe. Use that feature.

Should the PDF include my prices? A line at the end is allowed: “If you want the full 10-day file, it lives here.” The body of the PDF should still work with that line deleted.

How fast is “instant”? On the thank-you screen and in the first email. Not “check your promotions tab in a few hours while we wait to look hungry.”

What is the first sale on this path? The first email from someone you have never met. Cash from the playbook is path 1 using the door. Count both. Do not pretend the list is failing because the $29 did not happen on day one.

Can I sell the diagnostic as a $9 product? Then it is not this path. This path is a free door. If you want a paid score, that is a small file on path 9, and you still need a free door later.

What about GDPR and consent? Say what they are joining, in one sentence, under the button. Include unsubscribe. Do not buy lists. Do not scrape emails out of comments.

When do I add ads? After the sequence exists, delivery works, and a handful of strangers have finished the form. Until then you are paying to learn that your PDF is a flyer.
